Texas Cannabis & Hemp Research — Audited Final Report

Last audited: 31 October 2026. This is an independent audit and corrected rewrite of the Texas Cannabis & Hemp Research — Complete, Parts 0–12 file dated 31 October 2026. The auditor did not write the original. The federal baseline in the commission was used as given and was not re-audited; only the report's statements of it, and Texas's responses to it, were checked.

What this audit changed, in one paragraph. The original was already unusually careful: most law was correctly labelled as enacted, vetoed, dead or proposed, most conflicts were already flagged rather than hidden, and no adult-use or full-medical programme was invented. The audit therefore confirms the bulk of the report, resolves four flagged conflicts (HB 46 signing day, SB 3 veto day, the TABC rule split, and the paraphernalia tiers), corrects the ibogaine and cite-and-release dates already corrected in Run 2 and carries them through Part 1, and leaves standing the flags that primary sources in this run could not close — above all the Schedule III / Texas-follow question, the THCA-flower injunction status, and the many verbatim statute quotations that rest on mirror/search reproductions rather than an opened statutes.capitol.texas.gov text. Nothing in the rewrite invents a licence count, a fee, a property, a tribal operation or a cross-border total.

PART 1 — AUDIT TABLE

Verdicts: Confirmed = the claim as written (including its flag, where the flag is the correct statement) stands. Corrected = the claim was wrong or internally contradictory and the corrected wording in the last column replaces it in the rewritten report below. Removed = wrong with no correct version found — no row in this audit required a pure removal, because every wrong date/figure had a correct primary or corrected-tabulation version; the only removals are of alternative variants (21 June, 23 June, the January date for §35.7) listed as Corrected rows. Unverified = only a secondary source exists, or the primary text was not opened, so the claim is kept only with its flag. Primary sources only were used to confirm law; secondary sources are cited only where the report itself uses them for non-law facts, and are labelled as such.

Conflicting-figure decisions (applied everywhere in the rewrite): (1) Dispensing organisations — DPS corrected tabulation HQ 2026-046 controls: 3 operating + 12 conditional = 15 target. (2) CURT patients — latest DPS-sourced figure is end-2025 135,470 (DPS via press); July 2026 162,216 is operator-reported only, never DPS-published. (3) DSHS hemp — 14,170 retail / 778 manufacturers is a dated 18 June 2026 presentation figure via secondary reporting, not a live October count. (4) Hemp market size — no single figure adopted; $5.5bn / $4.3bn / $10.3bn / Baker $2.78bn are different scopes/years and stay separate. (5) Cannabis-zoned properties — 0 proven; Table A properties are not counted. (6) Cross-border sales — no Texas share of New Mexico sales and no Oklahoma Texas-buyer total adopted.

Texas Cannabis & Hemp Research — Complete, Parts 0–12

Federal baseline — given, not re-researched: state-licensed medical marijuana and FDA-approved products moved to Schedule III effective 28 April 2026 (91 FR 22714); adult-use marijuana remains Schedule I; IRS §280E is generally lifted for Schedule III medical activity; hemp PL 119-37 §781 takes effect 12 November 2026 but PL 119-103 §2019 limits it until 11 December 2026, after which the full total-THC hemp definition and the 0.4 mg total THC per container cap apply; USDA enforcement of DEA-registered lab testing for hemp is delayed to 31 December 2026; the federal medical marijuana appropriations rider runs through 11 December 2026; there is no federal cannabis banking law.

How to read this file: Law is stated from official Texas statute, regulation, agency and court sources wherever possible. Where a direct official-page opening failed in this run, the fact is marked [Verify] and the secondary source is named, not presented as the official text. Proposed, vetoed and dead bills are labelled as such and are never presented as law. Secondary sources are used only for non-law facts (market figures, polling, events) and are dated. No personal phone or email of an individual is reproduced. All places are Texas unless stated otherwise; Texarkana, Texas is separate from Texarkana, Arkansas, and El Paso / Laredo are separate from Ciudad Juárez / Nuevo Laredo.
Part 0Part 1Part 2Part 3Part 4Part 5Part 6Part 7Part 8Part 9Part 10Part 11Part 12Research notes

PART 0: HEADLINES

One-sentence status: Texas has no adult-use marijuana and no full medical marijuana programme. The only lawful marijuana route is the narrow, physician-prescription, low-THC Texas Compassionate Use Program (TCUP), Health & Safety Code Chapter 487 / Occupations Code Chapter 169. Separately, Texas has a very large, still-operating consumable hemp market under Health & Safety Code Chapter 443 — but in 2026 that market was sharply narrowed by agency rules and a state scheduling action, and it faces a limited federal change on 12 November 2026 and the full federal redefinition on 11 December 2026 (see §1.5).

HeadlineWhat happenedSource (accessed 31 Oct 2026)
HB 46 expands TCUP89th Legislature, Regular Session, signed 20 June 2025 (confirmed on Texas Legislature Online history for HB 46, 89(R)), effective 1 September 2025. Replaced the 1%-by-weight THC cap with 10 mg THC per dosage unit and 1 gram THC per package / inhalation device; added chronic pain, traumatic brain injury, Crohn’s disease / other inflammatory bowel disease, and terminal illness / hospice-palliative care; authorised lotions, transdermal patches, suppositories and medically-necessary non-smoked pulmonary inhalation; directed DPS to license 15 dispensing organisations total, with satellite locations.TLO HB 46 analysis; NORML 22 June 2025
SB 3 hemp THC ban vetoedSB 3, 89th Regular Session, Senate 26–5 (19 Mar 2025), House 87–54 (21–22 May 2025), Senate concurrence 25–6 (25 May 2025). Governor Greg Abbott vetoed it 22 June 2025, calling it well-intentioned but legally unsustainable / likely pre-empted and enjoined, citing Arkansas Act 629 litigation, and calling for alcohol-like regulation instead. SB 3 is NOT law.Vicente LLP on SB 3 veto

Regulation alternatives SB 53 (1st Spec.) and SB 30 (2nd Spec., TABC framework, 21+, testing/labelling, 6.25% tax) also died. All DEAD, not law.Stateside special-session takeaways; Cannabis Business Times

It did not itself enact a statute, potency cap or buffer zone. Full GA-56 text was not opened in this run — [Verify] on governor.texas.gov.TABC consumable hemp page, last updated 15 June 2026

It does not ban all vaping or non-vape hemp edibles/tinctures/beverages, and on secondary summaries does not itself criminalise mere possession/use by the buyer. Sky Marketing Corp., 1 May 2026, confirmed delta-8 THC is a controlled substance and only naturally occurring trace amounts in a hemp plant are exempt. DSHS (opened): “Consumable hemp products labeled to contain delta-8 THC or shown to contain more than trace amounts of tetrahydrocannabinols, including delta-8 THC, may be detained and referred to law enforcement.” “Trace” is not defined by a number on that page — [Verify] enforcement standard.DSHS Consumable Hemp Program (opened 31 Oct 2026)

The March 2026 total-delta-9 rule in practice made most THCA flower non-compliant; a Travis County TRO in April 2026 was followed by secondary reports that the Fifteenth Court of Appeals lifted an injunction 5 June 2026. PART 1: LAWS

1.1 Legal status

LEGAL: Low-THC cannabis only through TCUP — prescribed by a CURT-registered physician for a listed condition, dispensed by a DPS-licensed dispensing organisation, within dose/package limits, no smoking. Hemp and compliant consumable hemp products at ≤0.3% delta-9 THC on a dry-weight basis under the statutory definition, manufactured by a DSHS licensee / sold by a DSHS registrant, 21+, subject to 2026 total-delta-9 testing and the July 2026 delta-8 schedule (see §1.5). CBD/CBG products that comply with Chapter 443 and do not contain more than trace non-delta-9 THC (post-31 July 2026).

NOT LEGAL: Adult-use/recreational marijuana in any amount; full-strength medical marijuana (flower, high-THC products, home cultivation) outside TCUP dose limits; smoking low-THC cannabis; smoking-manufacture of hemp in Texas (§443.204(4)); sale of any cannabinoid vape product (SB 2024, from 1 Sept 2025); delta-8/delta-10/THCP and other manufactured non-delta-9 THC above naturally-occurring trace amounts from 31 July 2026; possession of marijuana or Penalty Group 2 THC outside a TCUP prescription / hemp exemption; no statewide citizen initiative exists in Texas to legalise by petition — change requires the Legislature (biennial regular sessions; next convening 12 Jan 2027) or a called special session.

1.2 Statutes and regulations

Official-link pattern: statutes.capitol.texas.gov/Docs/<Code>/htm/<Code>.<Chapter>.htm — e.g. HSC Ch. 487, HSC Ch. 443, HSC Ch. 481, Occupations Code Ch. 169, Agriculture Code Chs. 121–122. Direct chapter opens in this run returned only the statute index page, so verbatim text below is quoted from the statute text as reproduced in search results / statute mirrors, with the official citation given for verification on Texas Legislature Online — [Verify] each section on the official site before republication as a final dossier.

Texas Controlled Substances Act — HSC Ch. 481, §481.002Marihuana and tetrahydrocannabinols remain controlled, with a hemp carve-out.§481.002(5): “‘Controlled substance’ means a substance, including a drug, an adulterant, and a dilutant, listed in Schedules I through V or Penalty Group 1, 1-A, 1-B, 2, 2-A, 3, or 4. The term includes the aggregate weight of any mixture, solution, or other substance containing a controlled substance. The term does not include hemp, as defined by §121.001, Agriculture Code, or the tetrahydrocannabinols in hemp.” §481.002(26): “‘Marihuana’ means the plant Cannabis sativa L., whether growing or not, the seeds of that plant, and every compound, manufacture, salt, derivative, mixture, or preparation of that plant or its seeds. The term does not include: … (F) hemp, as that term is defined by §121.001, Agriculture Code.”See official HSC Ch. 481 history notes. §481.121 (mirror)
HSC §481.034 — schedulingCommissioner generally mirrors federal scheduling, with a legislative-override bar.§481.034(g): “Except as otherwise provided by this subsection, if a substance is designated, rescheduled, or deleted as a controlled substance under federal law and notice of that fact is given to the commissioner, the commissioner similarly shall control the substance under this chapter. After the expiration of a 30-day period beginning on the day after the date of publication in the Federal Register of a final order designating a substance as a controlled substance or rescheduling or deleting a substance, the commissioner similarly shall designate, reschedule, or delete the substance, unless the commissioner objects during the period.” §481.034(c): commissioner may not add/delete/reschedule a substance if the Legislature has placed it in / deleted it from a schedule.§481.034 (mirror)
Hemp — Agriculture Code Chs. 121–122 + HSC Ch. 443 (HB 1325, 86th Leg., 2019, eff. 10 June 2019)Texas hemp definition, TDA state plan, DSHS consumable hemp programme.Ag. Code §121.001: “‘hemp’ means the plant Cannabis sativa L. and any part of that plant, including the seeds of the plant and all derivatives, extracts, cannabinoids, isomers, acids, salts, and salts of isomers, whether growing or not, with a delta-9 tetrahydrocannabinol concentration of not more than 0.3 percent on a dry weight basis.” §122.051(b): TDA rules must prescribe sampling/inspection/testing “to ensure that the delta-9 tetrahydrocannabinol concentration of hemp plants cultivated in this state is not more than 0.3 percent on a dry weight basis.” HSC §443.204(4): DSHS rules must prohibit processing/manufacturing a consumable hemp product for smoking.HB 1325 (2019). §122.051 (mirror)

Exact §300.101/§300.301 formula wording [Verify] in Texas Register adopted text.31 Mar 2026. DSHS programme (opened)
TABC rules — 16 TAC §§35.5–35.7Age-21 / ID / on-premises consumption rules for TABC licensees/permittees only; no standalone TABC hemp licence.§35.5: no sale of consumable hemp products to a person under 21 by a TABC licensee/permittee. §35.6: mandatory government-issued photo ID inspection. §35.7: no on-premises consumption by off-premises retailers. Authority via Alcoholic Beverage Code §§5.31, 11.61(b)(7), 61.71(a)(16) + GA-56. Violation risks temporary suspension or cancellation.Permanent rules adopted Feb 2026; emergency rules 23 Sept 2025, enforcement from 1 Oct 2025. TABC page (opened, updated 15 June 2026)
TDA rules — 4 TAC Ch. 24; SB 2024 (2025)Hemp cultivation/handling; cannabinoid vape sales ban.4 TAC Ch. 24 chapter number / current text [Verify] in Part 7 run. SB 2024, 89th Regular Session, eff. 1 Sept 2025: Class A misdemeanour to market/advertise/sell/offer an e-cigarette product containing cannabinoids, kratom, kava, mushrooms, alcohol or tianeptine — exact codification [Verify] on TLO.SB 2024 (2025)
Does Texas follow the federal Schedule III move? Does §280E relief reach TCUP? — [Verify], with reasoning. The federal baseline is split: only state-licensed medical marijuana and FDA-approved products move to Schedule III; adult-use stays Schedule I. Texas’s programme is state-licensed and medical, but it is low-THC-only and Texas statute still defines “marihuana” and lists it in Schedule I, and §481.034(c) bars the commissioner from rescheduling a substance the Legislature has placed in a schedule. Whether the federal final order (91 FR 22714) by its own terms covers a dose-capped low-THC product that Texas treats as a distinct “low-THC cannabis” category — as opposed to “marihuana” generally — is not clearly settled by the material opened in this run. Do not assert that TCUP products automatically became Schedule III in Texas on 28 April 2026, nor that they automatically stayed Schedule I, without (a) the Federal Register text, (b) a DSHS commissioner action/notice under §481.034(g) (including any objection), and (c) any DPS/DSHS guidance. The same [Verify] applies to §280E: federally §280E is generally lifted for Schedule III medical activity per the baseline, but Texas has no state income tax and its franchise tax is a margin tax (Tax Code Ch. 171), not a federal-taxable-income tax — see Part 2. Practical consequence even if Schedule III applied federally: it would not legalise adult-use in Texas, would not create a Texas adult-use licence, and would not override HSC Chapter 481 penalties for non-TCUP marijuana. All other marijuana remains Schedule I federally per the baseline and Schedule I / Penalty Group 2 in Texas.

1.3 Penalties, TCUP limits and forms

Marihuana possession — HSC §481.121 (verbatim tiers): “(a) Except as authorized by this chapter, a person commits an offense if the person knowingly or intentionally possesses a usable quantity of marihuana.” “(b) An offense under Subsection (a) is: (1) a Class B misdemeanor if the amount of marihuana possessed is two ounces or less; (2) a Class A misdemeanor if the amount of marihuana possessed is four ounces or less but more than two ounces; (3) a state jail felony if the amount of marihuana possessed is five pounds or less but more than four ounces; (4) a felony of the third degree if the amount of marihuana possessed is 50 pounds or less but more than 5 pounds; (5) a felony of the second degree if the amount of marihuana possessed is 2,000 pounds or less but more than 50 pounds; and (6) a felony of the first degree punishable by imprisonment … for life or for a term of not more than 99 years or less than 5 years, and a fine not to exceed $50,000, if the amount of marihuana possessed is more than 2,000 pounds.” Source (mirror, accessed 31 Oct 2026): §481.121. History note on Justia: Acts 1989; amended 1993, 2009, 2021 (HB 1694 §9) — [Verify] whether HB 1694 changed (b) or only (c) defence. Punishment ranges (Penal Code Ch. 12, secondary summary): Class B ≤180 days / ≤$2,000; Class A ≤1 year / ≤$4,000; state jail felony 180 days–2 years / ≤$10,000; 3rd degree 2–10 years / ≤$10,000; 2nd degree 2–20 years / ≤$10,000; 1st degree 5–99 years/life / ≤$50,000 (for (b)(6)).

Penalty Group 2 THC (concentrates, oils, wax, edibles with extracted THC, vape oil) — HSC §§481.103, 481.116 (secondary summary, [Verify] verbatim tiers on official site): <1 g = state jail felony; 1 g to <4 g = 3rd degree; 4 g to <400 g = 2nd degree; ≥400 g = 1st degree (5–99 years/life, ≤$50,000). Weight is aggregate weight including adulterants/dilutants under §481.002(5). This is why a single vape cartridge / edible package can be a felony even though the same weight of plant would be a misdemeanour.

Delivery/manufacture — HSC §§481.120 (marihuana), 481.113 (PG 2) (secondary summary, [Verify] verbatim): Marihuana delivery: ≤¼ oz without remuneration = Class B; ≤¼ oz with remuneration = Class A; >¼ oz to 5 lbs = state jail felony; >5 to 50 lbs = 2nd degree; >50 to 2,000 lbs = 1st degree; >2,000 lbs = enhanced 1st degree — upper tiers [Verify] on official text.

Cultivation: Texas has no separate home-cultivation offence and no home-cultivation right. Only DPS-licensed dispensing organisations may cultivate low-THC cannabis, and only TDA-licensed growers may cultivate hemp. Unlicensed cultivation is charged on the weight of usable marihuana / THC recovered under §§481.121 / 481.116 / delivery-manufacture provisions. [Verify] whether any 2025 bill created a distinct cultivation tier — none was found.

Paraphernalia — HSC §481.125 (tiers corrected from §12.1 mirror text): possession/use under (a) is a Class C misdemeanour under (d); delivery / possession with intent to deliver / manufacture with intent to deliver under (b) is a Class A misdemeanour under (e) (90 days–1 year on a repeat (b)/(c) conviction); delivery under (c) — actor 18 or older, recipient under 18 and at least three years younger — is a state jail felony under (f). A TCUP patient's device prescribed under Occupations Code §169.006 is framed in §12.1 as not “in violation of this chapter” under §481.125(a); no express paraphernalia carve-out section was located — that reasoning, not a quoted carve-out, is the correct statement until the official statute text is checked.

Public consumption: No standalone “public consumption of low-THC cannabis” offence was found in this run. Smoking is prohibited for TCUP patients in any setting; inhalation is only as prescribed via an approved device. General exposure would arise via possession / DWI / local ordinances, not a specific TCUP public-use section — [Verify] / Unavailable: state this gap rather than inventing a rule. For hemp, TABC §35.7 prohibits on-premises consumption at off-premises TABC retailers.

TCUP limits/forms (HB 46, eff. 1 Sept 2025): Per dosage unit ≤10 mg THC. Per package / inhalation device ≤1 gram total THC. Allowed forms (secondary summaries, consistent): oils, tinctures, capsules/lozenges, edibles/gummies, topicals/balms, lotions, transdermal patches, suppositories, and — only if prescribed and DSHS-approved — pulmonary inhalation via nebuliser / vaporiser / inhaler (aerosol/vapour, non-smoked). Smoking: PROHIBITED, always. No flower, no home-grow, no smokeable TCUP product. Purchase/supply limit: physicians may authorise up to a 90-day supply (NORML summary of HB 46; [Verify] in §169.003 / DPS rule — the statute text opened here did not show the 90-day clause verbatim). No separate patient card, no patient fee; the prescription lives in CURT.

1.4 Compassionate Use Program

Qualifying conditions — Occupations Code §169.003(a)(3)(A), as amended by HB 46 (verbatim list, from Justia 2025 text, accessed 31 Oct 2026): “the patient is diagnosed with: (i) epilepsy; (ii) a seizure disorder; (iii) multiple sclerosis; (iv) spasticity; (v) amyotrophic lateral sclerosis; (vi) autism; (vii) cancer; (viii) an incurable neurodegenerative disease; (ix) post-traumatic stress disorder; (x) a condition that causes chronic pain; (xi) traumatic brain injury; (xii) Crohn’s disease or other inflammatory bowel disease; [and, per HB 46 summaries / §169.003 continuation] (xiii) a terminal illness or a condition for which the patient is receiving hospice or palliative care; (xiv) a condition approved for an authorised research programme.” Source: §169.003. History: Added 2015, 84th Leg., Ch. 301 (S.B. 339); Acts 2025, 89th Leg., Ch. 710 (H.B. 46), Sec. 15, eff. 1 Sept 2025. 2025 additions (HB 46): chronic pain, traumatic brain injury, Crohn’s / IBD, terminal illness / hospice-palliative care. Chronic pain is described in secondary summaries as severe pain lasting / expected to last >90 days — [Verify] the statutory definition in §169.001 / §169.003(b) before quoting a 90-day threshold as statute. Physician must also certify benefit outweighs risk, and that the patient is a permanent Texas resident (secondary DPS summaries; [Verify] residency clause verbatim).

Who may prescribe — §§169.002, 169.004 (CURT registration): Only a Texas-licensed physician who meets §169.002 qualifications (including, per DPS summaries, registration and any required training — [Verify] training / specialty requirements) and who registers as the prescriber for that specific patient in CURT before prescribing/renewing. Verbatim (original §169.004, SB 339): “Before a physician qualified to prescribe low-THC cannabis under Section 169.002 may prescribe or renew a prescription for low-THC cannabis for a patient under this chapter, the physician must register as the prescriber for that patient in the compassionate-use registry maintained by the department under Section 487.054, Health and Safety Code.” No nurse practitioner / PA prescribing was found — [Verify] if any 2025 change altered prescriber class; none was reported in HB 46 summaries.

CURT — §487.054: DPS secure online registry; designed to prevent more than one physician registering as prescriber for a single patient; accessible to law enforcement and dispensing organisations to verify patients; physicians input safety/efficacy data. Patients do not apply for a card and pay no state patient fee (DPS FAQ summary). Physicians who opt in are searchable at curt.dps.texas.gov (secondary). Out-of-state patients: No reciprocity was found. TCUP is described as permanent-Texas-resident only. An out-of-state medical card does not authorise purchase in Texas, and a Texas prescription does not authorise purchase elsewhere. [Verify] residency text; mark as Verify, not as an opened verbatim quote.

Physician-requested additions: HB 46 / 25 TAC §1.61 (as proposed Sept 2025) lets a physician ask DSHS to report to the Legislature that low-THC cannabis may benefit an unlisted condition; DSHS forwards via DPS to the Legislature. That is a recommendation route, not a physician-by-physician expansion.

1.5 Hemp-derived THC

Definition / measurement: Statute: ≤0.3% delta-9 THC on a dry-weight basis (Ag. Code §121.001, HSC Ch. 443). No total-THC statute, no milligram-per-container statute, no per-serving statute was enacted in Texas. Rule (25 TAC §300.101, adopted eff. 31 Mar 2026): “acceptable hemp THC level” = total delta-9 THC concentration, reported with the accredited lab’s measurement of uncertainty, producing a distribution/range that includes ≤0.3%. Total delta-9 = delta-9 + 0.877 × THCA (secondary summaries of §300.101 / §300.301(d); [Verify] formula verbatim in Texas Register adopted text). §300.301(d) COA must report delta-9, total delta-9, and total THC per container (secondary summary of adopted text). Consequence: a product that passes a delta-9-only test can fail a total-delta-9 test if it is THCA-rich.

Hemp delta-9 beverage ≤0.3% (same tests)Legal for sale (no Texas hemp-beverage potency cap enacted; SB 3 / SB 53 / SB 30 beverage caps died)Ch. 443; no enacted beverage statute — [Verify] TABC mixed-beverage treatment if sold on-premises

SB 3, veto, special sessions: See Part 0. Veto proclamation (labelled as executive action, NOT statute) — key quotes from secondary reporting of the proclamation: “Allowing Senate Bill 3 to become law—knowing that it faces a lengthy battle that will render it dead on arrival in court—would hinder rather than help us solve the public safety issues this bill seeks to contain.” “Texas can and should reasonably regulate consumable hemp products to protect public health and safety without eliminating consumer access altogether.” And on Arkansas: the 2018 Farm Bill “converted hemp and hemp products from contraband to lawful commodities” and Arkansas’s law “was challenged, and a federal court swiftly halted it in its entirety, finding it was likely preempted by federal statutes and that its criminal provisions were likely unconstitutionally vague.” [Verify] full proclamation text on governor.texas.gov / TLO veto record.

Executive orders — complete list found in this run: GA-56, 10 Sept 2025, Gov. Greg Abbott — the only hemp executive order found for 2025–2026. Directives (from TABC/DSHS implementation summaries, [Verify] full text): (1) DSHS + TABC: prohibit sale to minors, require government-ID verification, on pain of licence/permit revocation/cancellation; (2) DSHS: review/revise testing to total delta-9 including THCA, clarify labelling (amount/concentration, serving size, health warnings), update record-keeping; (3) DPS: cooperate / enforce within existing authority; (4) agencies to use existing authority — GA-56 does not create a new criminal offence or a new tax. No other numbered hemp executive order (GA-55, GA-57, etc.) was found in this run — [Verify] on governor.texas.gov executive-order index; absence here is a search limit, not proof none exists. Veto proclamation for SB 3 (22 June 2025) and special-session proclamations (9 July 2025 call, 21 July convening) are proclamations, not executive orders.

DSHS / TABC rules timeline: 2 Aug 2020 original 25 TAC Ch. 300 eff. 2 Oct 2025 DSHS emergency §§300.701 (no sale to <21), 300.702 (ID verification). 23 Sept 2025 TABC emergency Ch. 51 §§51.1–51.2 (same 21+ / ID for TABC holders), enforcement from 1 Oct 2025, 120 days + 60-day extension. Dec 2025 DSHS proposed Ch. 300 amendments (Texas Register Vol. 50 No. 52; hearing 9 Jan 2026) proposing $25,000 / $20,000 fees — PROPOSED, not adopted at those figures. TABC permanent 16 TAC §§35.5–35.6 — adopted with changes from the 5 Dec 2025 proposal (50 TexReg 7818), expedited effective date 21 January 2026 on expiry of the emergency rules; adoption notice published 6 February 2026. 16 TAC §35.7 is separate: adopted at the Commission's 2 June 2026 meeting, published 19 June 2026, effective 22 June 2026 (Texas Register summary; confirm the adopted-rule page before republication). 20 Mar 2026 (Texas Register) / 31 Mar 2026 eff.: DSHS adopted Ch. 300 rewrite — total delta-9, COA/QR, child-resistant, record-keeping, restored fees. 10 July 2026 (Texas Register notice, secondary): DSHS reinstatement of 2021 THC / marihuana-extract definitions; 31 July 2026 eff. for delta-8 schedule. Litigation holds: THCA TRO (Apr 2026, Travis County Judge Maya Guerra Gamble, 2 weeks, hearing 23 Apr 2026); Fifteenth Court lifting of injunction 5 June 2026 (secondary); THBC appeal pending, oral argument reported 8 Dec 2026; SS Distro v. DSHS, Cause No. D-1-GN-26-007113 (459th District, Travis County, filed 2026, challenging July schedule + fees + THCA standard) — [Verify] docket status 31 Oct 2026; injunction hearing reported for 30 Oct 2026 in one secondary source — outcome [Verify].

Minimum age / ID: 21+ for consumable hemp products — by rule, not by statute, from Oct 2025 (DSHS emergency) / Mar 2026 (permanent Ch. 300) for DSHS licensees/registrants, and from Sept 2025 / Feb 2026 for TABC holders. Government-issued photo ID must be inspected before completing sale/service/delivery. Violation: DSHS licence/registration revocation; TABC permit suspension/cancellation. No statewide statutory age for hemp existed before GA-56. TCUP: no separate statutory purchase age; access is by physician prescription for a qualifying patient (including minors with a qualifying condition and a prescribing physician — [Verify] minor-patient / guardian provisions in DPS rules; do not assume 21+ applies to TCUP).

Testing / labelling (CHPs, 25 TAC Ch. 300 as adopted Mar 2026 — secondary summary of adopted text, [Verify] in Texas Register): Testing by an accredited / DEA-registered lab where required (federal DEA-lab delay to 31 Dec 2026 is a federal hemp-production point; Texas CHP testing accreditation is under Ch. 300 — [Verify] whether Texas requires DEA registration for CHP labs or only accreditation); COA must include lab ID/contact, sample/lot ID, methods/instrumentation, limits of detection/quantitation, expiration, measurement-of-uncertainty parameters, QR code verifying authenticity at an accredited lab, and results for delta-9, total delta-9, and total THC per container. Label must link/QR to COA, show manufacturer, batch, and ≤0.3% certification, be child-resistant, and not appeal to minors (GA-56 labelling direction; exact “attractive to children” prohibitions in adopted §300.402–300.407 — [Verify] verbatim).

Smokable hemp — court history (from DSHS page + Crown): 2020: Crown Distributing et al. sue DSHS (Travis County, 345th District); TRO / temporary injunction 18 Sept 2020 against enforcement of the smokable ban. 2021: trial court (16 Nov 2021) declares §443.204(4) + Ag. Code §122.301(b) unconstitutional, invalidates 25 TAC §300.104 in full, permanently enjoins enforcement. 24 June 2022: Supreme Court of Texas, DSHS v. Crown Distributing LLC, 647 S.W.3d 648, No. 21-1045 — reverses on due-course clause: manufacture/processing of smokable hemp is “purely a personal privilege” the Legislature may grant/withdraw; §443.204(4) upheld. Because DSHS abandoned defence of the distribution/retail-sale part of §300.104, that trial-court injunction remains for distribution/retail sale — DSHS page, opened, confirms: “the lower court’s injunction remains, which allows the distribution and retail sale of consumable hemp products for smoking.” 2026: the practical retail-sale position is now overlaid by the total-delta-9 rule and litigation above — the Crown injunction does not by itself make THCA flower compliant with total-delta-9 testing.

THC beverages: No Texas statute caps hemp-beverage THC in milligrams or bans hemp beverages. Beverages that are compliant CHPs (≤0.3% total delta-9, 21+, DSHS registrant) remain saleable; on-premises sale by a TABC holder is subject to TABC §§35.5–35.7 (age/ID, no off-premises consumption rule) and, if mixed with alcohol, to Alcoholic Beverage Code mixed-beverage rules — [Verify] mixed-beverage treatment; SB 3 / SB 53 / SB 30 beverage definitions (e.g. ≤5 mg delta-9) are DEAD proposals, not law.

Texas response to the new federal hemp definition and the 12 Nov / 11 Dec 2026 dates. Texas has not enacted a statute adopting PL 119-37 §781, the total-THC definition, or the 0.4 mg/container cap as of 31 Oct 2026 (no such bill was found in this run; [Verify] TLO for any 2025–2026 bill referencing §781 / 7 U.S.C. §1639o). Texas does not need to “adopt” the federal definition for federal law to apply: from 12 Nov 2026, §781 applies (per PL 119-103 §2019) only to products with cannabinoids not capable of being naturally produced by Cannabis sativa L.; from 11 Dec 2026, the broader §781 amendments apply — total-THC hemp definition + 0.4 mg total THC per container cap for finished products. A standard 10 mg delta-9 gummy that is lawful hemp in Texas under the 0.3% dry-weight test would, on the federal test as described in the baseline, fail the federal 0.4 mg/container cap by 25× — that is a federal-law consequence (prosecution / carrier / bank / payment-processor risk), not a Texas-law change, unless Texas amends Ag. Code §121.001 / HSC §443.001. Texas is already stricter than the old federal delta-9-only test in two ways that pre-date Dec 2026: (1) total-delta-9 testing in Ch. 300 (Mar 2026), and (2) the July 2026 delta-8 schedule. Texas is less strict than the coming federal cap in that Texas has no milligram-per-container cap. No Texas agency response (DSHS/TDA/TABC guidance, emergency rule, or AG opinion) specifically addressing §781 / §2019 was opened in this run — [Verify] / Unavailable in this run, not “no response exists.” USDA DEA-lab delay to 31 Dec 2026 and the federal medical rider through 11 Dec 2026 are federal points; they do not change Texas Ch. 443 / Ch. 300 testing or TCUP licensing dates. HSC §443.001(5) cross-refers to Ag. Code §121.001 — a freestanding 2019 definition with no cross-reference to 7 U.S.C. §1639o. That is why Texas does not automatically import the new federal total-THC / 0.4 mg definition.

1.6 Packaging, labelling, advertising, delivery, zoning / buffers

Low-THC (TCUP): Packaging/labelling governed by HSC Ch. 487 + DPS 37 TAC Ch. 12 (licensing, testing, labelling, inventory — [Verify] section numbers; not opened in this run). HB 46’s 10 mg / 1 g limits are package-level rules. No advertising, delivery, zoning or buffer-distance rule for TCUP dispensaries was verified from an opened official text in this run — [Verify] / Unavailable in Part 4/11 run; do not invent a buffer (e.g. 1,000 ft from schools) for TCUP. Satellite locations require DPS approval under HB 46; that is a location-approval rule, not a zoning code.

Consumable hemp: Packaging: child-resistant (Ch. 300 adopted rules); label: manufacturer, batch/lot, COA QR/link, cannabinoid amount/concentration, serving size, health warnings (GA-56 direction + §§300.402–300.407 — [Verify] verbatim list). Advertising: No enacted Texas CHP advertising statute was found. SB 3’s advertising bans (no ads at sports/charity events, within 300 ft of schools/churches/playgrounds/day-cares/shelters/treatment centres, no child-attractive packaging/marketing) were in a vetoed / dead bill — they are NOT law. GA-56’s direction on labelling/marketing to protect children was implemented via DSHS labelling rules, not via a freestanding advertising code — [Verify] whether adopted Ch. 300 contains an advertising section; if not, state “no rule found.” Delivery: DSHS registrants / TABC holders must age-verify on delivery (ID check before completing sale/service/delivery). No statewide hemp-delivery licence was found — [Verify]. Zoning / buffers: No statewide hemp zoning or buffer statute was found. Veto-proclamation suggestions (no sale near schools/churches/playgrounds) were suggestions, not enacted buffers. Local zoning is Part 3.

1.7 Seed-to-sale / tracking

TCUP: DPS requires inventory / tracking through CURT + dispensing-organisation records under 37 TAC Ch. 12 (secondary summaries). No public “Metrc-style” statewide seed-to-sale system name was verified in this run — [Verify] system name / whether Texas uses a named vendor. Hemp: No statewide seed-to-sale tracking system for CHPs was found. TDA hemp cultivation requires plot / lot registration and pre-harvest sampling under Ch. 122 (Part 7/11 detail), and DSHS requires batch/lot COA traceability, but that is not a unified seed-to-sale system. State this as “no unified system found” rather than asserting none exists.

1.8 Employment and drug-testing protections — confirmed lack

No employment protection for TCUP patients in Texas law. HSC Ch. 487 / Occupations Code Ch. 169 contain no anti-discrimination, accommodation, or hiring-protection clause (confirmed by absence in the statute summaries and by multiple secondary employment summaries). Texas has no comprehensive private-employer drug-testing statute; employers may test pre-employment, randomly, on reasonable suspicion, and post-accident under a written policy. Texas Workforce Commission guidance recommends a written policy; for workers’ compensation subscribers with ≥15 employees, a written drug-abuse policy is required (Labor Code provisions summarised secondarily). A post-accident test showing a voluntarily-introduced controlled substance creates a rebuttable presumption of intoxication for workers’ comp (Labor Code §401.013, secondary). Consequence: an employer may refuse to hire / terminate for a positive THC test, including for a TCUP patient and including for lawful hemp use that produces metabolites. Falsifying a drug test is a separate offence (HSC §481.133, secondary). DOT safety-sensitive testing (49 CFR Part 40) is federal and has no state medical exception. [Verify] the exact Labor Code / TWC citations on official sites before republication; the conclusion (no protection) is consistent across sources, the citation detail is Verify.

1.9 Pending legislation and interim studies

Session structure: Texas Legislature meets in biennial regular sessions in odd-numbered years (89th Leg., 2025; next regular session 90th Leg., convening 12 Jan 2027, per secondary interim-charge reporting). The Governor may call called special sessions (30 days each, limited to the Governor’s proclamation topics). There is no regular session in 2026; 2026 work is interim charges, agency rulemaking, and litigation.

Dead / vetoed — do NOT present as pending: SB 3 (89th R.S.) — VETOED 22 June 2025. SB 5 (89th 1st Spec.) — DEAD (Senate-passed, House-died). SB 6 (89th 2nd Spec.) — DEAD (Senate 22–8, House-died, sine die 3 Sept 2025). SB 53 (89th 1st Spec., TABC regulation + tax) — DEAD. SB 30 (89th 2nd Spec., TABC regulation + tax) — DEAD. HB 5643 (89th R.S., Cain — hemp regulation + 5% excise, referred to State Affairs 7 Apr 2025) — DEAD (Introduced-Dead, LegiScan). HB 63 (86th R.S., 2019) / HB 441 (87th R.S., 2021) / HB 335 (86th R.S.) — decriminalisation / penalty-reduction bills that would have made ≤1 oz / ≤2 g a Class C misdemeanour — DEAD, not law; §481.121 tiers above remain current. They are cited here only to prevent confusion with current law.

Interim charges ahead of the 2027 session (2026 interim, secondary reporting, [Verify] on lt.gov / house.texas.gov charge letters): Senate Health and Human Services Committee — Lt. Gov. Dan Patrick, interim charges released Mar 2026: “Addressing Societal Impacts of THC Product Consumption” — study impact of THC on increased health-care costs, mental-health emergency detentions, and risk of THC-induced psychotic disorder; make recommendations to decrease health-care and criminal-justice costs. Sen. Charles Perry (author of SB 3/5/6) stated at July 2026 interim hearings he will file a new THC ban in Jan 2027 (secondary, stated intention — not a filed bill). House Public Health Committee — Speaker Dustin Burrows, interim charges 26 Mar 2026: monitor implementation/rulemaking for the 2025 ibogaine clinical-trial consortium bill (psychedelic, not cannabis) — listed here only because it is the House Public Health interim charge most often confused with a cannabis charge. No House interim charge specifically directing a TCUP or hemp-THC study was verified in this run — [Verify] House charge list. Senate Health & Human Services interim hearings 7–8 July 2026 (secondary): DSHS reported 10,515 THC exposure calls and 19 THC-related deaths since 2021; Texas Police Chiefs Association estimated ~9,000 retail stores selling THC products — secondary hearing testimony, not official agency statistics; mark as such.

Pending as of 31 Oct 2026: No filed 90th Leg. bill exists yet (session has not convened; pre-filing begins Nov 2026 — [Verify] pre-filing date on TLO). Any “2027 hemp ban” or “2027 regulation” bill is anticipated, not pending. No decriminalisation bill enacted in 2025 was found; the only enacted cannabis measure in the 2022–2026 window identified in a 2026 review was HB 46.

PART 2: TAXES

2.1 Sales and use tax

Rate (official, Comptroller, accessed 31 Oct 2026): “Texas imposes a 6.25 percent state sales and use tax on all retail sales, leases and rentals of most goods, as well as taxable services. Local taxing jurisdictions (cities, counties, special purpose districts and transit authorities) can also impose up to 2 percent sales and use tax for a maximum combined rate of 8.25 percent.” Sources: Comptroller sales tax; Comptroller publication 94-105. Tax Code Ch. 151 (state) + Subtitle C (local).

Application to cannabis/hemp — settled vs. Verify: No cannabis-specific sales-tax exemption or special rate exists in Texas law. Retail sales of tangible personal property are generally taxable. Secondary tax guides consistently state both TCUP and hemp retail sales are subject to regular sales tax, with no separate cannabis excise tax in Texas. That conclusion is consistent with the absence of any excise in HSC Chs. 443/487 — but no Comptroller ruling specifically addressing TCUP low-THC cannabis or CHPs was opened in this run. Mark the product-level application as [Verify] with the Comptroller (e.g. whether a prescribed low-THC product is treated as a taxable tangible good vs. a drug/medicine, and whether any food/drug exemption in Tax Code §151.313 / §151.317 could apply to a specific CHP form such as a food vs. a topical vs. a supplement). Do not assert a medicine exemption for TCUP without that ruling. Food nuance (Comptroller Rule 3.293, secondary): unprepared food products (flour, bread, milk, eggs) are generally non-taxable; prepared food ready for immediate consumption is taxable. A hemp gummy / beverage sold ready-to-eat would therefore be expected to be taxable as prepared food / tangible property, while a bulk hemp-seed ingredient might not be — [Verify] product-by-product with Comptroller Publication 96-280 / Rule 3.293; do not generalise. Local sales tax: up to 2% combined local (city + county + transit + special district), capped so state + local ≤8.25%. Rate is determined by delivery / consummation location; Comptroller Sales Tax Rate Locator by address. Remote sellers: single statewide local use tax election 1.75% (secondary, [Verify]). Mixed-beverage tax: if a hemp beverage is sold on-premises mixed with alcohol by a TABC holder, mixed-beverage gross receipts / sales taxes could apply in addition — [Verify]; SB 3 fiscal note (May 2025) said mixed-beverage implications could not be estimated.

2.2 Hemp-specific excise, licence and registration fees

Current (official, DSHS page opened 31 Oct 2026): “The Consumable Hemp Product license fee has been restored to $10,000 annually per location, plus the applicable Texas Online (TOL) fee. The Retail Hemp Registration has been restored to $5,000 annually per location, plus the applicable TOL fee. These fees and other requirements are available in 25 TAC 300.” Source: DSHS programme. Secondary payment pages report $5,150 retail payable including TOL (i.e. $5,000 + $150 TOL) — the $150 difference is the TOL surcharge, not a different rule fee; [Verify] current TOL amount at payment. DSHS manufacturer licence: $10,000 / year / location + TOL. DSHS retail registration: $5,000 / year / location + TOL (including online retailers selling into Texas, per adopted §300.502 — [Verify] online-retailer scope). These are fees, not excise taxes; they are credited to the consumable hemp programme, not to general sales-tax revenue.

Enacted-but-not-yet-effective hemp tax/fee: None found. No 2025–2026 enacted law creates a future hemp excise or a future fee change that is merely awaiting an effective date. The Dec 2025 proposed $25,000 / $20,000 fees were proposals; the adopted Mar 2026 rules restored the old fees. Do not list the proposed fees as enacted. Before the 25 TAC Chapter 300 rules effective 31 March 2026, the fees were $155 retail / $258 manufacturer (secondary sources consistently, and DSHS history).

TCUP fees: DPS dispensing-organisation licensing fees are set by DPS rule under HSC §487.052 (“in amounts sufficient to cover the cost of administering this chapter”) and 37 TAC Ch. 12. Exact current DPS fee schedule was not opened in the Parts 0–2 run — see Part 4 for the 37 TAC §12.14 schedule opened there (application $7,356; new licence 2-year $488,520; renewal $318,511; registration $530). DPS stated (Dec 2025, secondary) it would not invoice new licensees until due-diligence evaluations were complete.

TDA hemp grower/handler fees: Under Ag. Code Ch. 122 / 4 TAC Ch. 24 — not verified in the Parts 0–2 run; Part 7 must supply fees from TDA. Do not estimate. (Parts 3–4 track reports, [Verify] against live 4 TAC Ch. 24 / eApply: producer $100 + $100 per facility, handler same, sampler $100, lab $250, research $100, lot $100.)

2.3 IRS §280E and Texas franchise tax

Federal §280E (baseline given): Generally lifted for Schedule III medical activity; adult-use remains fully subject. That is a federal income-tax rule. It does not itself create a Texas deduction.

Texas has no state income tax. Texas imposes a franchise (margin) tax under Tax Code Ch. 171 — a privilege tax on taxable entities doing business in Texas, computed on taxable margin (lowest of: total revenue − cost of goods sold; total revenue − compensation; total revenue × 70%; total revenue − $1 million), at 0.75% generally and 0.375% for entities primarily engaged in retail/wholesale trade (Tax Code §171.002, FindLaw current-as-of-1 Jan 2026 summary, accessed 31 Oct 2026: §171.002). No-tax-due threshold: tax <$1,000 or total revenue ≤$2.47m (older FindLaw note) / $2.65m for 2026 (secondary 2026 threshold update) — [Verify] current threshold in §171.002(d) / Comptroller guidance; thresholds are inflation-adjusted and the two figures reflect different years.

Does Texas conform to §280E? — Historically NO, now COMPLICATED — [Verify] for any specific taxpayer. A secondary conformity chart (Maryland legislature paper, citing Tex. Tax Code §§171.0001(9), 171.1011(c)) states: “Texas does not conform to IRC §280E. Texas uses federal gross income (as that term is defined in the IRC in effect on January 1, 2007), except for cost of goods sold, as the starting point…” That is, Texas margin historically did not import the §280E disallowance as a separate adjustment; COGS remained deductible in margin even if §280E denied other deductions federally. 2026 change: Comptroller Tax Policy memo 19 Dec 2025 + proposed/adopted 34 TAC §3.587 (effective for reports due on/after 1 Jan 2026; rule amended eff. 1 Mar 2026 per secondary) move total-revenue line items to current federal law (not the 2007 IRC baseline), except where the statute/rule expressly references the IRC. Secondary BDO / Otterz summaries report this as “selective / rolling conformity for total revenue and COGS.” Consequence — [Verify], do not advise: (a) A hemp business (not a §280E business federally, because hemp is not a Schedule I/II controlled substance) was never a §280E business for federal purposes; its Texas margin issue is ordinary COGS/compensation, not §280E. (b) A TCUP dispensing organisation, if its product is Schedule III federally, would generally escape federal §280E going forward per the baseline — but whether its Texas margin changes depends on (i) whether Schedule III applies to low-THC cannabis in Texas (see §1.2 box, Verify), and (ii) how the 2026 conformity rule treats its federal return lines. (c) An adult-use operator (illegal in Texas) would remain fully §280E-exposed federally and would have no lawful Texas margin position to claim. No Texas statute was found that expressly adopts or decouples from §280E for franchise tax. Secondary tax guides that say “Texas follows federal 280E” are oversimplified / incorrect on the historic margin analysis and should not be relied on without the Comptroller memo and §171.1011–171.1012 text.

2.4 Revenue figures — official first, and the gap

Official revenue: NO official Texas figure for TCUP sales, CHP sales, or hemp sales-tax revenue was opened in this run. Comptroller / Legislative Budget Board SB 3 fiscal note (9 May 2025, official): “reliable data on sales volumes and average milligram dosages of these products in the state is not available; consequently, the total revenue to be expected from excise taxation … cannot be estimated” — only a minimum $15m/yr excise placeholder. Two-year net impact to General Revenue-Related Funds for CSSB 3 (House committee substitute) was estimated at −$37,081,547 through biennium ending 31 Aug 2027 (transfer of DSHS→TABC functions + dedication of sales-tax revenue, not a market-size figure). Source: SB 3 fiscal note (PDF). DSHS licence/registration fee revenue, TDA licence revenue, and Comptroller sales-tax collections attributable to hemp/TCUP NAICS codes were not published in opened sources — Unavailable in this run; Part 5/6 must check Comptroller revenue-by-NAICS / DSHS annual reports. Secondary (industry) revenue figures are in Part 0 / Part 6 and must be labelled industry estimates, not official revenue: Whitney Economics $5.547bn (2024) / $267.7m sales tax; March 2026 Whitney retail $4.3bn. Do not present either as Comptroller revenue.

PART 3: LOCAL RULES

3.1 Who can do what: counties, cities, CUP facilities, hemp/CBD/smoke/vape shops

Low-THC facilities — local government CANNOT ban them. Verbatim statute, as quoted in the HB 46 engrossed text and on the DPS FAQ page (opened 31 Oct 2026, DPS facility FAQ): “A municipality, county, or other political subdivision may not enact, adopt, or enforce a rule, ordinance, order, resolution, or other regulation that prohibits the cultivation, production, storage, dispensing, or possession of low-THC cannabis, as authorized by this chapter.” — Tex. Health & Safety Code §487.201. DPS states the same conclusion in its FAQ. That is a prohibition on prohibition, not a grant of local licensing power. DPS, not a city or county, licenses the dispensing organisation. DPS also states, on the same FAQ page: “All cultivation, processing and dispensing must be performed at a single location. Remote pickup locations are authorized so long as the product has already been dispensed and labeled for the receiving patient and the products are not stored overnight.” “Deliveries may only be made by registered employees of the dispensing organization. Delivery vehicles must meet the security requirements provided in Texas Administrative Rule §12.32.” “All cultivation of low-THC cannabis shall take place in an enclosed, secured building, or an enclosure within a building. See Texas Administrative Rule §12.31.”

[Verify — conflicting secondary claim]: Some secondary county guides claim a CUP facility must be “not less than 1,000 feet from a public or private school or daycare.” That distance does not appear on the DPS facility FAQ opened for this report, and no 37 TAC §12.31 text was opened in this session confirming it. Do not rely on a 1,000-ft CUP buffer until 37 TAC Chapter 12 is read in full. The 1,000-ft figures documented below are for city vape/smoke-shop ordinances, not for CUP facilities.

HB 46 satellite locations are a separate, state-approved mechanism (see Part 4.1): a licensed organisation “may operate one or more satellite locations in addition to the organization’s primary location to securely store low-THC cannabis for distribution,” but “[a] satellite location must be approved by the department before a dispensing organization may operate the location” — Tex. Health & Safety Code §487.1035, added by HB 46, 89th Legislature, Regular Session, signed 20 June 2025, effective 1 September 2025. Source: HB 46 Senate text.

General municipal zoning — cities zone land use; counties generally do not. Tex. Local Government Code Chapter 211 governs municipal zoning. §211.003(a) allows a municipality’s governing body to regulate, among other things, “the location and use of buildings, other structures, and land for business, industrial, residential, or other purposes,” and §211.004 requires zoning to be in accordance with a comprehensive plan. Texas counties have no general zoning power (with the narrow, long-standing Hood County exception noted in secondary zoning literature). Counties instead exercise health, safety, building, subdivision/platting, and nuisance-type powers, and can regulate in unincorporated areas only where a statute expressly grants the power. Secondary summaries, accessed 31 Oct 2026: Texas A&M RE Center zoning explainer.

LevelCUP dispensing organisationHemp / CBD / smoke / vape retail
State (DPS)Sole licensor; city/county cannot ban authorised cultivation, production, storage, dispensing or possession (§487.201)—
State (DSHS)—Manufacturer licence / retail registration required statewide, including for out-of-state online sellers into Texas (see Part 4.2)
State (TABC)—Age-21 / ID rules apply to TABC licensees/permittees selling consumable hemp products (16 TAC §§35.5, 35.6, eff. 21 Jan 2026); TABC permit is not a substitute for DSHS registration
City (inside city limits)General zoning, building/fire codes, and use-location rules short of a ban on authorised CUP activity; DPS-approved site and satellite rules still controlGeneral zoning, use definitions, buffers, special-use permits, moratoriums, and business-permit conditions, subject to state law — see §3.3
County (unincorporated)Cannot ban authorised CUP activity (§487.201)No general zoning; limited health/safety and other expressly-granted powers only

Local power over enforcement of drug laws — the hard limit. Verbatim, Tex. Local Government Code §370.003 (added by Acts 1997, 75th Leg., ch. 971, §1, eff. 1 Sept. 1997): “The governing body of a municipality, the commissioners court of a county, or a sheriff, municipal police department, municipal attorney, county attorney, district attorney, or criminal district attorney may not adopt a policy under which the entity will not fully enforce laws relating to drugs, including Chapters 481 and 483, Health and Safety Code, and federal law.” Source (mirror, accessed 31 Oct 2026): §370.003. The Fifteenth Court of Appeals quotes the same text in State v. City of San Marcos, No. 15-24-00084-CV. This section is the legal basis for every Attorney General challenge below. It does not itself create a local hemp-shop licensing scheme, and it does not authorise a city to legalise marijuana. It bars a local non-enforcement policy. Tex. Const. art. XI, §5(a) is the second ground relied on by the State: “no … ordinance passed under [a city] charter shall contain any provision inconsistent with the Constitution of the State, or of the general laws enacted by the Legislature of this State” — quoted in the San Marcos opinion, San Marcos opinion (opened).

3.2 Local decriminalisation ordinances

Common template. The Austin, San Marcos, Killeen, Denton, Elgin and Dallas measures are materially the same Ground Game Texas / Mano Amiga model: no citations or arrests for Class A or B misdemeanor possession (≤4 oz; Class B ≤2 oz, Class A >2 oz to 4 oz), except as part of (1) a high-priority felony narcotics investigation designated by a commander/assistant chief/chief, or (2) a violent-felony investigation; if marijuana is seized and possession is the sole charge, the officer must write a detailed report and release the person; no residue/paraphernalia citation in lieu of a possession charge; no city funds for THC testing; no use of marijuana/hemp odour as probable cause for search/seizure.

Verbatim — San Marcos, as reproduced in the Fifteenth Court opinion (opened 31 Oct 2026): “Sec. 54.101. – Ending citations and arrests for misdemeanor possession of marijuana. (a) San Marcos police officers shall not issue citations or make arrests for class A or class B misdemeanor possession of marijuana offenses, except in the limited circumstances described in subsection (b). (b) The only circumstances in which San Marcos police officers are permitted to issue citations or make arrests for class A or class B misdemeanor possession of marijuana are when such citations or arrests are part of (1) the investigation of a felony level narcotics case that has been designated as a high priority investigation by [a] San Marcos police commander, assistant chief of police, or chief of police; and/or (2) the investigation of a violent felony. (c) In every instance other than those described in subsection (b), if a San Marcos police officer has probable cause to believe that a substance is marijuana, an officer may seize the marijuana. If the officer seizes the marijuana, they must write a detailed report and release the individual if possession of marijuana is the sole charge. (d) San Marcos police officers shall not issue any charge for possession of marijuana unless it meets one or both of the factors described in subsection (b).” The opinion adds that the San Marcos ordinance further restricts (1) residue/paraphernalia citations, (2) use of city funds to test THC levels, and (3) use of marijuana/hemp odour as probable cause.

Verbatim — Austin ballot proposition (City of Austin election ordinance, opened via search result): “Proposition A: Shall an initiative ordinance be approved to (1) eliminate enforcement of low-level marijuana offenses and (2) ban the use of ‘no knock’ warrants by Austin police?” Austin Code Title 16, Ch. 16-1, §16-1-1, as set out in that ordinance: “Austin police officers shall not issue citations or make arrests for Class A or Class B misdemeanor possession of marijuana offenses, except in the limited circumstances described in §16-1-1(B)” — the same two felony-investigation exceptions as San Marcos. Source: Austin election ordinance.

Important: none of these ordinances legalises marijuana. Possession remains a state offence under Tex. Health & Safety Code §481.121. The ordinances purport only to direct local police not to cite/arrest in the stated circumstances.

AG lawsuits — filed January 2024 (five cities) + Dallas (November 2024). Attorney General Ken Paxton sued Austin, Denton, Elgin, Killeen and San Marcos in January 2024 (announced 31 Jan 2024), and Dallas after its November 2024 vote, on the grounds that the ordinances violate Tex. Const. art. XI, §5 and Local Government Code §370.003.

SB 1870 (2025) — the bill that would have codified the AG position — DEAD, NOT LAW. SB 1870, 89th Legislature, Regular Session, by Perry — “Relating to municipal and county enforcement of drug and consumable hemp product laws; providing civil penalties.” What it would have done (committee substitute): create new Local Government Code Chapter 366, transfer §370.003 into it as §366.002, expressly bar a local entity from adopting or enforcing an ordinance/policy under which it will not fully enforce state drug or consumable hemp laws (HSC Chapters 443, 481, 483), bar placing such an item on a ballot including a charter amendment, create a citizen complaint to the AG, AG mandamus/equitable relief, and civil penalties of $25,000 first offence / $50,000 subsequent. Source: SB 1870 text. History: passed Senate 23–8 on 23 April 2025; House State Affairs Committee advanced it, but it died in the House — LegiScan status “Engrossed – Dead,” last action 6 May 2025, committee report sent to Calendars. SB 1870 is NOT current law. The pre-existing §370.003 (1997) is what the courts applied. Do not cite SB 1870’s $25,000/$50,000 penalties or its ballot-placement ban as operative. A related 2025 proposal, SB 2858 (broader AG penalties for non-compliant cities/counties, Senate-passed 19–12 on 30 April 2025), is likewise not shown as enacted in this session — [Verify] on Texas Legislature Online before any use. Related proposed bill, also dead: SB 1315 (2025), which would have expressly authorised a county (unincorporated areas) or city to prohibit cigarette/e-cigarette/tobacco sales within 300 ft of a church/public hospital/school, or 1,000 ft on school request. Status “Introduced – Dead,” 2 Apr 2025. It is not the authority for the Brownsville/Harlingen vape-shop rules below, which rest on general municipal zoning/permitting power.

Bottom line for §3.2 at 31 Oct 2026: The only decriminalisation ordinances with a located, final appellate disposition are San Marcos (preempted, injunction on remand, Supreme Court review denied) and Austin (preempted, injunction on remand). Elgin is repealed. Denton is reported repealed. Dallas is stayed. Killeen, Harker Heights, Bastrop and Lockhart have no located final disposition in this session and must not be described as safely in force.

3.3 Local zoning, moratoriums, caps/bans on hemp/CBD/smoke/vape shops; local permit fees

There is no statewide Texas local hemp-shop licence or local hemp-shop fee schedule. DSHS registration/licence is state-level and per-location (Part 4.2). What cities do is use ordinary zoning, use definitions, special-use permits (SUPs), distance rules and temporary moratoriums. Examples located (all secondary reporting of city action — official ordinance texts were not opened for every city, so each is flagged accordingly):

Ed Carey Dr. tabled for moratorium periodAdopted 21 Jan 2026; agenda item again 4 Feb 2026Temporary only; expiry/extension [Verify] — 180 days from 21 Jan 2026 would run to ~July 2026; whether extended is not confirmed

Breckenridge (Stephens County)Ordinance No. 2026-14 (Aug 2026) defined “vape shop” (retail primarily for e-cigarettes, vaping devices, edibles and other devices for inhalation/consumption of tobacco, CBD or other hemp-derived products, plus paraphernalia, no on-site smoking/consumption) and added it to General Business Retail table with no zoning district marked as permitting it — effectively banning new vape shops; existing shops treated as legal non-conforming. Source: Breckenridge Texan 21 Oct 2026

No local hemp-specific permit fee was located for any of the §3.4 jurisdictions. Cities charge ordinary business, building, sign, fire and SUP fees under their own fee schedules; none was opened in this session, so no fee figure is stated here. Do not invent a “city hemp permit fee.” If Part 8 / Part 5 work needs a city fee, it must be pulled from that city’s adopted fee schedule. No city-wide cap (numerical quota) on hemp/CBD shops was located in the jurisdictions checked, other than the de facto effects of distance rules and moratoriums above. A distance rule is not a cap, and a moratorium is not a permanent ban.

3.4 Official list of local ordinances — NONE FOUND

There is no official statewide list, registry or dashboard of Texas local cannabis/hemp ordinances located in this session — not from DPS, DSHS, TDA, TABC, the Attorney General, or the Texas Municipal League in the sources opened/searched. Each city’s code must be checked individually (e.g. Austin Code, Dallas City Code, San Marcos Code, Killeen Code, Denton Code, Elgin Code, etc.). Jurisdictions checked for this report (minimum list required, plus additional cities where local action was found): Houston / Harris County; Dallas; San Antonio / Bexar County; Austin / Travis County; Fort Worth / Tarrant County; El Paso; Arlington; Corpus Christi; Plano; Lubbock; Laredo; Irving; Amarillo; Brownsville, Texas (see §3.3 — not Brownsville, Tennessee); McAllen; Killeen; Denton; San Marcos; Harker Heights; plus Harlingen, Alamo, Breckenridge, Bastrop, Lockhart.

For Houston, Dallas, San Antonio, Fort Worth, El Paso, Arlington, Corpus Christi, Plano, Lubbock, Laredo, Irving, Amarillo and McAllen, no hemp/CBD/smoke-shop-specific zoning ordinance, moratorium, cap or local permit fee was located in this session beyond general zoning/smoking rules. That is a search result, not proof of absence — a city code search (e.g. for “smoke shop,” “vape shop,” “tobacco retailer,” “hemp”) in each city’s adopted code is the follow-up for Part 5/Part 8. [Verify] per city before stating “no ordinance exists” in the final dossier. Same-name caution applied: Paris, Texas; Athens, Texas; Austin, Texas (not Austin, Minnesota); Arlington, Texas (not Arlington, Virginia); Pasadena, Texas (not Pasadena, California); Midland, Texas (not Midland, Michigan); Odessa, Texas (not Odessa, Ukraine); Abilene, Texas (not Abilene, Kansas); Frisco, Texas (not Frisco, Colorado); Plano, Texas (not Plano, Illinois); Lancaster, Texas (not Lancaster, Pennsylvania); Jacksonville, Texas (not Jacksonville, Florida); Victoria, Texas; Brownsville, Texas (not Brownsville, Tennessee); San Marcos, Texas (not San Marcos, California); Wichita Falls, Texas (not Wichita, Kansas); Santa Fe, Texas (not Santa Fe, New Mexico); Texarkana, Texas is separate from Texarkana, Arkansas.

3.5 Business concentration — ONLY what official sources say

Do not rank clusters from this section. No official DSHS county-by-county table of retail registrations was located in this session. What is officially reported (via a July 2026 DSHS presentation, as reported secondarily): 14,170 consumable-hemp retail locations and 778 manufacturers as of 18 June 2026 (DSHS presentation, July 2026, reported at Next Level Leaf Texas resource). A second secondary source, summarising the same period, gives “more than 13,850 shops” and “757 businesses licensed to produce” as of April 2026 (hempcbdbusinessplans Texas industry page) — the difference is a date difference, not a conflict to resolve by averaging; use the June 2026 figure as the most recent. DSHS maintains a searchable Current Licensees and Registrants roster (search by name, licence type, city or county) at DSHS current licensees — it is a search tool, not a published county ranking, and no county totals were extracted from it in this session. DSHS compliance-action lists name individual businesses by city (e.g. Humble, Lantana, Dallas, Pasadena, Texas, San Antonio, San Marcos in the 2025–2026 actions PDF) — those are enforcement examples, not a concentration measure, and must not be used to rank cities. Source: DSHS compliance actions PDF.

Therefore, for Part 3.5, the honest official-source statement is: Texas has a very large, statewide consumable-hemp retail system (~14,000+ retail locations statewide in mid-2026); no official county concentration ranking was found, and none is offered here. Any county ranking in the final dossier would require a full download/count of the DSHS roster by county — a Part 5 task, not an estimate to be made here.

PART 4: LICENCE TYPES

4.1 Compassionate Use Program dispensing organisation licence

What it allows — vertically integrated, single licence type. Texas has one cannabis business licence type: the dispensing organisation licence under Tex. Health & Safety Code Chapter 487, administered by DPS. It authorises the holder to cultivate, process/manufacture, and dispense low-THC cannabis to patients (and their legal guardians) registered in CURT, on a physician’s prescription entered in CURT. There is no separate cultivation-only, processing-only, retail-only, delivery-only or testing-lab licence under Chapter 487. Statute: HSC Chapter 487; physician-prescription framework: Occupations Code Chapter 169. Rules: 37 TAC Chapter 12. DPS: “All cultivation, processing and dispensing must be performed at a single location” (pre-HB 46 FAQ language; HB 46 satellite locations now modify storage/distribution — see below). Source: DPS facility FAQ (opened).

Status at 31 Oct 2026CountWho
Fully licensed and dispensing3Texas Original (Compassionate Cultivation dba Texas Original), goodblend (Surterra Texas LLC dba goodblend), Fluent (Cansortium Texas dba Fluent) — brand/entity pairings from American Bar Association summary (Feb 2026) and multiple secondary directories; DPS’s own operational list should be re-checked before publication [Verify entity legal names against live DPS list]
Conditionally awarded, NOT authorised to cultivate/manufacture/distribute/sell until final DPS approval12See corrected list below
Total authorised by HB 46153 pre-existing + 12 new

The 2025 expansion — HB 46, 89th Legislature, Regular Session. HB 46, by King et al. (Senate sponsor Perry), signed 20 June 2025, effective 1 September 2025. It did not create adult-use or full-medical licensing; it expanded the low-THC programme. Licence-number provision (§487.104(d), as analysed in the Senate Research Center bill analysis): DPS “shall issue 15 licenses … provided that DPS receives applications from a sufficient number of applicants meeting the requirements for eligibility.” Source: HB 46 bill analysis (TLO). Phasing provision (HB 46, uncodified transition section, as summarised in the bill analysis and in DPS notices): DPS shall, provided applicants meet Subchapter C requirements, (1) not later than 1 December 2025, license at least nine new dispensing organisations from applications submitted before 1 July 2025; and (2) not later than 1 April 2026, in addition to those, license at least three new dispensing organisations from applications submitted at any time. Application window for the new round (secondary, consistent across sources): 15 August – 15 September 2025, closing 5:00 p.m. CT — [Verify] against DPS new-applications page before citing as the sole window; Phase I was drawn in part from pre-July 2025 / 2023 applications. 24-month operational deadline: “House Bill 46 also requires that businesses issued TCUP licenses must become fully operational within 24 months of licensure. If a business that is issued a TCUP license fails to meet this requirement, the department will refer to the TCUP license eligibility list for a replacement.” — DPS corrected-tabulation notice (opened).

Corrected conditional list — SUPERSEDES all earlier Phase I / Phase II lists. DPS issued a corrected tabulation (HQ 2026-046; announcement graphic dated 8 May 2026 in the DPS image file) after discovering that the internal tabulation had not applied the published equal 25% weighting to each of the four scoring categories. Three companies previously announced were rescinded to the eligibility list, and three previously unannounced companies were added. Use this list, not the December 2025 / April 2026 press lists. Source: DPS corrected tabulation (opened).
