DPS warning, verbatim: “Conditional licenses do not grant the applicant permission to cultivate, manufacture, distribute or sell cannabis products until final approval by the department” and “The announcement of these businesses today does not guarantee that these businesses will be issued final TCUP licenses to operate as dispensing organizations.” DPS will not invoice licence fees until due-diligence evaluations are completed and passed. Eligibility list after correction (12, for replacement if a conditional licensee fails): Story of Texas, LLC; Cresco Labs Texas, LLC; Lone Star Life, LLC; MSC Lone Star, LLC; Texas Medica Collective, LLC; Village Farms, LP; Sua Vaping Inc dba VI Vape City; Yes TX, LLC; JG Texas LLC dba Justice Cannabis Co; Crown Grove Investments, LLC dba Smoke and Vape; Texas Health Systems, Inc; GB Farms Texas Investments, LLC. Conflict flagged: Secondary sources published before the correction still list Texas Medica Collective, LLC (Region 4) and Cresco Labs Texas, LLC (Region 5) as Phase II conditional licensees, and Story of Texas, LLC as a Phase I conditional licensee. Under the corrected tabulation opened here, all three are on the eligibility list only. The corrected DPS notice controls.

Satellite / dispensing locations. New §487.1035: a licensed organisation may operate one or more satellite locations in addition to its primary location to securely store low-THC cannabis for distribution; each satellite must be approved by DPS before operation; DPS must act on an application within 90 days (Senate committee-report version) and must adopt design/security rules for satellites. No additional licence is required for a satellite if its address was in the original application or DPS approval under §487.1035 is obtained (§487.101(b)). Pre-1 September 2025 DPS-approved patient pickup locations are treated as approved satellites (HB 46 transition language). DPS FAQ (pre-HB 46 language, still published): remote pickup locations are authorised as an extension of delivery, provided product is already dispensed/labelled for the receiving patient and not stored overnight — that overnight-storage limit is the point HB 46 satellites change, by allowing DPS-approved secure storage. [Verify] the current 37 TAC satellite rule text before advising on overnight storage at a specific site. No statutory cap on the number of satellites per organisation was located in the HB 46 text opened; the enacted §487.1035 language in the introduced/engrossed versions also contained a provision barring DPS from requiring a satellite in a region until the organisation operates one in each public health region in some drafts — [Verify] final enrolled §487.1035(d)–(f) text; do not cite draft satellite-distribution language as law.

Fees (37 TAC §12.14, confirmed on DPS FAQ opened 31 Oct 2026). Application fee (non-refundable) $7,356. Licence fee, new licence, 2-year period $488,520. Biennial renewal licence fee $318,511. Registration fee (original and renewal, per registration) $530. Payment electronically, in the manner determined by DPS; payment required upon licence approval; DPS states it will not invoice conditional licensees until due diligence is passed. Sources: DPS application FAQ (opened); 37 TAC §12.14 (mirror).

Requirements, renewal, transfers — summary (statute + 37 TAC Ch. 12, as summarised secondarily where the rule text was not opened). Eligibility (§487.102, HB 46 version): applicant must demonstrate, as determined by DPS, technical/technological ability to cultivate, process and dispense; ability to maintain security, inventory control, and testing protocols; and other Subchapter C requirements. Application must include name/address of applicant, directors/owners/managers/members/employees, satellite addresses, and other information DPS requires. Registration: DPS registers directors, owners, managers, members and employees of each dispensing organisation (HB 46 added owners/members to §487.053(a)). HB 46 also requires fingerprint-based background checks for persons owning 10% or more (secondary summary of HB 46 — [Verify] enrolled text). Residency: No Texas residency requirement for applicants (DPS FAQ, Q3: “No.”). Reduced fee: No — DPS FAQ, Q2: “No. The application fee must be paid to complete the application process.” Renewal: biennial, $318,511 + $530 registration, subject to compliance/inspection. DPS conducts regular physical inspections. Ownership change / transfer / relocation: No specific transfer rule was opened in this session — 37 TAC Chapter 12, Subchapter C (Compliance and Enforcement) and the licence-application rules govern amendments; [Verify] transfer, relocation and change-of-ownership provisions in 37 TAC §§12.11–12.14 and Subchapter C before advising a transaction. Do not assume a CUP licence is freely transferable. No new application window is open at 31 Oct 2026 on the record opened (the HB 46 window closed Sept 2025; the 12 new licences are the HB 46 tranche).

4.2 Consumable hemp licences and registrations

Two DSHS credentials — the distinction turns on what you do to the product, not what you call yourself. DSHS Licensing and Registration page (opened 31 Oct 2026): DSHS licensing and registration.

CredentialRequired if…TermFee (incl. Texas Online fee)
DSHS Retail Hemp RegistrationSeller retail-sells consumable hemp products (CHPs) and doesn’t make any changes, which includes adding a company name, to the product or its packaging; includes online sellers selling into Texas without a storefront1 year, per location$5,150 per location ($5,000 + TOL)
DSHS Consumable Hemp Product (CHP) LicenceCHPs are manufactured — preparing, compounding, processing, packaging, repackaging, labeling, relabeling; or the retailer white-labels/private-labels (places its own name/address on another manufacturer’s product); or repackages, labels or relabels a container1 year, per location$10,300 per location ($10,000 + TOL)

History of the fee jump (do not confuse old and new figures): Before the 25 TAC Chapter 300 rules effective 31 March 2026, the fees were $155 retail / $258 manufacturer (secondary sources consistently, and DSHS history). The March 2026 rules raised them to $5,000 / $10,000 (+ TOL). A December 2025 DSHS proposal would have raised them further to $20,000 retail / $25,000 manufacturer — that proposal was NOT adopted at those figures; the adopted figures are $5,000 / $10,000 (+ TOL). A Travis County temporary injunction in April–May 2026 paused enforcement of the March 2026 rules (including fees and total-THC testing) until a July 2026 trial setting — the later status of that injunction after July 2026 was not confirmed from a court order in this session, while DSHS’s live licensing page still lists $5,150 / $10,300 — [Verify] current enforceability of the March 2026 fee/total-THC rules against the SS Distro v. DSHS docket before advising a client to pay or not pay.

Manufacturer requirements (DSHS, 25 TAC Ch. 300, as summarised in secondary guidance — rule text not fully opened). Facility licence per location; GPS perimeter coordinates / legal property description; property-owner authorisation; written consent to entry/inspection by DSHS, TABC and law enforcement; FBI fingerprint-based criminal-history check (via IdentoGo after application); a controlled-substance felony within the prior 10 years is disqualifying and can lead to revocation if discovered after issuance. Retail registrants are not required to undergo the FBI fingerprint check (DSHS page, opened). Distributors: DSHS guidance points distributors to the wholesaler licensing framework under HSC Chapter 431 (Food, Drug, Device, and Cosmetic Act) — [Verify] whether a separate Chapter 431 wholesaler licence is required in addition to a CHP licence for a pure distributor; this is a Part 7 follow-up. Age: DSHS emergency rules 2 Oct 2025, then permanent 25 TAC §§300.701–300.702, prohibit sale to persons under 21 and require government-issued ID verification. See also TABC below.

TABC — no separate “hemp permit,” but TABC rules bite TABC licensees. TABC adopted new 16 TAC §35.5 (Prohibited Sales of Consumable Hemp Products to Minors) and §35.6 (Mandatory Age Verification for Consumable Hemp Product Sales), effective 21 January 2026, implementing Executive Order GA-56 (10 Sept 2025). They prohibit a TABC licensee/permittee from selling, offering, serving or delivering CHPs to a person under 21, and require inspection of government-issued photo ID before sale. Permanent rules allow suspension for less serious violations and cancellation for more serious ones (emergency rules Sept 2025 had contemplated cancellation). Sources: TABC board materials; Texas State Law Library hemp guide. A TABC permit (e.g. bar/restaurant) does not by itself authorise CHP sales: each location selling CHPs must also hold the applicable DSHS registration/licence (secondary law-firm summary, consistent with DSHS rules). No TABC hemp-specific application fee was located; TABC alcohol permit fees are a separate regime and are not stated here.

TDA grower / handler licences (Agriculture Code Chapters 121–122; 4 TAC Chapter 24). TDA regulates agricultural hemp production, handling, sampling and non-consumable processing. It is separate from DSHS consumable-hemp licensing: a TDA licence alone does not authorise manufacture/sale of a consumable product, and a DSHS licence alone does not authorise cultivation. Fees/requirements as summarised from an Association of Food and Drug Officials (AFDO) TDA presentation and TDA guidance summaries — [Verify] against current 4 TAC Chapter 24 and the live TDA eApply schedule before budgeting; TDA’s own fee page was not opened in this session: Producer licence (must register at least one hemp facility) $100 licence + $100 per facility registered (minimum $200); Handler licence (may register storage locations) $100 licence + $100 per facility registered; Sampler licence $100; Laboratory licence $250; Research licence (universities only) $100; Lot Crop Permit $100 per lot (secondary); GPS facility-location modification $500 (secondary). Eligibility (secondary, consistent): applicant 18+; no controlled-substance felony conviction within the preceding 10 years; mandatory TDA orientation; facility geospatial/GPS information and proof of ownership/control; FSA registration; pre-harvest official sampling and testing; harvest within the window after sampling; lot reporting. All TDA fees non-refundable (secondary). Sources: AFDO TDA presentation.

Any new licence types from 2025/2026 law or rules? No new standalone Texas hemp licence type was created by 2025/2026 statute located in this session. The changes were: (1) HB 46 CUP expansion + satellites (not a hemp licence); (2) DSHS 25 TAC Ch. 300 overhaul eff. 31 Mar 2026 (same two DSHS credentials, new fees, total-THC testing, 21+ rules, expanded records); (3) TABC 16 TAC §§35.5–35.6 (rules for existing TABC licensees, not a new permit); (4) DSHS reinstatement of 2021 “tetrahydrocannabinols” / “marihuana extract” definitions eff. 31 July 2026 (scheduling action, not a licence). The December 2025 DSHS proposal for $25,000 / $20,000 fees and a TABC-entry consent requirement is proposed text, not adopted law at the adopted figures — do not cite it as the current fee. Cross-reference: Full TDA/DSHS/TABC licence inventory, with registries and download links, is Part 7 (Run 2). Part 4.2 above is the licence-type summary only.

4.3 Testing laboratory requirements

CUP — no independent testing-lab licence. Texas does not authorise or license independent cannabis testing facilities under TCUP. The dispensing organisation itself is responsible for required product testing, must describe its testing protocols and laboratory methods in its application, must use best practices to limit contamination (residual solvents, metals, mold, fungus, bacterial diseases, rot, pests, pesticides, mildew and other harmful contaminants), must maintain quality-history records showing laboratory testing results, and must have a written recall plan. 37 TAC §12.7 (as summarised secondarily). Secondary comparison table adds, by DPS practice, samples are routinely submitted to DPS for third-party testing because DPS does not test in-house — [Verify] DPS practice / current 37 TAC testing rule; do not state as a licensed-lab requirement. No DEA-registered-lab requirement for CUP product testing was located in this session; DEA-registered labs are relevant to hemp pre-harvest testing under the federal framework (Part 11), not as a CUP licence type.

Hemp — testing is by TDA-registered / DSHS-recognised labs, not by a single “hemp testing licence”. TDA: pre-harvest official samples must be collected by a licensed sampler and tested by a TDA-registered laboratory (TDA Laboratory Licence, $250 reported above). DSHS: consumable-product testing under 25 TAC Chapter 300 must be by an accredited/ISO-type third-party lab (exact accreditation standard — [Verify] 25 TAC §§300.301–300.303 text; secondary summaries say “full-panel, third-party, ISO-certified” testing with 3-year record-keeping, which was not confirmed from the rule text in this session). This is a summary for Part 4.3 only; full testing panels, sampling windows, failed-test and recall rules are Part 11 (Run 2).

4.4 Social equity / small-business provisions — NONE LOCATED

No social-equity programme, equity licence category, equity scoring preference, fee waiver/reduction, or residency preference was located in HSC Chapter 487, HSC Chapter 443, Agriculture Code Chapters 121–122, 37 TAC Chapter 12, or 25 TAC Chapter 300 in the sources opened/searched for this report. Affirmative evidence of absence of a fee preference: DPS FAQ states “No” to “Is a reduced application fee offered under special conditions?” and “No” to a Texas residency requirement for CUP applicants — i.e. the CUP system is competitive on qualifications, not on equity/residency criteria. Source: DPS application FAQ. Small-business effect, if any, is adverse in the current fee structure: the DSHS fee jump ($155 → $5,150 retail; $258 → $10,300 manufacturer) and the CUP licence fee ($488,520 / 2 years) are flat per-location/per-licence fees with no small-business tier located. That is an observation from the fee schedules, not a statutory “small-business provision.” [Verify] by full-text search of HSC 487, HSC 443 and Ag. Code 122 for “social equity,” “disproportionately impacted,” and “small business” before the final dossier states “Texas has no social equity provision” as an absolute negative.

4.5 Estimated startup costs — fee floors only; no official total published

No Texas agency publishes a total startup-cost estimate for a CUP dispensing organisation or a hemp business. The figures below are therefore fee subtotals computed from official fees, plus explicitly-labelled non-official items. They are not total startup costs.

Non-official estimates: Industry/consulting “startup cost” articles for Texas CUP and hemp businesses exist, but none was relied on for a total figure here because none is an official source and totals vary by orders of magnitude with facility scale. Mark any total startup figure taken from a consultant, law-firm or vendor article as NON-OFFICIAL ESTIMATE if it is used in the final dossier, and do not present it alongside official fees without that label.

4.6 Adult-use / broader medical licence types — PROPOSED ONLY, none enacted

No adult-use (recreational) licence type exists in Texas law at 31 Oct 2026. No broader “full medical” licence type separate from the low-THC CUP licence exists. The following are proposed only:

Do not list any of the above as a licence an applicant can apply for. The only licence an applicant can currently seek for cannabis in Texas is the CUP dispensing organisation licence (window currently closed except for the HB 46 tranche), and for hemp the DSHS/TDA credentials in §4.2.

5.1 DPS — Compassionate Use Program (CUP) and CURT

Holder type: Dispensing organisations licensed under HSC Chapter 487; CURT-registered physicians; patients are not listed — only aggregate counts are reported. URLs — use verbatim: DPS programme page, as cited in secondary sources: https://www.dps.texas.gov/section/compassionate-use-program; CURT physician search, as published by DPS and reproduced in DPS material: https://curt.dps.texas.gov/app/public/searchPhysician.xhtml; Texas.gov CUP explainer linking to the same DPS and CURT URLs: texas.gov CUP explainer data. Format / access: Interactive search only. No CSV/XLSX/PDF roster of dispensing organisations was found on DPS in this check. The CURT search is by ZIP code, city or specialty, and — per TexasCannabis.org’s summary of DPS practice — results show only physicians who have allowed their information to be published. Fields available (physician search): Physician name / practice as published by the physician, location/specialty search keys. Limitation: Absence from the public search does not prove a physician is unregistered; it may mean they opted out of publication. [Verify] the exact current field list on the live CURT page — this check could not run a live browser search inside CURT. Dispensing-organisation count — official-figure basis: 3 operating: Texas Original (Texas Original Compassionate Cultivation), goodblend, and Fluent (formerly Cansortium Texas). This is consistently reported across 2025–2026 secondary summaries of DPS records, and in DPS Phase I/II announcements that the 12 new licences are conditional only. 12 conditional, not yet authorised to sell: 9 selected Phase I, December 2025, plus 3 selected Phase II, April 2026, for a 15-licence total under HB 46 (89th Leg., 2025) — corrected by DPS HQ 2026-046, see Part 4. DPS’s own wording, reproduced in its Phase II announcement, is that conditional licences “do not grant the applicant permission to cultivate, manufacture, distribute or sell cannabis products until final approval by the department.” Sources: DPS Phase II announcement via EIN Presswire. Secondary licence-number claim — [Verify], do not publish as DPS-confirmed: one secondary page gives Fluent #0004, Texas Original #0005, goodblend (Surterra Texas LLC) #0006 (source: Miracle Leaf Texas dispensaries page) — this was not confirmed on a DPS page opened in this check. Update frequency / latest data date: Not stated on a DPS roster page found in this check — [Verify]. Patient/physician counts are reported episodically in DPS statements quoted by press/industry, not as a dated dashboard. See Part 6 for the count series. Limitation — critical: DPS does not publish CUP sales, revenue, or a patient list. Any “CUP sales” figure found elsewhere is an operator estimate, not a DPS total — see §6.1.

5.2 DSHS — Consumable Hemp Product Licensees and Retail Hemp Registrations

This is the only Texas cannabis/hemp source in this check with a current dated bulk download. Roster hub — opened as text 31 Oct 2026: DSHS current licensees and registrants. Page text, verbatim in substance: “Consumable Hemp Product Licensees / Retail Hemp Registrations / If you do not see the establishment you are looking for in the roster, please use the below license search tool.” Direct download links and file names — as linked from that hub page: consumable-hemp-product-licensees-10.19.26.xlsx and retail-hemp-registrations-10.19.26.xlsx. Date in file name: 19 October 2026. That is the latest roster date evidenced in this check. Text-extraction failure — flagged, not repeated: A text fetch of both XLSX files on 31 Oct 2026 returned no extractable content. That does not prove the files are empty or broken; XLSX is a spreadsheet format. Therefore: row counts, fields and county breakdowns inside the 19 Oct 2026 XLSX files are marked [Verify] until the files are opened in a spreadsheet application. Do not quote a row count from them in Run 1 without that check. Online search alternative — opened as text 31 Oct 2026: Hub page links to DSHS Online Licensing Services at vo.ras.dshs.state.tx.us — “Verify that an establishment has a current license or registration with DSHS. Search by name, license type, city or county. It is not necessary to log in to view a license or certification.” Licensing/registration explainer, opened 31 Oct 2026: DSHS licensing and registration — Retail Hemp Registration: one-year, $5,150 per location including Texas Online fee (current DSHS page text). Consumable Hemp Product License: one-year, $10,300 per location including Texas Online fee. Retail applicants are not required to undergo an FBI fingerprint criminal background check (DSHS page text). Fields available — [Verify] for the XLSX: The hub page does not list the XLSX column headings in readable text. The DSHS disciplinary PDF (§5.3) shows the fields DSHS does publish for enforcement cases — name/DBA, licence number, city, violation citation, disciplinary action, date — but that is not proof the rosters use the same columns. Do not assume roster fields. Update frequency as stated: Not stated on the hub page in readable text — [Verify]. The file-name date (19 Oct 2026) suggests periodic re-posting, not a stated daily/weekly cycle. Business-concentration limitation: Because the XLSX contents were not extracted, no county ranking, cluster map, or “most retailers in X county” claim can be made from Part 5 in this check. DSHS search is by name/type/city/county, one query at a time; it is not a bulk county table.

5.3 DSHS — Enforcement, disciplinary and embargo actions

Downloadable PDF — opened as text 31 Oct 2026: DSHS compliance actions — consumable hemp (PDF). Title in document: “COMPLIANCE ACTIONS — CONSUMABLE HEMP”. Fields in the table: Name / DBA, Licence Number, City of Residence, Violation (statute/rule citation), Disciplinary Action (e.g. Agreed Order, Settlement Agreement, Acceptance Order, dollar amount), Date of Action. Entries visible in the extracted text, latest first in the file as crawled: Modern Day Hemp Co., San Marcos, TX, Licence 423, Settlement Agreement $1,250, 06/15/26; Daniel Chavez dba Danny Boys Hemp Co, San Antonio, TX, Licence 914, Acceptance Order $16,250.00, 01/28/26; Mary Janes CBD Dispensary, Inc., San Antonio, TX, Licence 2641, Settlement Agreement $6,725, 12/02/25; GB Imports USA, Pasadena, TX, Licence 1247, Settlement Agreement $6,937, 11/18/25; Hemp Haus All In One LLC, Dallas, TX, Licence 1021, $1,750, 08/01/25; Delta Confections LLC dba Lantana, Lantana, TX, Licence 385, Settlement Agreement $5,000, 06/13/25; Butterfly Partners LLC dba Smoke Villa, Humble, TX, Licence 2396, Agreed Order $2,150, 06/11/25. FY25 trends PDF (secondary enforcement summary): DSHS FY25 trends — hemp (PDF). Search extract shows FY25: cases referred to Compliance — FY24 2, FY25 5; Notices of Violation FY25 5; Informal Conferences FY25 3; total penalty proposed/assessed FY25 $8,722; remitted/collected FY25 $7,00[0 — truncated in extract, [Verify] exact collected figure from the PDF itself]. Licence types coded 2201 = Retail Hemp Registration, 2202 = Consumable Hemp Product [licence, truncated]. Limitation: This is a trends summary, not a complete embargo list. No separate DSHS “embargo list” with product/lot-level embargoes was found in this check — mark as Unavailable / [Verify] if Run 1 needs lot-level embargoes. Do not conflate disciplinary fines with embargoes. Update frequency: Not stated in extracted text — [Verify].

5.4 TDA — Hemp producer, handler, sampler and processor licences

Programme hub: TDA hemp programme (also cited as texasagriculture.gov/Regulatory-Programs/Hemp). USDA grower-contact listing for Texas, opened via search: programme email published by USDA as Hemp@TexasAgriculture.gov; phone 979-542-3691 — source: USDA grower contact list. Access: Application via TDA eApply (online portal). The 2020 TDA announcement “Hemp Licensing Is Now Open” states producers “may now apply online for a producer license” and points to eApply and the TDA Hemp Page: TDA hemp licensing open (2020). Current public roster — NOT FOUND in this check: No current (2025–2026) TDA producer/handler/sampler roster, CSV, XLSX or searchable public list was returned by the searches on 31 Oct 2026. Mark as [Verify] / Unavailable for a current bulk list. The parent should not claim TDA publishes a current roster without a fresh live check. Historical lists only, from 2020, cited in Texas A&M AgriLife (July 2020): Handler/Sampler list (as of 7 July 2020): Handler_Sampler_List.pdf (2020); Lab registration list: HEMP_Lab_Registration.pdf (2020); source: AgriLife July 2020 update. Do not use 2020 lists as current data. Historical counts (for Part 6 time series only, not as current roster): July 2020, TDA via AgriLife: 861 producer licences, 351 crop permits, 84 handler/sampler licences (73 listed 7 July), 158 handler licences, 19 processor licences (27 by 3 July). March 2021, via Texas Farm Bureau / Commissioner statement: “over 1,150 producer licenses, permitted over 5,000 acres … and over 15 million square feet … in greenhouses.” Source: Texas Farm Bureau USDA final rule summary. Fields / limitations: Licence types in TDA rules are Producer, Handler, Handler/Sampler, and non-consumable Processor. A TDA licence does not authorise consumable-hemp manufacture or retail — that is DSHS. A DSHS registration does not authorise cultivation — that is TDA.

5.5 TABC — No hemp licence; alcohol-permit search only

Key point: TABC issues no hemp licence or permit. Holding a TABC licence does not authorise hemp sales; a business must first get DSHS authorisation. TABC hemp rules (16 TAC §§35.5, 35.6, 35.7) apply only to holders of a TABC licence/permit. Official guidance page — opened as text 31 Oct 2026, last updated 15 June 2026: TABC consumable hemp products. Page text: TABC initiated hemp efforts in September 2025 to implement Executive Order GA-56; TABC coordinates with DSHS by inspecting certain DSHS-licensed/registered businesses (some without a TABC licence), documenting CHP violations, issuing citations, and reporting them to DSHS; complaints via AIMS “File a Complaint” (licence number may be left blank if unknown). Penalties stated on the page: supplying to a minor or failing ID check — temporary suspension or cancellation; allowing on-premises consumption at off-premises BQ/BF/P/Q locations — base penalty $250. TABC licence-holder data sources (alcohol only): Public Inquiry / Public Roster (old and new layouts since 1 Aug 2022), with Master File ID, licence primary/secondary status, pending renewal flag, tier (Manufacturing / Distribution-Wholesaler / Retail / Other), and separate expiration month/day/year fields. Source: TABC public inquiry update (2022). AIMS (Alcohol Industry Management System) — holder account system; public AIMS License Search exists for finding an AIMS licence number by trade name. Source: TABC AIMS FAQs. Texas Open Data Portal — TABC License Information Dataset — same updated layout, bulk-access route for alcohol licences. No hemp flag field is described in the sources found — [Verify] whether the Open Data dataset can be filtered for hemp-citation history; it is not a hemp roster. TABC hemp enforcement list: No standalone published list of TABC hemp citations was found in this check. TABC states it reports citations to DSHS for processing — so the DSHS disciplinary PDF (§5.3) is the enforcement record to check first, not a TABC list. Mark TABC hemp-action list as Unavailable / [Verify].

5.6 Comptroller — Sales-tax permits and franchise-tax standing

These are general business tax tools, not cannabis/hemp registers. They are useful to verify that a named hemp/CUP business holds a sales-tax permit and is in good standing, but they cannot generate a list of “all hemp businesses.” Sources: Comptroller databases hub: Comptroller databases — “Franchise Tax Account Status — Use the Taxable Entity Search to determine the status of a taxable entity’s ‘right to transact business’. Search results reflect the Comptroller’s records at the time the query is made.” “Taxpayers may search by taxpayer number, employer identification number, legal name, business name, city and zip code to verify the status (active or inactive) of a sales and use tax permit. … Search results are limited to a maximum of 200 taxpayers.” Sales Taxpayer Search (permit search), as cited in Comptroller guidance: https://mycpa.cpa.state.tx.us/staxpayersearch/ — search by taxpayer ID, EIN, location name or ZIP; result limited to permit-holder name, address, and active/inactive status (per secondary summary of the tool; [Verify] field list on live tool). Tobacco / e-cigarette permits (relevant to smoke/vape shops that also sell hemp): Comptroller forms page Comptroller tobacco forms — AP-193 retailer tobacco permit, AP-175 non-retailer, AP-242 e-cigarette retailer permit. Secondary summaries give $180 / 2 years for tobacco retailer, $90 e-cigarette if tobacco permit already held at same location — [Verify] fees on the live Comptroller page before publishing in Part 2/4; this is a Part 5 access note only. Format / access: Interactive search only, no bulk download of “all permit holders” by industry. No export button described for the sales-tax search. Limitation: A business can hold a sales-tax permit and a tobacco permit without any DSHS hemp registration, and vice versa for online-only sellers. Never use Comptroller presence as proof of hemp authorisation.

5.7 Secretary of State — SOSDirect business-entity search

What it is: The legal-entity register (formation filings, registered agent, filing history, certified documents). It is not a licence register and contains no hemp/CUP status. Access, as consistently described in secondary guides: SOSDirect: https://direct.sos.state.tx.us/. Requires a funded SOS Client Account; $1.00 statutorily authorised fee per search, waived when the search leads to an order/filing. Certificate of Fact $15; certified copies $1/page + $15 certificate fee (secondary guides; [Verify] on SOS site). Search by entity name, SOS file number, 11-digit Comptroller taxpayer number, or 9-digit federal EIN; also by registered-agent name in SOSDirect. Sources: Commenda Texas entity search guide. Free alternative — Comptroller Taxable Entity Search (see §5.6): free, shows franchise-tax standing / right to transact business, SOS status and file number, agent, state of formation, and officers/directors as last reported on the Public Information Report. Ownership/members of an LLC are not public in either tool. Limitation: SOSDirect will confirm a CUP/hemp company exists as an entity; it will not confirm it holds a DPS or DSHS licence, and sole proprietors / general partnerships that never formed an SOS entity will be invisible in SOSDirect but may appear in Comptroller sales-tax search.

5.8 USDA — Texas state plan, HeMP, and public-search limits

Texas plan status — opened as text 31 Oct 2026: USDA AMS, List of USDA-approved Hemp Plans, updated 17 February 2026: USDA approved hemp plans. Table entry, verbatim: “Texas | TX Approved Plan (pdf)” — Texas runs its own USDA-approved state plan; it is NOT a USDA-direct licensing state. The plan was submitted 2 Dec 2019 and approved 27 Jan 2020 (secondary histories; the AMS page itself does not give those dates in extracted text — [Verify] dates from the plan PDF). Consequence: a Texas grower must be licensed by TDA, not by USDA. USDA direct licences apply only in states/tribes without an approved plan. HeMP / public search: HeMP (Hemp eManagement Platform): USDA HeMP — secure system for USDA producers, states, tribes, labs and sampling agents; requires eAuth/Login.gov. It is not a public Texas roster. Law Enforcement Search Tool (LEST): USDA LEST — law-enforcement only, via hempsearch.ams.usda.gov, by email request to FarmBill.Hemp@usda.gov. AMS warns: “at first, the tool may not capture all approved hemp growing locations. If your search does not return any results, please contact USDA before taking legal action.” “USDA Hemp Public Search Tool” count cited secondarily: 362 active licensed hemp producers in Texas as of April 2026, per hempcbdbusinessplans Texas industry page citing the USDA tool. This count is secondary and undated in the primary USDA source opened here — mark as [Verify] / secondary, do not present as a USDA-published Texas total without re-checking the tool. No public USDA Texas producer list with names was found in this check. USDA NASS acreage data: See Part 6.5 — NASS is a survey, not a licence register, and does not list licence holders.

5.9 Tribal records

USDA approved-plan list (opened, see §5.8) includes under Tribes: “Ysleta Del Sur Pueblo | Approved Plan (pdf)”. No Texas tribal hemp licence roster, licence count, or tribal ordinance list was found in this check. The other two federally recognised Texas tribes (Alabama-Coushatta Tribe of Texas, Kickapoo Traditional Tribe of Texas) do not appear as approved-plan holders on the USDA list extracted 31 Oct 2026. That is not proof they have no hemp activity — it proves only they have no USDA-approved plan listed there. Mark tribal licence data as Unavailable / [Verify]; Part 10 must use official tribal/government sources only. No tribal CUP dispensing-organisation list exists in DPS/DSHS sources found — CUP is a state programme, not a tribal programme.

5.10 Local (county/city) permit records where published

Finding: No statewide or consolidated local hemp-permit list exists in sources found. Local authority in Texas for hemp retail is mainly general business regulation (zoning, occupancy, food establishment where applicable), plus state tobacco/e-cigarette permits mirrored in city open data — not a hemp-specific local register. What was found: City of Austin Open Data: Austin open data — distributors — catalogue entries for Comptroller cigarette/tobacco permit-holder files (active, and suspended/revoked), with one entry noted “Last updated October 30, 2026” and another “Last updated June 29, 2026” in search extract. These are Comptroller tobacco files mirrored by Austin, not Austin hemp licences, and not a list of hemp retailers. [Verify] the exact dataset titles/fields on data.austintexas.gov before citing them as a local source. Austin service catalogue (PDF, 8 Nov 2024): Austin APH service catalogue (PDF) — lists Certificate of Occupancy, Smoking in Public Places Ordinance, Minors Access to Tobacco Ordinance, and Food Establishment Permitting inspections. No hemp-specific local permit is listed. Comptroller tobacco/e-cigarette permits (state, per location) — see §5.6 — are the closest systematic permit record for smoke/vape shops, but they are state permits, searchable only one business at a time, and do not identify hemp sales. Jurisdictions checked for a local hemp register in this Part 5/6 check: Houston/Harris, Dallas, San Antonio/Bexar, Austin/Travis, Fort Worth/Tarrant, El Paso, Arlington, Corpus Christi, Plano, Lubbock, Laredo, Irving, Amarillo, Brownsville, McAllen, Killeen, Denton, San Marcos, Harker Heights — no city-specific hemp retailer register was located for any of them in the searches run for Parts 5–6. This is a search-result negative, not a proof of non-existence for every city portal — Part 3 of the full report must do city-by-city code/portal checks. Do not state “no city has a permit” as a confirmed legal fact; state “no consolidated local list was found.”

Physicians: ~800 registered to prescribe, of ~80,000 board-certified physicians in Texas — DPS/operator statements reported Jan–Mar 2026: CultureMap; Lubbock Avalanche-Journal (says “around 900 doctors”). The only price datum found is anecdotal/operator: medical products “can range from $40 to $70” (Texas Tribune reporting via San Antonio Current, above). That is a product price range, not market size, and must not be multiplied by patient counts to invent sales. Texas Original footprint (operator-reported, for context, not sales): moved from a 7,700 sq ft facility to a 75,000 sq ft headquarters in Bastrop; 6 approved satellite locations and 13 pickup sites statewide as of Sept 2026 (Texas Original post above). Goodblend: first satellite in San Antonio for same-day pickup (CultureMap San Antonio, above). These are access indicators, not sales. Enrolment context (secondary, for Part 6 only): Texas Original states Texas enrols roughly 0.5% of its population vs a national average near 2.1%; another secondary summary gives 0.44% at end-2025. Both are secondary calculations — [Verify] denominator and national comparator before reuse.

They are not errors; they are snapshots. The Run 1 time series must keep the date on every figure. What DSHS counts do NOT show: How many registrations are active vs lapsed at a given date, how many are online-only vs storefront, or county distribution — the XLSX rosters (§5.2) would be needed for that, and their contents were not extracted here.

6.3 Hemp-derived THC market size — official first, secondary clearly marked

Official market-size total: NONE FOUND. No Texas state agency publishes a hemp-derived THC retail-sales total, a CUP sales total, or a hemp GDP total in the sources opened. Every dollar figure below is secondary / industry / think-tank, dated, and must be labelled as such in Run 1.

Secondary figures, in chronological order of the underlying estimate: (1) Baker Institute for Public Policy, Rice University, May 2024 report (as reported secondarily): Hemp-derived cannabinoid sales (excluding non-impairing CBD) grew 1,283% from 2020 to 2023, reaching $2.78 billion in 2023. “As of April 2024, Texas had over 7,000 registered hemp dispensaries. More than 50,000 Texans are estimated to be employed through the hemp sector.” Sources reporting the Baker findings: Nano Hemp Tech Labs 24 Apr 2025. Note: The Baker report itself was not opened as a primary PDF in this check — the $2.78bn / 1,283% / 50,000 figures are reported Baker findings. Mark as “Baker Institute via secondary reporting” until the report PDF is opened. (2) Whitney Economics, 2025 report (as reported secondarily): Texas hemp industry generates $5.5 billion in annual sales and ~$268 million in tax revenue annually, primarily sales tax, from over 8,500 registered retailers. Source reporting Whitney: Merissa Hansen summary of Whitney 2025. Corroborated as “estimated $5.5 billion hemp market” in MJBizDaily, Sept 2026: MJBizDaily Sept 2026. (3) “$8 billion+” total economic activity / “$10 billion” figures — DO NOT conflate with sales: Some secondary pages give “$8 billion market” / “$8+ billion in annual revenue” (2023 economic study, via secondary) and “more than $10 billion in total economic activity, with $5.5 billion in annual sales” (Beard Bros summary). Total economic activity (including indirect/induced effects) is not retail sales. Run 1 must keep the $5.5bn (sales) and $8–10bn (broader activity, methodology unstated in sources found) in separate sentences with separate labels, or omit the broader figure as [Verify] methodology. A stray secondary page giving “$55 billion in 2025” is an apparent decimal/order-of-magnitude error against every other source and is excluded as unreliable; do not cite it. (4) Tax revenue — secondary, consistent across sources: $268 million per year in tax revenue (sales tax) — repeated in Baker-era and Whitney-era reporting. It is an industry/think-tank estimate, not a Comptroller total. No Comptroller hemp-specific tax-receipt total was found. (5) Post-31 July 2026 disruption — claims, not totals: Industry experts quoted by Texas Tribune (via Beard Bros) estimated the 31 July 2026 reclassification (delta-8, delta-10, THCP, THCA flower to Schedule I, following DSHS v. Sky Marketing Corp. d/b/a Hometown Hero, No. 23-0887, Texas Supreme Court, May 2026) could remove 60–90% of THC products from circulation. That is an expert range, not a measured sales fall. A Western District of Texas lawsuit filed Aug 2026 (reported via CB Insights) claims 36,000 jobs lost, retail sales down >50%, and billions in lost revenue. Those are plaintiff claims in litigation, not measured market data. Label them as such if mentioned at all.

Bottom line for Run 1: The defensible market sentence is: “No official Texas hemp-sales total was found. The most-cited secondary estimates are Baker Institute $2.78bn in 2023 (ex-CBD cannabinoids) and Whitney Economics $5.5bn annual sales / $268m tax (2025), with DSHS registrations rising to 14,170 retail locations and 778 manufacturers by 18 June 2026.” Anything more precise would be invented.

6.4 Jobs — where officially reported, and where not

No Texas Workforce Commission, Comptroller, or other state-agency hemp-jobs total was found in this check. The 50,000 figure is a think-tank/industry estimate, not an official jobs count. 50,000 Texans employed through the hemp sector — Baker Institute, Apr 2024, as reported in multiple secondary sources (see §6.3). Repeated in 2025 SB 3 reporting (Bisnow, 22 May 2025): “more than 50,000 jobs”: Bisnow 22 May 2025. No CUP-specific jobs total (dispensing-organisation headcount) was found. Texas Original’s facility expansion (7,700 → 75,000 sq ft) is not a headcount. Litigation job-loss claims (36,000 jobs lost, Aug 2026 suit, via CB Insights) are plaintiff claims — see §6.3. Do not present them as a jobs total.

Sources: 2023–2024 official-basis figures: Texas Farm Bureau (“The 2024 report, released in April, shows total value of U.S. hemp production … at $445 million, a 40% increase from 2023. … In 2024, growers planted 4,900 acres and harvested 1,500 acres. That’s a sharp increase from the 250 acres planted and 110 acres harvested in 2023.”). 2020–2022 and 2025 secondary series: hempcbdbusinessplans Texas industry. U.S. context (NASS 2024, released 17 Apr 2025): 45,294 acres planted nationally, 32,694 harvested, $445m total value (open + protected), $417m open-field, floral $386m on 11,827 harvested acres (93% of open-field value), fiber 18,855 harvested acres / $11.2m. Texas under-protection (NASS Census of Horticulture table, 2024): 11 operations, 69,878 (1,000 sq ft) under protection, $786,000 sales value — source table: NASS Horticulture table (PDF) — note the table year/coverage differs from the annual Hemp Report; do not add it to open-field acres.

Licence counts by year (TDA, secondary/historical only): 2020 (July): 861 producers, 351 crop permits, 158 handlers, 84 handler/samplers, 19–27 processors (see §5.4). 2021 (Mar): >1,150 producer licences (see §5.4). Apr 2026: 362 active producers via USDA tool (secondary, [Verify] — see §5.8). No continuous official TDA licence-count series 2022–2025 was found. Do not interpolate. The fall from >1,150 (2021) to 362 (2026, secondary) may reflect active-vs-cumulative counting, licence expiries, or data-source differences — flag as an unresolved counting difference, not as a proven collapse, until TDA publishes a series.

6.6 Texans buying in New Mexico and Oklahoma — official first

Important distinction: No New Mexico or Oklahoma agency publishes a “sales to Texans” total. What exists is (a) official total sales for border towns, and (b) attribution to Texas customers in press/regulator reporting. The Texas share itself is estimated / attributed, never directly measured in sources found.

New Mexico — official totals, Texas attribution secondary: State totals (official, via NM Regulation & Licensing Department Cannabis Reporting Online Portal, CROP): Cumulative sales through June 2026: >$2.35 billion — >$1.7bn adult-use + ~$643m medical. Source: El Paso Times 20 Aug 2026. First half 2026: ~$284.9m combined; July 2026: $49.8m (monthly record at the time); 2025 calendar: $567m (nearly +25% vs 2024). Source: MJBizDaily 20 Aug 2026 (citing NM RLD data). Note on dates in secondary reporting: Some NM articles misstate legalisation dates (medical Apr 2022 / adult-use Apr 2024 in one summary vs the actual NM adult-use sales start Apr 2022). Do not copy those dates into Run 1; Part 10 must verify NM dates separately. The sales totals above are the usable figures. Border-town totals (official CROP data, via press): Sunland Park (borders El Paso, TX): Q2 2026 — Apr >$5.8m, May >$5.7m, June >$5.3m; cumulative >$204.5m since legalisation; 2nd statewide after Albuquerque in Q2 2026. “Attributed in large part to customers crossing from El Paso, Texas” (El Paso Times / MJBizDaily, above). Chaparral: Q2 2026 ~$3.2m (up >$200k vs Q1); 4-year total ~$43m. Santa Teresa: Q2 2026 >$508k (vs ~$445k Q1); cumulative cited as >$4.2m in one report. Las Cruces: $2.6–2.7m/month in Q2 2026 (3rd statewide). July 2026 border-town tracker (secondary compilation of NM CCD monthly retailer reports): $7.3m across border dispensaries in July 2026, Sunland Park 78% of that total; $128m tracked since Jan 2025. Source: Green Border tracker — secondary tracker, label as such, not as a NM agency publication. Sunland Park has 24 licensed dispensaries, most on McNutt Road / Appaloosa Drive, ~15 minutes from downtown El Paso; 43+ licensed shops within a short drive of El Paso across Sunland Park, Chaparral, Santa Teresa and Anthony (same tracker). No dispensaries are in El Paso itself — Texas has no adult-use stores. What is NOT established: The dollar amount or share of Sunland Park/Chaparral/Santa Teresa sales that went to Texas residents specifically. No agency asks buyers for home state at the till in published data. Any “Texans spend $X in New Mexico” headline that multiplies border-town totals by 100% would be invented. Report border-town totals + the attribution (“largely Texas customers” in press/regulator reporting) and mark the Texas share as Unavailable / estimated, not measured.

Oklahoma — even thinner: No Oklahoma official total for sales to Texans was found. What was found: Oklahoma offers a 30-day temporary medical licence for out-of-state patients (fee cited secondarily as $104.30) and Texas does not recognise Oklahoma cards, nor does Oklahoma’s card protect a holder once in Texas. A secondary page claiming “385,000 active patient licenses” in Oklahoma in 2026 and other border figures was found on a low-authority SEO site that also contains demonstrably false Texas claims (e.g. CUP “terminated,” felony reclassification) — excluded as unreliable; do not cite it for Oklahoma totals. Conclusion for Run 1: Oklahoma cross-border estimate = Unavailable. Do not invent a figure from dispensary counts or patient counts. The only defensible Oklahoma sentence is the reciprocity rule above, with Part 10 to verify OMMA temporary-licence rules from OMMA itself. General cross-border legal note (for Part 10 cross-reference): Transport across a state line remains a federal offence even between two legal states, and Texas does not recognise out-of-state medical cards — secondary guides state this consistently, but Part 10 must cite statute, not guides.

6.7 Cannabis/hemp-friendly banks and credit unions

No Texas state list exists. There is no Texas Department of Banking list of cannabis-serving banks, and no federal banking law for cannabis (per the federal baseline in the commission). What exists is (a) national fintech/bank marketing, and (b) a secondary Texas directory. Both must be labelled as commercial/secondary, and account availability must be verified with the institution — banks change cannabis policies without public notice. National provider with Texas coverage — official FAQ: Safe Harbor Financial (fintech, not a bank): “Safe Harbor provides banking services in 41 U.S. states and territories, including … Texas”; deposits held by federally insured partner banks/credit unions, FDIC/NCUA up to $250,000 per depositor per ownership category; serves dispensaries, cultivators, manufacturers, distributors, testing labs, ancillary businesses, hemp and CBD; requires valid state licences. Source: Safe Harbor FAQ. Limitation: Safe Harbor does not name its Texas partner bank(s) on the FAQ page opened via search. Do not name a partner bank for Texas without a primary source. Texas-focused hemp banking — commercial, single-institution: Herring Bank (Texas): Markets a “Hemp Banking Texas” programme — checking/savings, 24/7 online/mobile banking, ACH/wire, FDIC-insured accounts, tailored to legal hemp/CBD businesses in Texas; its checklist requires TDA licence and/or DSHS licence/registration per location, 21+ ID checks, and TABC compliance where applicable. Source (commercial, dated 18 Oct 2026 on page): Herring Bank hemp banking Texas. Label as commercial marketing, not an official endorsement or a guarantee of account approval. Secondary Texas directory — use with caution: TexasCannabis.org banking page lists, without primary verification in the extract: Amalgamated Bank, Bison State Bank, Chase (CBD only), Farmers Bank & Trust, Green Check (fintech), KeyPoint Credit Union, Lighthouse Biz Solutions (CUSO), Needham Bank, North Bay Credit Union, Safe Harbor (fintech), TFNB (bank). Source: TexasCannabis.org banking. Do not republish this list as “banks that serve Texas cannabis businesses” without checking each institution’s current policy. Several listed institutions are out-of-state; “CBD only” vs THC-plant-touching is a material distinction the directory compresses. For Run 1, the safe form is: “No official Texas list exists; secondary directories name [list] as claiming to serve Texas hemp/cannabis, subject to individual verification, and national fintech Safe Harbor states it covers Texas.” Regulator: Texas Department of Banking regulates state-chartered banks, but no cannabis-banking guidance list from the Department was found in this check — [Verify] if Part 2/11 needs it.

PART 7: HEMP LICENCES — HOW TO GET ONE, WHAT EACH ONE COVERS, AND WHAT DOES NOT EXIST

Research date / accessed: 31 October 2026 (Parts 7–8 pages crawled 1 November 2026, 00:23 UTC, i.e. 31 October 2026, 19:23 EDT). Parts 7–12 were researched to match Parts 0–6 exactly. Where a Run 2 track found a conflict with Run 1, both figures are kept and flagged — nothing in Parts 0–6 was silently overwritten.

Headline: Texas has two separate hemp systems, plus a narrow TABC overlay. Holding one authorisation does not authorise the other. A fully vertical hemp business needs, in combination: a TDA producer licence plus lot permits for cultivation, a DSHS consumable hemp product (CHP) licence for manufacture, a Health & Safety Code Chapter 431 wholesaler authorisation if it distributes other people's products, a DSHS retail registration for each retail location, and a TABC permit only if it also sells alcohol. No new standalone hemp licence type was created in 2025 or 2026.

7.1 The two systems at a glance

SystemRegulatorWhat it coversWhat it does NOT cover
Agricultural hempTexas Department of Agriculture (TDA)Cultivation, handling, sampling, non-consumable processingConsumable hemp manufacture or retail; CUP dispensing
Consumable hemp productsTexas Department of State Health Services (DSHS)Manufacture, distribution and retail sale of consumable hemp productsCultivation; CUP dispensing
Alcohol-licensee overlayTexas Alcoholic Beverage Commission (TABC)Rules for TABC licensees/permittees that also handle CHPs (see §7.6)Any standalone hemp licence — none exists

State plan confirmed: Texas runs its own USDA-approved state plan via TDA, not USDA-direct licensing. TDA submitted the plan 2 December 2019; USDA approved it 27 January 2020; TDA hemp rules were published in January 2020 and took effect 11 March 2020; online licensing opened 16 March 2020. Source: TDA hemp background page, texasagriculture.gov/Regulatory-Programs/Hemp-Background. No TDA plan amendment addressing the federal §781 hemp dates was found — [Verify].

7.2 TDA agricultural hemp credentials and fees

CredentialFee reportedTerm / notes
Producer Licence$100 licence + $100 per facility (minimum $200 for one facility)1 year, annual renewal, no automatic renewal
Handler Licence$100 + $100 per facility if any[Verify] in eApply
Handler / Sampler (official sampler)$100A producer cannot sample its own crop
Lot Crop Permit$100 per lotOne crop/variety per planting area; required before planting; lot report due within 30 days after final sample or 180 days from permit issue, whichever is first
Non-consumable Processor Registration$100 participation fee4 TAC §24.5(c) [Verify]; 4 TAC §24.19: “(a) All persons who intend to process nonconsumable hemp products shall register with the Department. (b) Only a processor registered with the Department shall process nonconsumable hemp products in the State of Texas.” Adopted TexReg Vol. 45 No. 10, 6 Mar 2020, eff. 11 Mar 2020
Independent Testing Laboratory Registration$250 reportedStatute wording is registration (Agriculture Code §122.152), not “licence” — [Verify]
Research Licence$100 reported, universities only[Verify] existence as a distinct adopted credential — the only authority-like text found was HB 3948 (2021), which is dead, not law. See also §11.9 for the AgriLife research-licence procedure citing 4 TAC Ch. 24, Subchapter J, Rule 24.49.
Fee conflict — do not silently change the Run 1 figures. Agriculture Code §122.052(a) caps fees at: initial application ≤$100, renewal ≤$100, participation ≤$100 per location, site modification ≤$500, department collection/testing ≤$300; fees are non-refundable and appropriated only to TDA. But 4 TAC §24.5 summaries say “at least $100” / modification “minimum of $500” (the opposite direction on its face), and a January 2026 Texas Cannabis Policy Conference slide says “Producer License (TDA): $100 (up to 5 facilities)” alongside “Facility Registration $100/facility”. Budget at the $100 / $100 / $500 lines as in Run 1 and [Verify] the amount actually charged in eApply (licensing.texasagriculture.gov). No TDA fee change in 2025/2026 was found. HB 28 (March 2025 summary mentioning a $5,000 processing fee) is a bill — status [Verify] on TLO; do not cite its fees as law.

7.3 TDA process, eligibility and sampling

Statute (from mirrors — [Verify] on statutes.capitol.texas.gov): §122.101(a) — no cultivation, handling, or out-of-state transport without a licence; §122.101(b) — no TDA licence is required to manufacture a CHP under Health & Safety Code Title 6, Subtitle A. §122.103(a) — an application must include a legal description plus GPS perimeter coordinates for each location, written consent for TDA / DPS / law-enforcement entry and inspection, the application fee, and other rule-required information. §122.103(b) — TDA shall issue to a qualified applicant by the 60th day after a completed application and fees. §122.103(c) — a CUP (Health & Safety Code Ch. 487) licence holder pays no TDA application fee; whether that waives facility/participation fees is [Verify]. §122.102 — ineligibility: a controlled-substance felony within 10 years (holder or governing person), and material falsification bars issuance; TDA must revoke on a controlled-substance felony conviction.

Process in order: mandatory orientation video first; apply in eApply; at least one facility is required for a producer; register the facility with the local USDA Farm Service Agency and report planted acreage within 30 days after planting; obtain a Lot Crop Permit before planting; have an official sample taken by a TDA-licensed Handler Sampler and sent to a TDA-registered lab; obtain a transport manifest after acceptable results; if the lot is over threshold, destroy the crop and file a Disposal Report (see §11.5). Sampling-window conflict — [Verify] in 4 TAC §24.21: the programme page says harvest within 30 days from sampling (maximum 2 official samples per lot, unofficial samples unlimited), while the Sampling & Collection Procedure citing 7 CFR §990.3(a)(2)(i) says samples within 15 days before anticipated harvest. Collector-eligibility wording also differs (Handler Sampler vs TDA Inspector / authorised representative / law enforcement) — [Verify] in 4 TAC Ch. 24, Subchapter D. No licence cap and no minimum acreage (TDA FAQ, 1 April 2020 — [Verify] unchanged). Age 18, mandatory orientation, and a possible 5-year exclusion after specified revocations are [Verify] by section; a sampler-contract requirement under 4 TAC §24.9(e) is [Verify] whether it still applies. Seed: from May 2020, selling, possessing or purchasing hemp seed requires a valid active TDA licence; an out-of-state seller needs no Texas licence if the seed is certified/approved under 4 TAC §24.48; a commercial greenhouse distributing to other producers may also need a TDA Nursery Floral licence ([Verify] — not a hemp licence).

7.4 TDA laboratories

Agriculture Code §122.151 — testing by (1) TDA, (2) an institution of higher education, or (3) an independent lab registered under §122.152. The lab must be ISO/IEC 17025-accredited (or a comparable/successor standard); the holder may not select a lab in which it has an ownership interest (or ≥10% if publicly traded); the holder pays collection/testing costs; results are sent directly to TDA and to the holder; TDA notifies the holder by the 14th day after sample collection (§122.154) or receipt of results. The TDA sampling procedure mentions “ISO accredited and DEA-registered” labs — whether Texas currently requires DEA registration or only ISO/IEC 17025 is [Verify] in 4 TAC §24.24 (the statute on its face requires ISO only; federal DEA-lab enforcement is delayed to 31 December 2026 under the given baseline).

Processor scope (2025–2026): a CRAFT analysis (joincraft.org — Texas hemp processor: TDA / DSHS handoff), based on a written TDA response and not on statute, reports TDA saying a registered processor may perform any duties necessary to convert the agricultural commodity to marketable form, including contract processing with chain-of-custody documentation, and that the form's “will not handle hemp it does not own” certification is not an ownership requirement. Treat contract-processing scope as [Verify] against the TDA response itself. TDA regulates only non-consumable processing; finished consumables are DSHS.

7.5 DSHS consumable hemp licences and registrations

The DSHS licensing page was opened in this track and matches Run 1 exactly — no headline fee conflict: Retail Hemp Registration $5,150 per location per year including the Texas Online fee ($5,000 + TOL); Consumable Hemp Product Licence $10,300 per location per year including TOL ($10,000 + TOL); both 1-year, per location. Manufacturing includes preparing, compounding, processing, packaging, repackaging, labelling, relabelling, and private/white-labelling; it excludes ordinary growing, harvesting, packing or holding of raw agricultural hemp. A CHP licence covers manufacturing and retail at the licensed location; a separate retail location needs retail registration.

New fee detail (not in Run 1, not a correction): adopted 25 TAC §300.202(c) / §300.502(g) — initial/renewal manufacturer $10,000 per facility base; change-of-ownership amendment $10,000 base; minor amendment (location/name/address) $125 base; late renewal +$1,000 delinquency; fees are not prorated. Whether the Texas Online fee also applies to the $125 / $1,000 amounts, and the all-in ownership-change total, are [Verify] at payment.

Application, per location: legal description including GPS perimeter coordinates; written consent for DSHS / DPS / TABC / law-enforcement entry (the TABC-entry consent wording was in the December 2025 proposal — [Verify] in adopted §300.201 text, separately from the proposal's rejected fees); fingerprint-based background check per applicant at the applicant's expense (IdentoGo after application, using a DSHS-emailed Texas Fingerprint Service Code; do not fingerprint before applying). Health & Safety Code §443.102: a controlled-substance felony within 10 years bars holding a licence or being a governing person; DSHS must not issue, and shall revoke if discovered after issuance ([Verify] §443.105 wording). §443.104 renewal: a licence is valid 1 year and is renewed if the holder (1) is not ineligible under §443.102, (2) submits the renewal fee, and (3) owes no outstanding DSHS fees. An expired licence/registration is not valid; no regulated activity may occur on an expired authorisation. Retail registrants have no FBI fingerprint check (DSHS page); whether any background disclosure applies to retail registrants is [Verify] in §§300.501–300.502 / the Retail Registration Guide (not opened). No fixed DSHS processing deadline was found (contrast TDA's 60 days) — do not assert one; secondary guidance says weeks to months. Records: test results retained at least 3 years; recall plans and complaint files are licence conditions (Part 11 detail). “Approved hemp source” definition: [Verify] whether adopted March 2026 §300.101 kept the December 2025 proposal's “licensed with the department under Health & Safety Code Chapters 431 and 443” wording or adopted the commenters' plan-based wording — do not advise out-of-state suppliers from proposal text. The smokable-manufacture ban (§443.204(4)), the SB 2024 vape ban, and the 31 July 2026 delta-8 schedule are licence limits that no licence overrides.

7.6 There is no single “Texas hemp distributor licence”

25 TAC §300.101: “Distributor — A person who distributes consumable hemp products for resale, either through a retail outlet owned by that person or through sales to another retailer. A distributor is required to hold a wholesaler license per Texas Health and Safety Code Chapter 431.” The DSHS adoption notice (Texas Register, March 2026) states: “A consumable hemp product distributor is required to hold a wholesaler license from the department per HSC Chapter 431. Out of state manufacturers' products must comply with Texas statutes and rules in order to be sold in Texas.” A CHP manufacturer may distribute only products it manufactured; reselling other people's products wholesale needs separate Chapter 431 authorisation. Health & Safety Code §431.222(a-3) (via secondary summary — [Verify] wording) says a Chapter 443 licence holder acting within its scope needs no additional Chapter 431, Subchapter J food manufacturer/wholesaler licence; that does not remove drug-manufacturing or out-of-scope licences.

Commodity types: food wholesaler, food warehouse, nonprescription-drug distributor, device distributor, and OTC Multiple Products Distributor (two or more product types). Food framework, 25 TAC §229.182: a food manufacturer licence is per place of business; a manufacturer distributing its own plus others' food needs only a manufacturer licence (fee on total food manufactured plus wholesaled); a non-manufacturing distributor needs a food wholesaler licence per place; a combination-products wholesaler licence covers food plus drugs/devices; fees are gross-annual-sales tiered for a 2-year licence (only the ≥$10 million tier was extracted: $1,350 / 2 years — do not quote a single food-wholesaler fee; [Verify] the tier in §229.182(b)). OTC form EF23-13010 (dated 30 November 2021 — [Verify] the current schedule in 25 TAC §229.249): LV1 $0–$199,999.99 = $520 per facility; LV2 $200,000–$499,999.99 = $780; LV3 $500,000–$999,999.99 = $1,040; LV4 $1,000,000–$9,999,999.99 = $1,300; LV5 ≥$10,000,000 = $1,950; late renewal +$100; the application is distribution only and does not cover manufacturing. Chapter 300 has no distributor-only fee. The out-of-state distributor licensing question is the “approved hemp source” [Verify] above.

7.7 TABC — no hemp permit, and only for TABC holders

No TABC hemp permit, licence or registration exists. TABC page (updated 15 June 2026): “TABC has three rules related to CHPs. These rules only apply to holders of a TABC license or permit. However, holding a TABC license or permit does not authorize a business to engage in the CHP industry. Any business that wants to engage in the CHP industry must first get proper authorization from DSHS.” Non-intoxicating hemp-seed / hemp-seed-derived FDA-GRAS products (for example many lotions and shampoos) are not subject to the TABC CHP rules. §35.5 — no sale to a person under 21 (penalty: temporary suspension or cancellation under Alcoholic Beverage Code §§11.61(b)(7), 61.71(a)(16)); §35.6 — ID inspection before completing sale, service or delivery; §35.7 — no on-premises consumption at off-premises permits BQ, BF, P and Q only, base penalty $250. Other codes in secondary guides (BG, MB, BE, N, NB, FB, LH) are alcohol permits only — [Verify] codes and fees on the TABC permit list; only BQ / BF / P / Q are confirmed from TABC's own CHP page. TABC also inspects and reports certain DSHS businesses to DSHS (Run 1, Part 5.5).

7.8 Registries — unchanged from Run 1, Part 5

TDA eApply is an application portal, not a public roster (no 2025–2026 TDA roster, CSV, XLSX or public search was found; historical 2020 PDFs only). DSHS hub (dshs.texas.gov — current licensees and registrants) plus the Online Licensing Services search (vo.ras.dshs.state.tx.us, search by name, type, city or county, no login needed to view) plus XLSX rosters dated 19 October 2026 (consumable-hemp-product-licensees-10.19.26.xlsx and retail-hemp-registrations-10.19.26.xlsx) — contents still not extracted — [Verify]. DSHS enforcement PDF (entries through 15 June 2026). Chapter 431 wholesaler licences are searchable in the same DSHS portal but are not hemp-coded, so “all hemp distributors” cannot be generated from it. TABC Public Inquiry / AIMS / Open Data cover alcohol only, with no hemp flag described. The USDA plan PDF only, and HeMP / LEST systems are restricted.

7.9 Not hemp licences

A CUP licence (its only hemp-adjacent effect is the TDA application-fee waiver in §122.103(c)); a Comptroller sales-tax, tobacco or e-cigarette permit; a TABC alcohol permit; a Secretary of State entity filing; a single “hemp distributor licence”; a federal USDA hemp licence for Texas growers; and any licence under SB 3, SB 5, SB 6, SB 53, SB 30 or HB 28.

PART 8: CANNABIS-ZONED PROPERTIES IN TEXAS — NONE PROVEN

Headline finding: ZERO (0) Texas properties proven to be “cannabis-zoned” were found. This is not a failure of searching. Texas has no statewide cannabis zoning category. Hemp cultivation is TDA facility/lot licensing on (mostly unincorporated, generally unzoned) county land; hemp retail and manufacture are DSHS per-location licensing; CUP facilities are DPS-licensed and §487.201 bars local prohibition, so there is no CUP zoning district; cities zone by general use (industrial, commercial, agricultural), not “cannabis”. Thousands of Texas properties are lawfully used for hemp under TDA/DSHS licensing (14,170 DSHS retail locations at 18 June 2026), but their authorisation is the licence, not a zoning district, and no “cannabis-zoned” address list is published.

Sources searched: 420Property.com (Texas pages and the Spicewood detail), LoopNet (Texas industrial / hemp), Crexi, Land & Farm / Land.com, Realtor-commercial via web search (no live Realtor.com login), Bisnow Texas hemp real-estate reporting (2025, secondary, context only — its “five Texas properties” claim is a 2025 secondary claim, not a current count; do not use it as a Part 8 total), plus Run 1, Part 3.4 (no official statewide local-ordinance list; no city cannabis zoning district located — [Verify] any specific city code parcel by parcel).

8.1 What would prove a listing

(1) A city zoning map or code naming a cannabis/hemp-specific use or district for the parcel (none found in Texas); or (2) a listing stating a cannabis-specific zoning classification and citing the city/code (Spicewood states the opposite — “unrestricted”); or (3) a DPS satellite approval or DSHS licence/registration for that address plus a for-sale/lease listing for that address (none paired was found); or (4) a county appraisal or deed record with cannabis-specific approval (no parcel-by-parcel title/appraisal search was done — a Run 2 follow-up if Tyler wants it).

9.2 Timeline additions and conflicts (all other dates match Run 1)

  • 2017: first three TCUP licences issued.
  • 16 March 2020: TDA online hemp licensing opens (secondary Cannabis Industry Journal corroboration; the TDA portal is the official route).
  • Smokable hemp: August 2020 DSHS rule banned manufacture and distribution/retail sale; Crown suit filed August 2020; September 2020 Travis County temporary restraining order; 3 August 2021, Third Court: manufacture ban enforceable, out-of-state-made smokable products could not be barred from Texas sale; 23 August 2021, 261st District Court (Judge Lora Livingston): §443.204(4) unconstitutional, §300.104 invalid in full, permanent injunction — both superseded in part by 24 June 2022, Crown (manufacture ban upheld, retail injunction remained).
  • October 2021: DSHS scheduled delta-8 definitions; November 2021 district injunction blocked them pending a final ruling (resolved 1 May 2026).
  • 2023 cite-and-release / penalty changes: NONE ENACTED. HB 218 (Moody, 88th Legislature) would have made possession of ≤1 oz a Class C offence (maximum $500, no jail), cite-instead-of-arrest for ≤1 oz, and expungement for ≤2 oz for $30, plus Penalty Group 2-B changes — passed House committee 9–0 and the House 87–59 (26–27 April 2023), died in the Senate — DEAD, not law; §481.121 tiers remain current. Similar 2019 and 2021 House-passed decriminalisation bills also died in the Senate. 2024 Senate State Affairs interim charge “Banning Delta 8 and 9” ([Verify] against the official charge letter).
  • DPS application window 15 August – 15 September 2025, closing 5:00 p.m. CT (DPS news HQ 2025-079).
  • GA-56 announcement summary (official): DSHS and TABC must ban sales to minors and require government-ID verification, with loss of retailer licence for failure; DSHS must review rules (total delta-9 including THCA, labelling amount/concentration/serving size/health warnings, increasing licensing fees, record-keeping); DSHS, TABC and DPS coordinate enforcement with local law enforcement; TABC, DSHS, Texas A&M AgriLife Extension Service and other agencies will study a comprehensive regulatory model similar to HB 309; the order follows the Legislature not passing an under-21 ban. A secondary summary adding location/marketing restrictions (near schools, churches and playgrounds; candy-style packaging) is not in the official announcement summary — [Verify] against the full order text. TABC permanent §§35.5–35.7 adopted February 2026 (effective date [Verify]).
  • THBC / Hemp Industry & Farmers of America suit vs DSHS, HHSC and the Attorney General filed 7 April 2026 (Travis County) — pending, docket [Verify].
  • 1 July 2026: Army CID raids (see Part 10). 7 July 2026: Senate Health & Human Services interim hearing. 10 July 2026: DSHS notice reinstating the 2021 definitions (secondary). Schedule effective 31 July 2026 — does not affect CUP (Texas Cannabis Policy Center).

9.4 Ballot history — Texas has no statewide citizen initiative

Ballotpedia (Ballotpedia — List of Texas ballot measures): only the Legislature can place a constitutional amendment on the statewide ballot (joint resolution, two-thirds of each chamber); Texas is one of 24 states without statewide citizen-initiated measures; voters have decided 729 measures, all constitutional amendments, 548 (75.2%) approved. Voters rejected an initiative/referendum amendment in 1914. Local initiative exists only in home-rule cities (Texas Constitution art. XI, §5) whose charter provides for it; charter amendments may be petitioned at 5% of qualified voters or 20,000 signatures, whichever is smaller (Local Government Code Ch. 9 — secondary summary, [Verify]). Thresholds: at least 100/150 in the House and 21/31 in the Senate; a simple majority of votes cast ratifies; joint resolutions are not presented to the Governor and are not vetoable (Texapedia secondary — [Verify] against the official Constitution text if quoted verbatim).

The local decriminalisation ballot table in the final file must match Run 1, Part 3.2 exactly (Austin, San Marcos, Killeen, Denton, Elgin, Harker Heights, San Antonio failed, Lubbock failed, Dallas, Bastrop, Lockhart, Houston not yet held at the 31 October 2026 cut-off). Not cannabis ballot history (do not cite as such): Austin Proposition I (November 2026, subject not established); the 2023/2025 “Empowering Texans Ballot Initiative Act” (neither reached the floor, secondary); the “Trust Texans Initiative” packet (an advocacy proposal for the 90th Legislature, not enacted, not a confirmed filed bill). A post-cut-off secondary report (Community Impact, 19 December 2025, cited in a 29 October 2026 search result) claiming the Supreme Court declined Austin's petition was not adopted — re-check the Supreme Court docket if Run 2 updates Austin; do not fold post-cut-off outcomes into a 31 October 2026 dossier without changing the cut-off.

PART 10: TRIBAL, INTERSTATE AND INTERNATIONAL RULES

10.1 Tribal — exactly three federally recognised Texas tribes, and no tribal cannabis operation found for any of them

Exactly three federally recognised Texas tribes: the Alabama-Coushatta Tribe of Texas, the Kickapoo Traditional Tribe of Texas, and the Ysleta del Sur Pueblo. No tribal cannabis dispensary, marijuana cultivation operation, tribal cannabis ordinance, or tribal-state cannabis compact was found for ANY of the three tribes in official tribal or government sources. State that as a search result, not as proof of non-existence.

Restoration Act: the Ysleta del Sur Pueblo and Alabama and Coushatta Indian Tribes of Texas Restoration Act, Pub. L. 100-89, 101 Stat. 666 (18 August 1987), 25 U.S.C. §§1300g et seq. (Ysleta Title I; Alabama-Coushatta Title II, §§731–737). 25 U.S.C. §1300g-6(a): “All gaming activities which are prohibited by the laws of the State of Texas are hereby prohibited on the reservation and on lands of the tribe” with the same civil/criminal penalties as Texas law. §1300g-6(b): “Nothing in this section shall be construed as a grant of civil or criminal regulatory jurisdiction to the State of Texas.” §1300g-6(c): U.S. courts have exclusive jurisdiction over a §1300g-6(a) offence by the tribe or a tribal member on the reservation/tribal lands; Texas may bring a federal-court action to enjoin violations. Fifth Circuit, Texas v. Ysleta del Sur Pueblo, No. 19-50400 (2 April 2020): the Restoration Act, not the Indian Gaming Regulatory Act, governs Pueblo gaming; it bars gaming violating Texas law; the Texas Attorney General had authority to sue. Jurisdiction conflict — present both, do not resolve: a Congress.gov summary for H.R. 318 says the Act “Grants Texas civil and criminal jurisdiction within such reservation”, against the §1300g-6(b) text — mark the general-jurisdiction question [Verify] against the full Act and the Fifth Circuit opinion. No Restoration Act provision addresses cannabis or hemp specifically — do not use its gaming provisions as a direct cannabis rule. The Restoration Act does NOT cover the Kickapoo. The Schedule III “state-licensed” characterisation for tribal operations remains [Verify] (carried from Run 1) — do not assert that a tribal hemp plan makes tribal marijuana “state-licensed”, and do not assert that Ysleta's hemp plan authorises marijuana.

10.2 Ysleta del Sur Pueblo

Official site: ysletadelsurpueblo.org (do not reproduce tribal office phone numbers or street addresses from directory listings). USDA approved-plans list, updated 17 February 2026, directly opened: States — “Texas | TX Approved Plan”; Tribes — “Ysleta Del Sur Pueblo | Approved Plan”. Alabama-Coushatta and Kickapoo do not appear. Ysleta Hemp Plan PDF directly opened (ams.usda.gov — Ysleta Del Sur Pueblo Hemp Plan), headed “Ysleta Del Sur Pueblo Hemp Plan (DRAFT)” — [Verify] whether the posted PDF is the final approved text.

Plan content: to be codified in the Pueblo Law and Order Code as The Ysleta del Sur Pueblo Hemp Production Plan; findings on dependent-sovereign status, the 2018 Farm Bill (7 U.S.C. §1621 et seq., Subtitle G), financial benefit, and resources/personnel. Definitions (§3): “Acceptable Hemp THC Level” = a certified-lab sample with measurement of uncertainty applied to the reported delta-9 THC (dry weight) producing a distribution/range of 0.3% or less; “Hemp” = Cannabis sativa L. and any part including seeds, derivatives and extracts with delta-9 THC ≤0.3% dry weight; “Marijuana” = all cannabis with tested THC >0.3% dry weight; “License” = a Pueblo-issued licence to grow, handle, store, process, transport or market hemp per USDA format; “Law Enforcement Agency” = the Ysleta del Sur Pueblo Tribal Police Department, the DEA, or another federal law enforcement agency, expressly not any state law enforcement agency; “Indian Country” per 18 U.S.C. §1151. Duties (§4): the Tribal Council enforces; wilful non-compliance may mean immediate revocation with no reapplying for 1–3 years; desk audits and site-visit audits, annual inspections of a random sample; licensee access to fields, greenhouses and storage plus records; the Pueblo collects, maintains and provides to USDA each applicant/licensee's contact information, licence status changes, an annual crop report to the Farm Service Agency per licensee and an annual report to the Farm Service Agency by 15 December each year (7 CFR §990.70(c) information); information is retained for the duration of the Pueblo's hemp involvement plus 10 years after cessation; sharing includes contact information, legal land description, licence status, and acreage planted, harvested and destroyed; producers report crop acreage to the Farm Service Agency per 7 CFR §990.7. Do NOT claim any Ysleta acreage, producer count, operating farm, dispensary or CHP retail operation — none was found. A USDA-approved hemp production plan is not a Texas DSHS CHP licence and does not by itself authorise CHP sale in Texas under Health & Safety Code Ch. 443; interaction with Ch. 443 / TABC is [Verify]; no tribal–State hemp agreement was found.

10.3 Alabama-Coushatta Tribe of Texas

Official site: alabama-coushatta.com (history: alabama-coushatta.com — history). The tribe's own page: the oldest reservation in Texas, about 11,000 acres in the Big Thicket, more than 1,400 members, about half on the reservation, an elected Tribal Council and Principal Chief, and a full sovereign government including law enforcement. Conflict — [Verify], do not merge: a secondary source gives about 4,593.7 acres, 17 miles east of Livingston, Polk County, more than 1,200 citizens, 589 on-reservation (2022), and 1854 establishment (1,110.7 acres originally). The federal relationship was terminated 23 August 1954 (Pub. L. ch. 831, 68 Stat. 768) and restored 18 August 1987 (Restoration Act, Title II). Attorney General opinion JM-17 (22 March 1983) on State power over tribal property — [Verify] against the opinion text if cited. No cannabis/hemp operation, ordinance, plan, licence or agreement was found; the tribe is not on the USDA list. Gaming litigation (Alabama-Coushatta Tribe v. State of Texas, No. 19-403, Supreme Court certiorari materials) is not a cannabis holding.

10.4 Kickapoo Traditional Tribe of Texas

Official site: kickapootexas.org — the tribe's description: 1,148 enrolled members, officially recognised by the Texas Indian Commission in 1977, a reservation on the Rio Grande in western Maverick County, south of Eagle Pass, in the Rosita Valley community (do not reproduce office phone/address details from directories). Not a Restoration Act tribe. Separate statute: 25 U.S.C. §1300b-13 (members are entitled to freely pass and repass the U.S. border and live and work in the U.S. notwithstanding the Immigration and Nationality Act). Not on the USDA list; no cannabis/hemp operation, ordinance, plan, licence or agreement was found. Gaming proposals (SJR 41 in an earlier session; SJR 58, 89th Legislature, referred to Senate State Affairs 6 March 2025 per LegiScan) are gaming only, not cannabis; SJR 58's status beyond referral was not confirmed.
