10.5 Interstate hemp transport

2018 Farm Bill, Pub. L. 115-334, §§10113–10114 (7 U.S.C. §§1639o–1639s). §10114(a): nothing prohibits interstate commerce of hemp or hemp products. §10114(b): “No State or Indian Tribe shall prohibit the transportation or shipment of hemp or hemp products produced in accordance with subtitle G of the Agricultural Marketing Act of 1946 … through the State or the territory of the Indian Tribe, as applicable.” Critical limit — Serna v. Denver Police Department, No. 21-1446 (10th Cir. 24 January 2023): §10114(b) does not create a private cause of action against state officials (affirming a Rule 12(b)(6) dismissal of a Colorado-to-Texas hemp-flight transport claim). State both halves; do not present §10114(b) as guaranteed safe passage. Texas: HB 1325 contains no interstate-transport restriction (secondary TexasCannabis.org — [Verify] against Agriculture Code Chs. 121–122); a secondary summary of Agriculture Code §122.002 says local governments may not prevent hemp cultivation, handling, transportation or sale ([Verify] verbatim). Practical risk: a load testing over 0.3% delta-9 (or over 0.3% total delta-9 under Chapter 300 from 31 March 2026), or containing delta-8 or other non-delta-9 THC over trace after 31 July 2026, is not compliant hemp and may be detained or referred. No Texas interstate hemp compact, agreement or memorandum of understanding with New Mexico, Oklahoma, Louisiana or Arkansas was found — Unavailable / [Verify] (TDA / Governor), not proof that none exists. No Texas hemp export programme was found.

10.6 New Mexico — match Run 1, plus two new points

Match Run 1 exactly on state totals and border towns (cumulative New Mexico sales over $2.35 billion through June 2026; Sunland Park cumulative over $204.5 million, Q2 2026 April over $5.8 million / May over $5.7 million / June over $5.3 million; Chaparral Q2 about $3.2 million, 4-year about $43 million; Santa Teresa Q2 over $508,000, cumulative over $4.2 million in one report; Las Cruces $2.6–2.7 million per month in Q2; Green Border tracker July 2026 $7.3 million border total, $128 million tracked since January 2025, secondary tracker). New detail: in May 2026 the Sunland Park City Council rejected special-use permits for two proposed retailers (Station X Cannabis, Cronica Cannabis) after Texas lawmakers raised safety concerns about customer routes back into El Paso (MJBizDaily, secondary — [Verify] against council minutes). Conflicts: Sunland Park dispensary count 24 (Green Border tracker) vs 48 (Fox Sports / El Paso Inc., 5 January 2026, which also reported January–November recreational sales $53.7 million, +8.7% year on year, cumulative over $165 million — the $165 million figure is superseded by the over-$204.5 million June 2026 figure; do not use the 48-count or the $165 million figure as current). Secondary articles misstate New Mexico legalisation dates (medical April 2022 / adult-use April 2024 in MMJDaily / El Paso Times summaries) — do not copy; verify New Mexico dates from New Mexico Regulation and Licensing Department / Cannabis Control Division. Texas share: Unavailable. Return-trip legality: a legal New Mexico purchase does not make interstate transport lawful (a federal offence even between legal states; Texas recognises no out-of-state cards) — the statutory citations for these propositions were not opened in that session, mark [Verify].

10.7 Oklahoma — no official total for sales to Texans; the CURT question is unresolved

No Oklahoma official total for sales to Texans was found; do not invent one. Oklahoma Medical Marijuana Authority (OMMA) temporary licence (secondary sources citing OMMA — [Verify] on the official OMMA portal): Oklahoma does not automatically recognise out-of-state cards; a 30-day temporary out-of-state patient licence is available to visitors holding a valid state-government-issued medical marijuana licence from their home state (a doctor's letter or prescription alone does not qualify); fee $100 + $4.30 processing = $104.30, with no reduced fee for temporary patients; renewal by a new application (one secondary source says renewable once consecutively before a waiting period — [Verify]). Standard Oklahoma patient licence: 2 years, $104.30, or $22.50 reduced ($20 + $2.50) with SoonerCare / Medicare / 100% disabled-veteran proof; the recommending physician must be an Oklahoma-licensed MD/DO registered with OMMA (required as of 1 January 2026, per secondary sources). Statutory anchor — Oklahoma Statutes §63-420 (Justia): “A temporary medical marijuana patient license application shall also be made available on the website of the Authority for residents of other states. Temporary medical marijuana patient licenses shall be granted to any medical marijuana license holders from other states, provided that such states have state-regulated medical marijuana programs, and applicants can prove they are members of such programs. Temporary medical marijuana patient licenses shall be issued for thirty (30) days. The cost for a temporary license shall be One Hundred Dollars ($100.00). Renewal shall be granted with resubmission of a new application. No additional criteria shall be required.” Texas wrinkle — [Verify] prominently: Texas issues no patient card (the prescription lives in CURT); whether CURT registration counts as a “valid state-government-issued medical marijuana licence” for OMMA was not confirmed from OMMA. Leafwell's flat statement that licences “issued by an out-of-state medical provider rather than a state government (for example, Texas) do not qualify” is a secondary interpretation, not an OMMA ruling on CURT — do not present it as OMMA's ruling. Texas does not recognise Oklahoma licences (secondary clinic summary, consistent with Run 1; the Texas statutory basis is [Verify]). Excluded: the 385,000-patient SEO page (Run 1) and OklahomaCannabis.us “over 360,000 active patient cards” as an OMMA count.

10.8 Borders, checkpoints, Mexico and the military

Texas border about 1,254 miles; five major Border Patrol sectors in Texas: Rio Grande Valley, Laredo, Del Rio, El Paso, Big Bend (secondary — [Verify] against the official Customs and Border Protection list). Checkpoint examples are secondary or historical only, not an inventory: I-35 Laredo (March 2016, 252.8 lb, turned over to the DEA — historical); I-10 Sierra Blanca (May 2025, 2,300 lb of THC products, “worth $1.8 million” — US Border News, secondary; product classification and valuation [Verify]; do not repeat $1.8 million as official); Falfurrias (4,008 lb in frozen broccoli — Operation Jump Start era, not a 2026 event). No Texas-specific Border Patrol hemp checkpoint policy was found in official CBP sources — interaction of the 11 December 2026 federal total-THC / 0.4 mg framework with checkpoint practice is [Verify] / Unavailable. Mexico context (secondary, label as such): CBP seized 175,000 lb of marijuana in the prior year vs about 3,000,000 lb at the 2013 peak; Dallas Police Department seized about 400 lb on 6 August 2025, Lewisville Police Department 285 lb on 3 August 2025 (anecdotes, not a statistical series). Mexico federal law still prohibits transporting cannabis across the international border into the U.S. (secondary — Mexican-law citation [Verify]). Claims to verify, NOT to repeat as fact: a secondary page (StateRegsToday) claiming Texas sued New Mexico in 2019 over its medical programme and that DPS operates permanent/temporary checkpoints — no official filing or DPS source was opened, [Verify] against Attorney General / court records and DPS; the same page's claim that “individuals cannot legally transport any form of cannabis, including CBD oil, across state lines” is overbroad against §10114(b) and must not be repeated without the hemp exception. Military (secondary guides — [Verify] against official Department of Defense / Uniform Code of Military Justice text): marijuana is illegal on military bases regardless of state law (Supremacy Clause); UCMJ Article 112a prohibits wrongful use, possession, manufacture, distribution and introduction including marijuana; zero tolerance; a state medical card offers no on-base protection; off-base use can still lead to discipline. Official event — Army Criminal Investigation Division release, 1 July 2026: the Department of the Army CID, Central Texas Field Office (Fort Hood), executed search warrants at four smoke shops in Killeen and Copperas Cove, Texas (towns near Fort Hood — NOT an on-base operation; do not describe it as a Fort Hood on-base cannabis operation). The businesses were identified as sources of illegal marijuana, THC products and synthetic cannabinoids sold to Soldiers; vape juices contained the synthetic cannabinoid MDMB-4EN-PINACA (Schedule I) marketed as “Blue Lotus”; thousands of suspected illegal THC products, marijuana, bulk suspected synthetic cannabinoids (liquid) and several firearms were seized. Supported by Texas DPS, Killeen Police Department, Copperas Cove Police Department, Bell County Sheriff's Office, Coryell County Sheriff's Office, Texas Comptroller Criminal Investigation Division, and the TABC Special Investigations Unit. Source: cid.army.mil — Army CID raids central Texas smoke shops. No Texas-specific rule for national parks, forests, federal courthouses or VA facilities was found — Unavailable / [Verify] per agency. Do not invent a list of Texas military installations beyond Fort Hood as sourced (Fort Bliss, Joint Base San Antonio and others were not established as cannabis-relevant sites in that session).

PART 11: OPERATIONS RULES — TESTING, LICENCES, CULTIVATION, SECURITY, WASTE, ENVIRONMENT, PROMOTIONS, STAFF CHECKS AND RESEARCH

Provenance — read first. Most Part 11 research came from public-web search results returned 31 October 2026, not from live-browser visits, and most verbatim rule/statute text came from Cornell / Justia / FindLaw mirrors or Texas Register summaries, not from direct opens of statutes.capitol.texas.gov chapter text (Run 1 already found direct chapter opens returned only the statute index). Four texts were opened directly in the Parts 11–12 session on 1 November 2026 (00:23–00:28 UTC): 37 TAC §12.7, §12.31, §12.32 and 4 TAC §24.31, all via Cornell. Those four are marked “direct open” below. Everything else is search-result provenance unless stated otherwise. Every mirror-sourced quote must be re-verified against official statute / Texas Register text before republication as a final combined dossier.

Federal baseline (given, not re-researched): state-licensed medical marijuana and FDA-approved products moved to Schedule III effective 28 April 2026 (91 FR 22714); adult-use remains Schedule I; §280E is generally lifted for Schedule III medical activity; hemp PL 119-37 §781 is effective 12 November 2026 but limited by PL 119-103 §2019 until 11 December 2026, after which the total-THC definition and the 0.4 mg total THC per container cap apply; USDA DEA-lab enforcement is delayed to 31 December 2026; the federal medical marijuana rider runs through 11 December 2026; there is no federal cannabis banking law. Texas application [Verify]: whether the Schedule III order by its own terms covers dose-capped low-THC cannabis treated by Texas as a distinct category, and its §280E / Texas franchise-tax (Tax Code Ch. 171; Comptroller Tax Policy memo 19 December 2025; 34 TAC §3.587, amended effective 1 March 2026 per secondary summaries) consequences, remain [Verify] — see Run 1, Part 1.2. Texas statute still lists marihuana in Schedule I and Health & Safety Code §481.034(c) bars the commissioner from rescheduling a substance the Legislature has placed in a schedule. Required before asserting either outcome: (a) the Federal Register text, (b) a DSHS commissioner action/notice under §481.034(g) including any objection, and (c) any DPS/DSHS guidance.

11.1 Testing

(a) Dispensing organisations (CUP). No independent licensed cannabis testing laboratory exists in Texas. Run 1, Part 4.3 and a TexasCannabis.org testing-lab page agree: Health & Safety Code Ch. 487 does not license independent low-THC testing labs; only licensed dispensing organisations may test low-THC cannabis, and DPS has not been authorised to license an independent facility. Source: TexasCannabis.org — testing lab.

37 TAC §12.7, Testing, Production, and Packaging — direct open, 1 November 2026 (Cornell mirror; see the staleness warning below): law.cornell.edu — 37 TAC §12.7. (a) Licensees must comply with all applicable provisions of the Texas Agriculture Code and TDA administrative rules, Title 4, Part 1. (b) “Representative samples of all processed products must be tested for the levels of tetrahydrocannabinol and cannabidiol, and for residual solvents, pesticides, fungicides, fertilizers, mold, and heavy metals, in accordance with applicable provisions of the Texas Agriculture Code and Texas Department of Agriculture's administrative rules, Title 4, Part 1, and Code of Federal Regulations, Title 16, Part 1107.” (c) Only pesticides of minimum risk exempted under the Federal Insecticide, Fungicide, and Rodenticide Act, 7 U.S.C. §136, may be used on cannabis. Active ingredients only those listed in 40 CFR §152.25(f)(1); inert ingredients only those in 40 CFR §152.25(f)(2), commonly consumed food commodities / animal feed items / edible fats and oils in 40 CFR §180.950(a), (b) and (c), and chemical substances in 40 CFR §180.950(e). All ingredients (active and inert) must be listed on the pesticide container label, active ingredients by display name and percentage by weight, each inert ingredient by display name. The product may not bear claims to control or mitigate organisms posing a threat to human health or insects/rodents carrying specific diseases. The label must comply with TDA rule 4 TAC §7.11. (e) Licensees must provide certification by a Texas-licensed professional engineer that the extraction system was commercially manufactured, safe for its intended use, and built to codes of recognised and generally accepted good engineering practice (ASME, ANSI, UL, ASTM, etc.). Final packaging for patient consumption must be in child-resistant packaging; the label must include potency (THC and CBD levels) and a statement that the product has been tested for contaminants with specific indications of all findings and the date of testing, in accordance with 16 CFR Part 1107 — §12.7(o)–(p) in the direct open, which also require physician name, patient name, dispensing organisation name / state licence number / telephone / mailing address, dosage and means of administration, packaging date, batch number / sequential serial number / bar code, potency, the testing statement with findings and date, and a bold statement that the product is for medical use only and intended for the exclusive use of the patient to whom it is prescribed.

STALENESS WARNING — do not republish §12.7 as fully current without a current-TAC check: the same direct open still shows §12.7(q): “The dispensed product may contain no more than 0.5% by weight of tetrahydrocannabinols and not less than 10% by weight of cannabidiol.” That is the pre-HB 46 cap and contradicts HB 46's 10 mg/dose / 1 g/package standard. The Cornell mirror is therefore not yet updated for HB 46 (and possibly not for 2025–2026 DPS amendments). Every §12.7 proposition in Part 11 must be checked against current 37 TAC text before republication, including the 1,000-ft rule in §11.3.

37 TAC §12.2, Requirements and Standards (Justia mirror, current through Reg. 50, No. 26; 27 June 2025, search return): regulations.justia.com — 37 TAC §12.2. (e) Licensees/registrants must cooperate fully with any DPS inspection/investigation, or state fire marshal / local designee inspection, including provision of laboratory test results, employee records, inventory and destruction records, or other records required under the Act or Ch. 12, and compliance with any lawfully issued subpoena. (f) Licensees/registrants may not cultivate, process or dispense low-THC cannabis or possess raw material / by-product if the licence or registration has expired or has been suspended or revoked. Licensees/registrants may not dispense to anyone other than a patient for whom low-THC cannabis is prescribed under Occupations Code Ch. 169, or the patient's legal guardian. Licensees/registrants may not permit or fail to prevent diversion of any controlled substance. (m) Licensees must comply with all applicable local, state and federal regulations and permitting requirements relating to air and environmental quality, advertising, business and occupancy, building, plumbing, electrical, fire safety, noise, and odor or other nuisances. (n) Licensees must use applicable best practices to limit contamination including residual solvents, metals, mold, fungus, bacterial diseases, rot, pests, pesticides, mildew, and any other contaminant identified as posing potential harm. (o) Licensees must have a plan for establishing a recall of their products in the event a product is shown by testing or other means to be, or potentially to be, defective or to have a reasonable probability that use or exposure will cause adverse health consequences. A secondary summary (TexasCannabis.org testing page, not official) adds that products failing lab tests are expected to be destroyed and that a recall plan should include how to identify affected products, notify consumers who purchased them, and destroy such products once retrieved. Mark that as secondary until §12.2(o) and any DPS recall procedure are read in full.

37 TAC §12.11 application exhibits (Cornell, search return): law.cornell.edu — 37 TAC §12.11 — an application must include proof of commercial general liability insurance (see §11.2), technical/technological ability (cultivation, analytical organic chemistry and microbiology, analytical laboratory methods, patient education/interaction, HIPAA familiarity), ability to secure premises, resources and employees, descriptions of all properties and methods, worker safety equipment and compliance plans, floor plans showing locking options for all ingress/egress consistent with life safety, alarm systems, video surveillance, the name/layout/function of each room, storage including safes and vaults, diversion-prevention procedures, an emergency management plan, a system for tracking source plant material throughout cultivation, processing and dispensing, an inventory control system as required by §12.8, electronic vehicle tracking systems, vehicle security systems, methods of screening and monitoring employees, employee qualifications/experience with chain of custody or other tracking mechanisms, a waste disposal plan, and recall procedures for any product with a reasonable probability of causing adverse health consequences based on a testing result, patient reaction, or other reason.

(b) Hemp businesses. Health & Safety Code §443.151, Testing Required (FindLaw, current as of 1 January 2026, search return — [Verify] against the official statute): codes.findlaw.com — HSC §443.151. (a) A consumable hemp product must be tested as provided by (b) and (c), or by (d). (b) Before a hemp plant is processed or otherwise used in manufacture of a consumable hemp product, a sample representing the plant must be tested, as required by the executive commissioner, to determine (1) the concentration of various cannabinoids, and (2) the presence or quantity of heavy metals, pesticides, and any other substance prescribed by the department. (c) Before material extracted from hemp by processing is sold as, offered for sale as, or incorporated into a consumable hemp product, the material must be tested, as required by the executive commissioner, to determine (1) the presence of harmful microorganisms, and (2) the presence or quantity of (A) residual solvents used in processing, if applicable, and (B) any other substance prescribed by the department. (d) Except as otherwise provided by (e), before a consumable hemp product is sold at retail or otherwise introduced into commerce in Texas, a sample representing the product must be tested (1) by a laboratory accredited by an accreditation body in accordance with ISO/IEC 17025 or a comparable or successor standard, to determine delta-9 THC concentration, and (2) by an appropriate laboratory to determine that the product does not contain a substance described by (b) or (c) in a quantity prohibited for those subsections. (e) A product is not required to be tested under (d) if each hemp-derived ingredient has been tested under (b) and (c), or under (d), and does not have a delta-9 THC concentration of more than 0.3%.

25 TAC Ch. 300, as adopted effective 31 March 2026 (Texas Register, March 2026 adopted-rules page, search return): sos.state.tx.us — March 2026 adopted rules, 25 TAC — amendments to §§300.100–300.103, 300.201–300.203, 300.301–300.303, 300.402–300.404, 300.501, 300.502, 300.601–300.606, and new §§300.204–300.208, 300.405–300.407, 300.701, 300.702, concerning the manufacture, distribution and retail sale of consumable hemp products. Effective 31 March 2026; proposal publication 26 December 2025 (50 TexReg 8486). Necessary to comply with Executive Order GA-56. Chapter 300 was originally effective 2 August 2020 under HB 1325. COA definition (adopted): “An official document from an accredited laboratory available to the manufacturer, processor, distributor, retailer, public, or department. The COA shows the concentrations of cannabinoid analytes and other measurements required by the department, including data on THC levels, and states whether a sample passed or failed content analysis limits.” CHP definition: any product processed or manufactured for consumption that contains hemp, including food, a drug, a device, and a cosmetic, as defined by Health & Safety Code §431.002. DSHS response to comment: the definition of accredited laboratory should only be ISO/IEC 17025 — DSHS disagreed and declined to revise, because HB 1325 includes “comparable standard” language. DSHS Chapter 300 rules PDF (search return): dshs.texas.gov — Chapter 300 CHP rules, 31 March 2026 (PDF).

Proposed-rules summary (26 December 2025, search return — check against the adopted text): sos.state.tx.us — December 2025 proposed rules, 25 TAC — new §300.204 master production records; new §300.205 individual batch production records; new §300.206 source and traceability requirements for raw materials and ingredients; new §300.207 requirements for conducting product recalls, including maintaining a written recall plan; new §300.208 documentation, evaluation and investigation of consumer complaints by CHP licensees; amended §300.301 clarifies testing for raw hemp, hemp-derived ingredients and CHPs and adds subsections (b) and (d)–(f) outlining COA requirements; amended §300.302 clarifies acceptable delta-9 THC limits and business responsibility for providing samples to DSHS at the business's own expense, retitled from “Sample Analysis of Consumable Hemp and Certain Cannabinoid Oils” to “Sample Analysis of Consumable Hemp Products”; the proposal repeals subsection (f) regarding DSHS acceptance of sample results from other accredited laboratories, and subsection (h) — text truncated in the search return, [Verify] the full adopted §300.303. Proposed §300.301 text as returned (December 2025 proposal, check against adopted): (a) Before a hemp plant is processed or otherwise used in manufacture, a representative sample must be tested to determine (1) the concentration and identity of cannabinoids, including all acids; (2) the presence and quantity of heavy metals, pesticides, microbial contamination, and other substances prescribed by the department; (3) the presence and concentration of delta-9 THC, total delta-9 THC, and total THC; and (4) a total delta-9 THC concentration of 0.3% or less on a dry-weight basis. (b) Before a consumable hemp product, including hemp-derived ingredients used for further processing, is sold at retail, distributed, or otherwise introduced into commerce, a representative sample must be tested to determine (1) the presence, concentration and identity of cannabinoids; (2) the presence and concentration of delta-9 THC, total delta-9 THC, and total THC; (3) the presence and quantity of residual solvents, heavy metals, pesticides, and harmful pathogens; and (4) a total delta-9 THC concentration of 0.3% or less on a dry-weight basis. A COA documenting tests conducted — text truncated, [Verify]. Proposed §300.303 / enforcement snippet: a person who “forges, falsifies, or alters the results of a laboratory test authorized or required by this chapter” — products in violation “must be retested and are subject to detention or embargo under Texas Health and Safety Code §431.048”; an accredited-laboratory ownership snippet requires that a licence holder have “no ownership interest in the accredited laboratory” or hold “10 percent or less ownership interest” if the laboratory — text truncated and bracketed in the proposal, so the adopted §300.303 ownership rule remains [Verify]. Expired COAs are not valid; products with expired COAs must be retested and are subject to detention or embargo.

Litigation status update — check the docket at publication: a Travis County district court entered a temporary restraining order in April 2026 blocking enforcement of the most consequential parts of the new DSHS rules (Kight on Cannabis summary, search return): it restrained enforcement of the challenged rules to the extent they condition manufacture, testing, transport, distribution or sale on a “total delta-9 THC” / “acceptable hemp THC level” standard instead of the Legislature's delta-9 THC standard, specifically §§300.101(1) and 300.101(45) definitions, §§300.301–300.303 testing/commercialisation provisions, §§300.206(c) and 300.404 transport/manufacturing restrictions, and §300.601(b) and related penalty/enforcement provisions including embargoes, detentions, licence suspensions/revocations and civil actions, to that extent. A hearing on a temporary injunction was set for 23 April 2026; a temporary restraining order expires after 14 days unless extended by agreement or further court order. Source: cannabusiness.law — Texas judge hits pause on DSHS hemp rules — but the Texas Hemp Business Council regulations page (updated 17 days before the search in that session) states: “As of the review date above, the challenged rules remain enforceable. The lawsuit does not currently give businesses permission to disregard the challenged requirements.” Source: texashempbusinesscouncil.com — Texas hemp regulations. The April temporary restraining order and the later “remain enforceable” statement are not reconciled by the searches alone. Do not state a single litigation status without checking the SS Distro / THBC docket at publication. The same THBC page confirms DSHS fees restored to $10,000 per licensed facility / $5,000 per retail location, plus online fees, and that §300.602 prohibits introducing noncompliant hemp-derived cannabinoids into commerce, packaging/labelling products with noncompliant labels, and refusing authorised entry, inspection, sampling, record review/copying, or inspection photography (expressly including impeding an inspection, aggressive behaviour, foul language, and threatening behaviour); manufacturing, processing, distributing, selling or introducing CHPs into commerce without the required licence/registration is a separate violation; each day a violation continues or occurs counts as a separate violation for administrative penalty calculation (authorities cited: 25 TAC §§300.601–300.602). Retailers selling CHPs manufactured or processed elsewhere must, upon request, provide DSHS with evidence of compliant production under the applicable USDA-approved state or Tribal plan, a federal plan where applicable, or the relevant foreign jurisdiction's laws, together with testing, THC-compliance and federal requirements in §300.403 (authority cited: Health & Safety Code §443.206; 25 TAC §300.403). Agricultural hemp is regulated separately from finished CHPs; a DSHS retail registration does not authorise growing or handling agricultural hemp, and a TDA producer licence does not authorise manufacturing finished cannabinoid products (Agriculture Code Chs. 121–122; 4 TAC Ch. 24).

Commercial lab summary (ACS Laboratory, secondary — do NOT present as Texas law): acslab.com — Texas hemp compliance — ACS states it is DEA-registered and ISO/IEC 17025:2017 accredited by A2LA, serves as a third-party laboratory under TDA, and provides DSHS-compliant testing measuring cannabinoids, solvents, heavy metals, pesticides and pathogens, issuing accredited COAs with QR links and measurement-of-uncertainty data. ACS states Texas pre-harvest testing: a TDA-licensed agent must collect representative plant samples within 15 days of harvest, analysed to verify total delta-9 THC ≤0.3% dry weight; laboratories must report results to TDA within 14 days. This 15-day figure conflicts with the 30-day pre-harvest window in 7 CFR Part 990 and with other secondary summaries stating 30 days (see §11.3). Flag it as a conflict to resolve against current 4 TAC Ch. 24, not as settled. ACS states Texas requires pre-harvest hemp crops to be tested for total delta-9 THC only; after processing, hemp ingredients and finished consumable products require full testing. ACS tests 32 cannabinoids by HPLC-UV, including total Δ9-THC, Δ8-THC, CBDV, CBG, CBC and CBN, with measurement-of-uncertainty; heavy metals arsenic, cadmium, lead and mercury by ICP-MS; mycotoxins aflatoxins B1, B2, G1 and G2 and ochratoxin A (beyond the Texas minimum categories, per ACS). ACS notes the new schedule concludes delta-8 and all THC isomers that are NOT delta-9 THC are considered controlled substances, effective 31 July 2026.

DEA-registered labs — federal layer, and the Texas-application conflict to flag: 7 CFR §990.25 (search return): analytical testing for total THC must use post-decarboxylation or other similarly reliable methods approved by the Secretary; methodology must consider the potential conversion of THCA into THC and the result must reflect total available THC from the sum of THC and THCA; methods include gas or liquid chromatography with detection; total THC is determined and reported on a dry-weight basis; measurement of uncertainty (MU) must be estimated and reported. “After December 31, 2022, USDA licensees may only use laboratories registered with the DEA to conduct testing under this section.” 7 CFR §990.3 (state/Tribal plans, search return): a state/Tribal plan must include sampling within 30 days prior to anticipated harvest by a sampling agent (producers may not collect samples from their own growing facilities); laboratories conducting total-THC testing must meet quality-assurance, validated-methods and MU requirements; “After December 31, 2022, States and Indian Tribes shall require that only laboratories registered with the DEA may conduct testing under this section.” Laboratories shall only submit test results used to determine compliance; informal in-season test results shall not be reported to USDA. Sources: law.lawstack.com — 7 CFR 990.25; law.lawstack.com — 7 CFR 990.3. Given the given baseline (USDA enforcement of DEA-registered lab testing delayed to 31 December 2026), how that delay interacts with the 2022 DEA-registration language in 7 CFR Part 990 and with Texas's own USDA-approved state plan (TDA-registered labs) was NOT resolved. Mark as [Verify]: does Texas currently require DEA registration for pre-harvest labs, for CHP labs, for both, or for neither, and does the 31 December 2026 delay change the Texas position?

11.2 Licence rules

(a) Dispensing organisations. Renewal: biennial renewal fee $318,511 + $530 registration, per 37 TAC §12.14 and Run 1, Part 4.1. The application form (DPS RSD-301, 03/2019, via TemplateRoller, search return) states: “Type of Application: New / Renewal / Previous License Number, if Renewal”; only the application fee is required at submission; the licence fee is required upon approval; all application fees are non-refundable and not transferable. Sources: regulations.justia.com — 37 TAC §12.14.

Insurance — confirmed minimums, 37 TAC §12.11(b)(6) (Cornell, search return): proof of commercial general liability insurance coverage against claims for damage to property of third parties and for personal injuries to third parties, including bodily injury, property damage, and product liability, with limits of (A) $1,000,000 each occurrence; (B) $2,000,000 General Aggregate limit; and (C) $1,000,000 Product Liability. No separate bonding minimum was located in Run 1 or in this session's searches; Run 1 flagged “no CUP bonding minimum was located” as [Verify]. Do not invent a bond amount.

Revocation — 37 TAC §12.23 (Cornell, search return; adopted Texas Register Vol. 41, No. 02, 8 January 2016, TexReg 493, eff. 10 January 2016; amended Vol. 42, No. 10, 10 March 2017, TexReg 1146, eff. 15 March 2017; HB 46 amendment adopted 2025, see below): law.cornell.edu — 37 TAC §12.23. (a) The Department may revoke a licence or registration if the licensee/registrant: (1) performed a regulated function prior to issuance; (2) misrepresents a material fact in any application or other information filed under the Act or Ch. 12; (3) prepares/submits false, incorrect, incomplete or misleading forms/reports on multiple occasions; (4) performs a regulated function while suspended; (5) exhibits a pattern of misconduct evidenced by previous violations for which previous suspensions have been inadequate to affect compliance; (6) is convicted of a disqualifying felony or misdemeanour offence pursuant to §12.3 (Criminal History Disqualifiers); (7) violates §§481.120, 481.121, 481.122, or 481.125, Health & Safety Code; or (8) submits a payment that is dishonoured, reversed, or otherwise insufficient or invalid. (b) Following notification of violation, the licensee will be provided 30 days to address the violation or request a hearing by submitting the request electronically through the department's website or as otherwise determined by the department. (c) Except as provided in (b) [in the returned text, (d) is the payment exception], an individual whose certificate of registration has been revoked may not be relicensed or reregistered earlier than 2 years from revocation. (d) An individual whose registration has been revoked for a dishonoured/reversed payment under (a)(8) may reapply at any time; approval is contingent on receipt of the full amount due, including additional processing fees. (e) Other than as provided in (d), an individual whose licence/registration has been revoked must follow the applicable procedures under §12.11 or §12.12 for new applications. HB 46 revocation amendment (Texas Register, adopted rules, 24 October 2025, search return): sos.state.tx.us — October 2025 adopted rules, 37 TAC — DPS adopts amendments to §12.23, concerning Revocation, without changes to the proposed text published 22 August 2025 (50 TexReg 5443). The proposed amendment implements HB 46, 89th Legislature, Regular Session (2025), “by authorizing revocation for a dispensing organization license for failing to begin dispensing low-THC cannabis within 24 months of license issuance or failing to continue dispensing low-THC cannabis during the term of the license.” Filed 8 October 2025, TRD-202503646, effective date 1 November 2025. Comments through 22 September 2025; written comments by Raj Patel on behalf of NXP Wellness, LLC in support. Note: the returned Texas Register summary cites Occupations Code §§1701.151 and 1701.153 as affected — that citation appears to be a Register-summary artefact; do not repeat it as the CUP statutory authority without checking the adopted rule preamble. CUP authority remains Health & Safety Code Ch. 487.

Denial — §§12.15 and 12.16 (old compiled rules, search return): the Department may deny a licence application if the applicant fails to pass an onsite inspection, fails to satisfy requirements, or violated the Act / Ch. 12 / §§481.120, 481.121, 481.122 or 481.125 prior to licensure or renewal. Following denial notice, the applicant is provided 30 days to request a hearing. The Department may deny registration of a director, manager or employee if disqualified under §12.3. Registrant denial: hearing request through the department website within 30 days of denial notice. [Verify] against the current §12.15/§12.16 text, as the returned compilation is dated.

Transfers, ownership changes, relocation — NOT FOUND as a specific rule. Run 1 already flagged: “No specific transfer rule was opened in this session — 37 TAC Ch. 12, Subchapter C and licence-application rules govern amendments; [Verify] transfer, relocation and change-of-ownership provisions in 37 TAC §§12.11–12.14 and Subchapter C before advising a transaction. Do not assume a CUP licence is freely transferable.” The DPS application form (RSD-301) requires current email, physical mailing address, facsimile, and the physical address of the location at which low-THC cannabis is cultivated, processed or dispensed, per 37 TAC §§12.5 and 12.6, and requires complete ownership documentation (Exhibit A, §12.11(b)(1)) and applications for directors, managers and employees (Exhibit B). That supports a DPS-approval / re-registration model for ownership or site changes, but the exact transfer/relocation procedure remains [Verify] / Unavailable until §§12.5, 12.6, 12.11 and Subchapter C are read in full. Application fees are expressly non-refundable and not transferable (RSD-301 attestation; §12.14(e) payment-dishonour rule) — that is about fees, not about licence transferability; do not conflate the two.

Background checks — CUP: see §11.8. §12.3 Criminal History Disqualifiers is referenced by §§12.16 and 12.23, but its full disqualifier list was NOT returned. Mark §12.3 detail as [Verify]. New in this session — proposed 2026 registration amendments (Texas Register, Proposed Rules, 24 April 2026, search return): sos.state.tx.us — April 2026 proposed rules, 37 TAC — “In conjunction with the dispensing organization's application for license, or prior to employment with a currently licensed dispensing organization, directors, owners, managers, members, and employees must submit: (1) Identifiers, including the individual's full name, date of birth, telephone number, electronic mail address, residential address, and driver license or state-issued identification number; [and] (2) Fingerprints submitted in the manner approved by the department” — plus proof of eligibility for registration under the Personal Responsibility and Work Opportunity Reconciliation Act of 1996 verified through the Department of Homeland Security SAVE Program. “The department may deny the application for registration of a director, owner, manager, member, or employee of a dispensing organization if the applicant is disqualified pursuant to §12.3 of this title (relating to Criminal History Disqualifiers) or the applicant is found to have violated any provision of the Act or this chapter. The applicant may request a hearing by submitting a request through the department's website within thirty (30) days of the date of the denial notice.” Filed 10 April 2026, TRD-202601569; earliest possible date of adoption: 24 May 2026. Check whether this proposal was adopted and in what final form — it would confirm the HB 46 expansion of registration to owners/members, but the proposal alone is not current law.

(b) Hemp businesses. DSHS: 1-year term, per location, $10,300 manufacturer / $5,150 retail including the Texas Online fee (Run 1, confirmed again by the DSHS programme page: dshs.texas.gov — Consumable Hemp Program). Renewal at the same fees on the live DSHS page; no separate renewal discount or small-business tier was located. Revocation/suspension grounds in the March 2026 Chapter 300 rules include sale to a person under 21 and ID failure (Run 1), plus §300.601–§300.602 prohibited acts (unlicensed manufacture/distribution/sale, noncompliant cannabinoids, noncompliant labels, refusing entry/inspection/sampling/record review). Each day of violation counts separately for administrative penalty calculation (THBC summary of §§300.601–300.602, secondary — verify in the adopted text). Transfers, ownership changes, relocation for DSHS licences/registrations: no specific transfer rule was located. DSHS licences/registrations are per location and per facility, with GPS perimeter coordinates / legal property description and property-owner authorisation required for manufacturers (Run 1, secondary guidance — rule text not fully opened). Do NOT assume a DSHS licence is transferable to a new owner or new address without a new application / approval. Mark as [Verify] / Unavailable. Distributor question carried from Run 1: DSHS guidance points distributors to the wholesaler licensing framework under Health & Safety Code Ch. 431 (Food, Drug, Device, and Cosmetic Act). Whether a separate Ch. 431 wholesaler licence is required in addition to a CHP licence for a pure distributor remains [Verify]; this is a Part 7 follow-up in Run 1 and remains open for Part 11. TDA: licence types Producer, Handler, Handler/Sampler, non-consumable Processor, plus Research (universities only) and Laboratory (Run 1, §4.2, fees [Verify]). Eligibility per Run 1 secondary summaries: 18+, no controlled-substance felony within the preceding 10 years, mandatory TDA orientation, facility geospatial/GPS information and proof of ownership/control, Farm Service Agency registration, pre-harvest official sampling/testing, harvest within the window after sampling, lot reporting. All TDA fees are non-refundable (secondary). The transfer/relocation procedure for TDA licences was not located; the GPS facility-location modification fee of $500 was reported secondarily in Run 1 ([Verify]). Mark TDA transfer/relocation as [Verify]. TABC: no hemp licence to transfer. TABC alcohol permit transfer rules are a separate regime and are NOT stated here, to avoid conflating alcohol permits with hemp authorisation.

11.3 Cultivation rules

(a) Dispensing organisations. Vertically integrated single licence: cultivate, process and dispense at a single location, as approved by DPS, plus DPS-approved satellites for secure storage/distribution under §487.1035 (Run 1). DPS facility FAQ (opened in Run 1): “All cultivation, processing and dispensing must be performed at a single location. Remote pickup locations are authorized so long as the product has already been dispensed and labeled for the receiving patient and the products are not stored overnight.” “All cultivation of low-THC cannabis shall take place in an enclosed, secured building, or an enclosure within a building. See Texas Administrative Rule §12.31.”

37 TAC §12.31, Security of Facilities — direct open, 1 November 2026: law.cornell.edu — 37 TAC §12.31 — see §11.4 for the full §12.31 text. No home cultivation, no outdoor CUP cultivation as a general right, no separate cultivation-only licence (Run 1). The indoor/enclosed rule above is the operative cultivation-location rule found. Whether greenhouses count as an “enclosed, secured building, or enclosure within a building” for a specific site is a DPS-approval question; do not generalise beyond the rule text. Pesticide limit for CUP cultivation: only FIFRA minimum-risk pesticides, 40 CFR §152.25(f) / §180.950, per 37 TAC §12.7(c) above. No other CUP cultivation pesticide list was found.

1,000-ft school/daycare buffer — RESOLVED IN PART, with a staleness caveat (new direct open): 37 TAC §12.7(n) in the direct open states: “All regulated premises shall be located at least 1000 feet from any private or public school or day care center that existed prior to the date of initial license application, measured from the closest points on the respective property lines.” Run 1 had flagged a secondary 1,000-ft claim as NOT appearing on the DPS facility FAQ and NOT confirmed from §12.31 text opened in Run 1. The buffer is in §12.7(n) as returned — but because the same Cornell page still carries the pre-HB 46 §12.7(q) cap (see the §11.1 staleness warning), the parent must confirm §12.7(n) survives in current 37 TAC text (including any HB 46 / 2025–2026 DPS amendment) before publishing it as a current CUP rule. Do not cite the secondary Ector County page as authority; cite §12.7(n) once current-TAC-checked. The 1,000-ft figures that ARE documented in Run 1 for city vape/smoke-shop ordinances (Austin proposal, Brownsville) are separate and must not be merged with the CUP buffer.

(b) Hemp (TDA). A licence is required before planting: a TDA Producer licence plus a Lot Crop Permit per lot (Run 1). A separate Lot Crop Permit is required before actually planting a crop (secondary summaries, consistent). Plot/facility registration: facility geospatial/GPS information, proof of ownership/control, Farm Service Agency registration (Run 1 secondary summaries — verify in 4 TAC Ch. 24 / TDA eApply instructions).

Pre-harvest sampling — conflicting windows, flag explicitly:

  • Federal / USDA plan rule, 7 CFR §990.3 and §990.24 (search return): samples must be collected within 30 days prior to anticipated harvest, by a sampling agent; producers may not collect samples from their own facilities; a producer must harvest no later than 30 days after sample collection, otherwise a second pre-harvest sample is required.
  • Texas rule, 4 TAC §24.23, Other Activities (Cornell, search return; adopted Texas Register Vol. 45, No. 10, 6 March 2020, TexReg 1669, eff. 11 March 2020): law.cornell.edu — 4 TAC §24.23 — (a) a licence holder shall not harvest a cannabis crop prior to samples being collected. (b) A licence holder shall harvest the crop not more than 15 days following the date of sample collection by the Department, unless specifically authorised in writing by the Department. (c) Prior to processing, cannabis from harvested lots shall not be commingled with cannabis from other harvested lots or other material without prior permission from the Department. (d) A licence holder may not sell or use harvested plants unless a test of the sample(s) for the lot associated with the harvested plants is at or below the acceptable hemp THC level.
  • Secondary training material (Docsity, Texas Hemp Sampling Agent Training practice exam, search return) states: sampling ensures compliance with the 0.3% dry-weight limit; samples must be collected within 30 days prior to the anticipated harvest date; a minimum of 8 plants per lot to be sampled; sampling during flowering when THC peaks; a COA is required before harvesting; requests outside the 30-day window must be denied/reported to TDA; sampling agent licences are renewed annually. This is training material, not rule text; the 8-plant minimum and annual sampler renewal remain [Verify] against 4 TAC Ch. 24.
  • ACS Laboratory summary (secondary, search return) states samples within 15 days of harvest and lab reporting to TDA within 14 days. That 15-day figure matches the Texas §24.23 harvest window (harvest ≤15 days after collection), not the federal 30-day sampling window, and may reflect a conflation of the two windows. Resolve: (i) the sampling window (federal 30 days vs Texas practice), (ii) the harvest deadline (Texas 15 days vs federal 30 days), and (iii) the lab reporting deadline (14 days — secondary only, [Verify]), before publishing a single timeline.

Indoor/outdoor hemp: no Texas rule prohibiting outdoor hemp cultivation was found; hemp is an agricultural crop grown outdoors and in greenhouses (Run 1 acreage includes greenhouse square footage in 2021: “over 15 million square feet in greenhouses”). No indoor-only rule for hemp was located. If a specific TDA indoor/greenhouse rule exists in 4 TAC Ch. 24, it was not returned; mark it as [Verify] rather than asserting “no indoor rule” absolutely, but do not invent one. Seed: AgriLife procedure (see §11.9) states individuals shall not sell, offer for sale, distribute, or use hemp seed in Texas unless the seed is certified or approved by TDA. [Verify] against current 4 TAC Ch. 24 seed provisions before republication.

11.4 Security requirements

(a) Dispensing organisations — 37 TAC Ch. 12, Subchapter D, §§12.31–12.32 and §12.11 exhibits. §12.31 — direct open, 1 November 2026 (full text, summarised with verbatim operative phrases): law.cornell.edu — 37 TAC §12.31. (a) Effective controls/procedures to prevent unauthorised access, theft and diversion; Subchapter D standards are minimum standards only. (b) No public/unauthorised access to cultivation, processing or product storage areas during regular business (except (d)); enclosed, secured building or enclosure within a building; access limited to the minimum number of individuals/employees; a designated individual (or limited number of individuals) responsible for each area with authority to enter/control entry; enclosed locked area access limited to a licensee, director, manager or registered employee acting in an official capacity. (c) Access to cultivation, processing or product storage areas by authorised employees shall at a minimum be restricted by a physical barrier with a mechanical locking device compliant with life safety requirements, kept closed and locked at all times when not immediately being used to enter or exit; areas clearly and conspicuously marked at all access points with signage indicating access is restricted to individuals registered with DPS under Health & Safety Code Ch. 487, in contrasting block letters at least one inch in height. (d) Unregistered individuals (employees or contractors, business guests or visitors, maintenance or other service providers not regulated under the Act) present in or passing through regulated premises must be continuously escorted by a registrant, provided a visitor's badge reflecting name and date of issuance, and all ingress/egress recorded in a daily log (full name, time of arrival, time of departure, purpose of visit). This does not apply to department or other state law enforcement representatives touring the facility as part of official duties. (e) Alarm system capable of continuously monitoring regulated premises for fire and intrusion by camera recording, door switches, motion sensors, and fire and smoke detectors; capability of immediately alerting local law enforcement of a fire at any time, of a security breach during non-business hours, and of being manually activated by staff during business hours; camera monitoring system capable of recording at least 90 days of footage to an external hard drive at minimum resolution of 720 x 350, with camera coverage of all regulated areas, including all ingress/egress areas, and the building exterior; point of sale areas, if applicable, must have a camera placed to provide visual identification of any patient or legal guardian seeking to fill a prescription; exterior lighting sufficient to support camera monitoring; the system must comply with local city/county alarm permitting requirements; capable of continuous function upon total power loss for a minimum of five (5) minutes. (f) Access to cultivation, processing or product storage areas must be through a metal security door on a metal frame with hinges protected from ingress; the metal door must have secure locking capability; the door, frame and locking mechanism must be compliant with life safety requirements; if magnetic locks are used, the lock must work independently without exterior power. Post-cultivation low-THC cannabis products must be stored in a locking safe or metal locking container, in compliance with 21 CFR §1301.72. (g) Cultivation, processing, production or raw material storage areas should have no windows unless the windows are shatter resistant or burglar proof, or reinforced with metal bars or grates to prevent entry. (h) If a facility shares space or has an adjacent building, the facility must ensure no mutual access points exist, including ceiling or roof areas enabling unauthorised access from the adjacent structure. (i) Final low-THC cannabis products and raw materials, including plants in any stages of growth, may not be visible from the exterior of the building during non-business hours.

§12.32, Security of Vehicles — direct open, 1 November 2026 (this resolves the prior §12.32 gap): law.cornell.edu — 37 TAC §12.32 — (a) Any vehicle used by a dispensing organisation for transportation of low-THC cannabis must have a vehicle security system and a securely attached and locked container within the vehicle; the licensee must ensure only authorised registered employees have access to the locked secure container. (b) Prior to transportation, the licensee shall complete a trip plan including: (1) the name of the registrant responsible for transportation; (2) the date and start time of the trip; (3) the anticipated route and destination; and (4) a detailed invoice or log of the specific type of product and amount to be transported. (c) Promptly following transportation, the licensee shall enter the end time of the trip and any changes to the trip plan, including any changes to the amount of product delivered to the location. Adopted Texas Register Vol. 41, No. 02, 8 January 2016, TexReg 494, eff. 10 January 2016; amended Vol. 42, No. 10, 10 March 2017, TexReg 1146, eff. 15 March 2017. DPS facility FAQ (opened in Run 1): “Deliveries may only be made by registered employees of the dispensing organization. Delivery vehicles must meet the security requirements provided in Texas Administrative Rule §12.32.” §12.11 security exhibits (see §11.1): floor plans with locking options for all ingress/egress consistent with life safety, alarm systems, video surveillance, room layout/function, storage including safes and vaults, diversion-prevention procedures, emergency management plan, source-plant tracking, inventory control (§12.8), electronic vehicle tracking, vehicle security systems, and employee screening/monitoring. A secondary summary (Flourish Software, Texas state page, search return — not official): flourishsoftware.com — Texas — “Under TAC §12.32 and related rules, dispensing organizations must maintain comprehensive physical security at all licensed locations. Required security measures include controlled-access areas, alarm systems, and surveillance coverage of all areas where cannabis is cultivated, stored, processed, or dispensed. Facilities must allow emergency access for fire departments and emergency medical services. All transportation of cannabis products must use locked containers, authorized registered employees” [truncated]. Also, under TAC §12.2 (Flourish summary): all licensees must establish/implement a drug-free workplace policy consistent with Texas Workforce Commission requirements, conspicuously display the DPS-issued licence at each location, and cooperate fully with DPS or state fire marshal inspection. The drug-free workplace sentence in Flourish is secondary; [Verify] against current §12.2 text, which in the Justia return quoted above does not include a drug-free workplace subsection in the excerpt returned.

(b) Hemp businesses — NO CUP-style security code found. No 25 TAC Ch. 300 or 4 TAC Ch. 24 security section equivalent to 37 TAC Subchapter D was located for DSHS manufacturers/retailers or TDA growers/handlers. What IS required (from Run 1 and the THBC summary above): written consent to entry/inspection by DSHS, TABC and law enforcement (DSHS manufacturer application); DSHS/TABC/law-enforcement inspection, sampling, record review/copying and inspection photography; refusal or impeding inspection is a violation under §§300.601–300.602; source/traceability and batch records (§§300.204–300.206); COA/QR traceability. General business security (locks, alarms, cameras) for a hemp retail/manufacturing site is therefore governed by general law, lease, insurance and local building/fire codes, not by a hemp-specific security rule found here. State this as “no hemp-specific security rule located” rather than “no security required”, and flag a full-text search of 25 TAC Ch. 300 and 4 TAC Ch. 24 for “security” as a follow-up. The TDA research procedure (see §11.9) does require unrestricted access for TDA, DEA, DPS and local law enforcement to all hemp plants and facilities used for production/storage, and notification of theft of cannabis materials by the licence holder. That is a research-licence condition, not a general hemp security code.

11.5 Waste disposal, including non-compliant hemp destruction

(a) Dispensing organisations. A waste disposal plan is a required §12.11 application exhibit (see §11.1). Inventory and destruction records must be provided on DPS / fire marshal inspection under §12.2(e) (Justia return above). Best-practices contamination control under §12.2(n) and the recall plan under §12.2(o) (see §11.1) are the closest operative waste/recall provisions returned. The exact method of destruction (rendering unusable, mixing, incineration, witnessed destruction, DEA reverse distributor, etc.) for CUP waste was NOT returned. Mark the CUP destruction method as [Verify] against full 37 TAC Ch. 12, Subchapter C / §12.8 inventory provisions. Do not import the TDA/DEA hemp destruction method below into CUP waste without a rule citation.

(b) Hemp — TDA non-compliant plants, 4 TAC §24.31 — direct open, 1 November 2026. law.cornell.edu — 4 TAC §24.31 — adopted Texas Register Vol. 45, No. 10, 6 March 2020, TexReg 1669, eff. 11 March 2020. (a) “Cannabis plants exceeding the acceptable hemp THC level constitute marijuana, a Schedule I controlled substance, which must be disposed of in accordance with the federal Controlled Substances Act (CSA) in 21 C.F.R. § 13 and DEA regulations in 21 C.F.R. 1317.15.” [Note: “21 C.F.R. § 13” in the returned text appears to be a truncated citation; verify the full citation in official §24.31.] (b) “A final test result exceeding the acceptable hemp THC level shall be conclusive evidence that the lot represented by the sample is non-compliant with state and federal law. The cannabis on that lot may not be further handled, processed, or enter the stream of commerce, other than for disposal purposes in strict compliance with the CSA and DEA regulations.” (c) Disposal: (1) “Within five (5) days of receiving a notice of disposal from the Department, the license holder shall contact an appropriate DEA-registered reverse distributor or other authorized person or entity to request disposal of the non-compliant cannabis plants in strict compliance with the CSA and DEA regulations.” (2) “The license holder shall pay all costs and fees required for the destruction of non-compliant cannabis plants and shall surrender such plants to the DEA-registered reverse distributor or other authorized person or entity for disposal in accordance with DEA regulations, without compensation from TDA, the State of Texas, or U.S. authorities.” (d) “License holders must notify USDA and the Department of intent to dispose of non-compliant cannabis plants and verify disposal by maintaining and submitting records of the disposal.” A federal remediation alternative (USDA, secondary summary, NOT Texas rule text): USDA guidelines list approved disposal methods including plowing under, composting, disking, bush mowing, deep burial in trenches at least 12 inches deep, and burning; alternative remediation is removing/destroying non-compliant flowers while keeping stalks/leaves/seeds, or shredding the entire plant into homogenous biomass that can be retested. Whether Texas's §24.31 (which says the lot “may not be further handled, processed, or enter the stream of commerce, other than for disposal”) permits federal-style remediation for a Texas lot was NOT resolved. Mark remediation availability in Texas as [Verify]; do not present federal remediation as a Texas right. A secondary summary (LegalClarity, search return) adds: if results confirm within the limit, the grower can apply for a transport manifest to move it; if above 0.3%, the grower must destroy the crop and file a disposal report with TDA. The transport-manifest requirement itself remains [Verify] against 4 TAC Ch. 24.

(c) Hemp — DSHS consumable products, failed / noncompliant / expired. Products found in violation (including forged, falsified or altered lab results, per the proposed §300.303 snippet) “must be retested and are subject to detention or embargo under Texas Health and Safety Code §431.048.” Products with expired COAs must be retested and are subject to detention or embargo (same snippet). The DSHS disciplinary PDF (Run 1, §5.3) lists fines/settlements, not lot-level embargoes. Run 1 already recorded: “No separate DSHS 'embargo list' with product/lot-level embargoes was found — mark as Unavailable / [Verify] if Run 1 needs lot-level embargoes. Do not conflate disciplinary fines with embargoes.” That remains true for Part 11; the embargo authority (§431.048) is not a published embargo list. CHP recall: new §300.207 requires a written recall plan (proposed/adopted March 2026, see §11.1). Whether §300.207 also imposes a mandatory recall (vs a plan), notification to DSHS/consumers, and destruction/return procedures was not returned in full; mark the mandatory-recall trigger and procedure as [Verify] against the adopted §300.207 text. General waste: no DSHS hemp waste-disposal method (for example rendering unusable) was located beyond detention/embargo and the TDA destruction rule for plants. Mark the CHP waste method as [Verify] / Unavailable.

11.6 Environmental rules: water, pesticide limits, energy

No Texas cannabis/hemp-specific water-use, energy-use, or carbon rule was located for either CUP or hemp businesses. Do not invent one. General environmental compliance IS required for CUP: 37 TAC §12.2(m) (Justia return above): licensees must comply with all applicable local, state and federal regulations and permitting requirements relating to air and environmental quality, advertising, business and occupancy, building, plumbing, electrical, fire safety, noise, and odor or other nuisances. That is a compliance-by-reference rule, not a cannabis-specific water/energy standard. Pesticides — CUP: only FIFRA minimum-risk pesticides, 40 CFR §152.25(f) / §180.950, per 37 TAC §12.7(c) above (direct open). Testing for pesticides, fungicides and fertilizers is required under §12.7(b). No other CUP pesticide list was found. Pesticides — hemp: no Texas hemp-specific pesticide list was located. The operative framework returned is federal plus general Texas pesticide law: the Environmental Protection Agency — since December 2019, EPA has approved adding hemp to the use sites of 49 pesticides (46 biopesticides and one conventional pesticide in the January 2021 EPA snapshot; a December 2019 news summary says 10 products initially, nine biopesticides and one conventional). Examples in the EPA snapshot include neem oil, azadirachtin, Bacillus thuringiensis subsp. kurstaki, Beauveria bassiana strain GHA, potassium bicarbonate, potassium silicate, acetic acid, vinegar, and others by EPA Reg. No. The EPA list is by label; a product is lawful on hemp only if its label includes hemp as a use site and it is used per label. Sources: epa.gov (January 2021 snapshot) — pesticide products registered for use on hemp; hempgazette.com — EPA hemp pesticide. Texas pesticide law: Agriculture Code Ch. 76 (Pesticide and Herbicide Law), administered by TDA, including state-limited-use pesticide lists (Agriculture Code §76.003, as returned in an AgriLife Extension laws supplement). Whether TDA has adopted a hemp-specific limited-use list or a hemp pesticide guidance document was NOT located; mark it as [Verify] on TDA pesticide pages. Testing for pesticides is required at the hemp-plant and CHP stages under Health & Safety Code §443.151(b)–(d) and 25 TAC §300.301 (see §11.1). No Texas maximum residue limit (MRL) table for hemp pesticides was located; the “quantity prohibited” standard in §443.151 is by department prescription, not by a table returned here. Mark MRLs as [Verify]. Water: no hemp-specific irrigation, groundwater or water-rights rule was located. Texas water law (Water Code, groundwater conservation districts, Texas Commission on Environmental Quality) applies generally to agricultural water use, but no cannabis/hemp-specific water rule was returned. Texas Tech / Ogallala Aquifer Program research on hemp water productivity (see §11.9) is research, not a rule. Energy: no Texas rule on CUP/hemp energy use, lighting, HVAC or greenhouse energy was located. General building/electrical/fire codes apply via §12.2(m) for CUP; general codes apply to hemp facilities by ordinary law.

11.7 Sampling, giveaways, discounts and promotions

(a) Dispensing organisations. No CUP-specific rule on patient sampling, giveaways, discounts or promotions was located. Do not invent one. What IS fixed: dispensing only to a CURT patient or legal guardian (37 TAC §12.2, Justia return); no diversion (§12.2); advertising must comply with applicable local/state/federal advertising regulations (§12.2(m), general compliance by reference). Whether DPS prohibits or restricts CUP discounts, loyalty programmes, free samples, or advertising content beyond general law was NOT returned; mark it as [Verify] / Unavailable against full 37 TAC Ch. 12 and DPS guidance. Note: a “sample” for a CUP patient would still be a dispensing event requiring a CURT prescription; it cannot be treated as a general free-sample programme without a rule citation.

(b) Hemp businesses. No DSHS rule specifically banning or permitting giveaways, free samples, discounts, coupons, loyalty programmes, or price promotions for CHPs was located. The absence must be stated as “no specific DSHS giveaway/discount/promotion rule located”, not as “giveaways are allowed”. What IS fixed and constrains any promotion: 21+ sale and government-ID verification before completing sale/service/delivery (25 TAC §§300.701–300.702; 16 TAC §§35.5–35.6 for TABC holders). A giveaway that is in substance a sale/delivery to a person under 21 would violate the 21+ rule; a free sample to an adult is not expressly addressed in the rules returned. Mark free-sample-to-adult treatment as [Verify]. Labelling/COA/QR, child-resistant packaging, and the “not appeal to minors” direction from GA-56 / Chapter 300 labelling rules (Run 1, §§1.5–1.6). The exact “attractive to children” prohibitions in adopted §§300.402–300.407 remain [Verify] verbatim in Run 1. SB 2024 bans marketing/advertising/selling/offering cannabinoid vape products (Class A misdemeanour, effective 1 September 2025). That is a product-specific advertising/sales ban, not a general hemp promotion ban. SB 3's advertising bans (no ads at sports/charity events, within 300 ft of schools/churches/playgrounds/day-cares/shelters/treatment centres, no child-attractive packaging/marketing) were in a vetoed/dead bill and are NOT law (Run 1, §1.6). Do not present them as current promotion rules. TABC alcohol promotion rules (for example drink prices, off-premises promotions) in the TABC World Cup 2026 retailer guide (search return) apply to alcohol, not to CHPs, and the guide itself states it is a summary, not legal authority, and does not cover requirements enforced by other entities. Do not transfer TABC alcohol promotion rules to hemp promotions. Source: tabc.texas.gov — World Cup 2026 guide for retailers (PDF). A separate secondary claim (Ecigator Texas vaping guide, search return): “another new law, SB 1316, prohibits outdoor advertising of cigarettes, e-cigarettes, or tobacco products within 1,000 feet of schools or churches, effective September 1, 2025.” That is a tobacco/e-cigarette advertising rule, not a hemp-CHP rule, and its bill number/effective date remain [Verify] on Texas Legislature Online before any use in Part 11/12. Do not present SB 1316 as a hemp promotion rule. Delivery sales: TABC guidance and Run 1 note that DSHS registrants / TABC holders must age-verify on delivery (ID check before completing sale/service/delivery). No statewide hemp-delivery licence was found in Run 1 ([Verify]).

11.8 Drug testing and background check rules for licence holders and staff

(a) Dispensing organisations. Criminal history disqualifiers: 37 TAC §12.3 is referenced by §§12.15, 12.16 and 12.23, but its full list was NOT returned. Mark §12.3 detail as [Verify]. What is fixed: conviction of a disqualifying felony or misdemeanour under §12.3 is a revocation ground (§12.23(a)(6)); violation of §§481.120, 481.121, 481.122 or 481.125 is a revocation ground (§12.23(a)(7)); denial is available for pre-licensure/renewal violations of the Act / Ch. 12 / those sections (§12.15, old compilation). Registration of personnel: DPS registers directors, owners, managers, members and employees of each dispensing organisation (HB 46 added owners/members to §487.053(a), per Run 1). HB 46 also requires fingerprint-based background checks for persons owning 10% or more (Run 1 secondary summary of HB 46 — [Verify] enrolled text). Registrants must carry or display their DPS-issued registration card while performing regulated services involving contact with or exposure to patients or the general public, including dispensing and transportation (old compiled rules, search return). Proposed 2026 registration rule (see §11.2): directors, owners, managers, members and employees must submit identifiers plus fingerprints in the manner approved by the department, plus SAVE-verified eligibility proof; denial if disqualified under §12.3 or found to have violated the Act / Ch. 12, with a hearing request within 30 days (Texas Register Proposed Rules, 24 April 2026, filed 10 April 2026, TRD-202601569, earliest adoption 24 May 2026 — check adoption status). Drug-free workplace / employee drug testing for CUP staff: no CUP-specific employee drug-testing rule (for example pre-employment, random, post-accident testing for CUP staff) was located in official text returned. A Flourish secondary summary states §12.2 requires a drug-free workplace policy consistent with Texas Workforce Commission requirements — [Verify] against current §12.2, as the Justia excerpt returned does not contain that subsection. General Texas position (Run 1, §1.8): Texas has no comprehensive private-employer drug-testing statute; employers may test pre-employment, randomly, on reasonable suspicion, and post-accident under a written policy. That general position applies to CUP employers unless a specific §12.2 drug-free workplace rule is confirmed. Do not state a CUP staff drug-testing mandate without that confirmation. Physicians: CURT registration and §169.002 qualifications (Run 1, §1.4). No separate physician drug-testing rule was located.

(b) Hemp businesses. DSHS manufacturers: FBI fingerprint-based criminal-history check via IdentoGo after application (Run 1, DSHS guidance summary). A controlled-substance felony within the prior 10 years is disqualifying and can lead to revocation if discovered after issuance (same summary — verify in 25 TAC §§300.201–300.203). Retail registrants are NOT required to undergo the FBI fingerprint check (DSHS page, opened in Run 1). TDA growers/handlers/samplers: no controlled-substance felony conviction within the preceding 10 years (Run 1 secondary summaries, consistent with federal plan requirements). Mandatory TDA orientation. The full TDA background-check procedure (fingerprint vs name-based, timing, key-participant definition) remains [Verify] in 4 TAC Ch. 24 / eApply. Federal plan background checks (for context, not as a Texas rule): under 7 CFR Part 990, Subpart C (secondary summary, search return): every applicant (or every key participant in a business entity) must submit a criminal background check dated within 60 days of application; anyone with a state or federal felony conviction related to a controlled substance is barred from obtaining a hemp licence for 10 years from conviction; falsifying application information disqualifies entirely. Source: legalclarity.org — Can you legally grow cannabis in Texas? (secondary — verify in 7 CFR Part 990 text before republication). Employee drug testing for hemp businesses: no DSHS or TDA rule requiring hemp employers to drug-test staff, or prohibiting them from doing so, was located. The general Texas employer drug-testing position in Run 1, §1.8 applies. DOT safety-sensitive testing (49 CFR Part 40) is federal and has no state medical exception (Run 1). Patients vs staff: do not conflate patient protections (none, Run 1, §1.8) with staff background-check requirements (above). A TCUP patient is not a licence holder/staff member for §12.3 purposes.

11.9 Research and university cannabis and hemp work

Texas A&M AgriLife — Industrial Hemp Breeding Program (secondary reporting, consistent across sources returned): led by Russ Jessup, Ph.D., hemp breeder and professor, Department of Soil and Crop Sciences, Texas A&M College of Agriculture and Life Sciences. The team is described as four graduate and four undergraduate students (Farm Progress profile, search return). Mission: develop hemp cultivars made for Texas heat and tolerant of drought that consistently meet legal requirements; identify new ways to add value; explore seed adaptability, bigger seeds, longer fibers, bioproducts such as insecticides derived from terpenes, bast/hurd fiber research, petiole propagation, trichome microscopy, hypocotyl strength, and phytochemical cancer research (SIU Cannabis Science conference schedule, search return, listing Texas A&M projects). Joined by AgriLife Extension agronomist and state hemp specialist Calvin Trostle (Lubbock) and AgriLife Research breeder Jorge de Silva, both in the Department of Soil and Crop Sciences (Farm Progress, search return). The programme began in spring 2020 after the 2018 federal Farm Bill and Texas HB 1325 (2019). Texas A&M and Texas Tech were described as the first schools in Texas approved to begin researching hemp (MMJ Daily / The Eagle summary, search return). Sources: farmprogress.com — Industrial hemp holds great potential in Texas; mmjdaily.com — Texas A&M leads study of industrialized hemp varieties.

Compliant Hemp Accelerated Improvement Network (CHAIN) — USDA National Institute of Food and Agriculture project, Texas A&M University (official NIFA project page, search return): portal.nifa.usda.gov — CHAIN project page — Recipient: Texas A&M University, 750 Agronomy Rd Ste 2701, College Station, TX 77843-0001. Non-technical summary: the goal is to publicly release and evaluate a collection of novel accessions produced by Texas A&M's Hemp Conversion Program (HCP) alongside other candidate materials to identify germplasm well adapted to the southern U.S.; it establishes a north-south transect of three locations from 26.2°N to 33.5°N; twelve fiber and six grain cultivars plus selected HCP accessions are tested at four planting dates per location; the state extension hemp specialist is to develop a team of extension colleagues across southern states on hemp photoperiod responses; a diverse public HCP germplasm collection incorporates the majority of genetic variation across Cannabis spp. (C. sativa, C. indica, C. ruderalis). Objectives include a southern U.S. public germplasm collection (fiber, grain, cannabinoid, multi-use ideotypes) distributed across universities, extension and private organisations; germplasm testing at three Texas A&M AgriLife test sites developing into a self-supporting fee-based testing programme after two years; and determining THC concentrations at designated pre-harvest conditions to eliminate unsuitable cultivars. CHAIN is USDA NIFA-funded (federal), not Texas state-funded. Do not re-label it as state-funded.

Hemp grain/fiber varieties for heat (HempToday, 2025 reporting, search return — secondary, date to be carried): hemptoday.net — Researchers say they've developed hemp grain/fiber varieties that can stand the heat — the Texas A&M team announced it is preparing to deliver its first set of hemp germplasms to USDA, the result of a breeding programme started in 2022 under a two-year grant from the National Institute of Food and Agriculture. Led by Russell Jessup. The grant was to grow 20 strains across five locations from the Panhandle down to Weslaco. Goal: resilient, productive varieties, particularly dual-cropping grain and fiber; plant material sourced globally (grain types from Japan and Switzerland, fiber varieties from China, per the article). A university-based public programme stands out because private genetics are often tightly controlled; varieties developed at Texas A&M will be accessible to licensed breeders across the U.S. through USDA's germplasm repository. Ph.D. student Clayton Moore is quoted: of roughly 100 varieties on the federally approved hemp genetics list, only about 10% are available. The team's cannabis research extends beyond industrial hemp, including anti-cancer research (per the article — [Verify] against Texas A&M primary sources before presenting anti-cancer work as a Texas A&M programme claim).

Texas A&M AgriLife Extension — Hemp in Research procedure (official AgriLife procedure PDF, search return): agrilifeas.tamu.edu — Procedure 15.99.02.X0.01, Hemp in Research (PDF) — Texas A&M AgriLife Extension Service Procedure 15.99.02.X0.01, Hemp in Research, revised 21 October 2021, next scheduled review 21 October 2026 (the review date is now past at the time of this work — [Verify] whether a revised procedure has issued). Summary: AgriLife provides guidance in accordance with TDA, USDA, FDA and DEA. All faculty, staff and students are responsible for ensuring compliance while working with industrial hemp, consistent with federal, state and local law and law enforcement as it continues to be developed. Researchers may conduct hemp research under the federal Farm Bills of 2014 and 2018; research must be for agricultural or academic research purposes. Individuals who do not hold a licence with TDA shall not produce or handle hemp in Texas. §2.0 Hemp Research Licence: per 4 TAC Ch. 24, Subchapter J, Rule 24.49, AgriLife Extension may apply for a licence to produce and handle hemp for agricultural or academic research. A licence issued to Texas A&M University or a Texas institution of higher education under this section is known as a “Hemp Research License”. A licence holder shall not produce/handle hemp in any location other than locations listed in the licence application or facility addition/modification request. A licence holder shall notify TDA of any theft of cannabis materials. A licence holder shall not produce any cannabis that is not hemp without express authorisation from TDA; without authorisation, any hemp with THC in excess of 0.3% will be destroyed per §4.0 and TDA/USDA hemp destruction protocols. §4.0 Disposal of Hemp: cites 4 TAC Ch. 24, Subchapter J, Rule 24.50; USDA AMS Rule 84 FR 58522, 7 CFR 990; and the Agriculture Improvement Act of 2018. Access: TDA, DEA, DPS and local law enforcement will be provided complete and unrestricted access to all hemp plants and facilities used for production/storage. Local law enforcement may be notified before hemp trials begin in an area, but it is not required. Seed: shall not sell, offer for sale, distribute, or use hemp seed in Texas unless the seed is certified or approved by TDA (see §11.3).

Texas Tech University: described in the MMJ Daily / Eagle summary (above) as, with Texas A&M, one of the first schools in Texas approved to begin hemp research.
