Ogallala Aquifer Program (OAP) FY24 Annual Report (search return): ogallala.tamu.edu — OAP 2024 annual accomplishments report (PDF) — OAP is a research/education consortium seeking solutions for declining water availability from the Ogallala Aquifer on the Southern High Plains, including Kansas State University, Texas A&M AgriLife Research and Extension Service, Texas Tech University, West Texas A&M University, and USDA Agricultural Research Service NP211 projects at Bushland and Lubbock, TX. OAP funding comes from USDA ARS at Bushland. Texas Tech researchers in an ARS OAP project showed the critical importance of planting dates and seeding densities for industrial hemp biomass/fiber/seed production, relationships between root morphology, soil water depletion and water use; early planting facilitated longer vegetative growth, improved plant height, stem diameter, biomass/fiber accumulation and better root development, and demonstrated better water productivity; later planting led to early transition to the reproductive phase, lower biomass/fiber production and water productivity, but higher seed yield due to early flowering. Dissemination via Texas A&M AgriLife Extension programmes reaching over 700 regional producers is for the Spring Cover Crop project — do not attribute that 700-producer figure to hemp alone without checking the report. No Texas Tech-specific cannabis (non-hemp, i.e. low-THC medical) research programme was located. Mark Texas Tech medical-cannabis research as [Verify] / Unavailable, not as “none exists”.

Texas A&M AgriLife + Ionization Labs collaboration (secondary, TX Cannabis Collective tag page, search return): txcannaco.com — AgriLife tag — “Texas A&M AgriLife and Ionization Labs collaborate on hemp crop testing” and “The nations top soil, water and forage testing laboratories will be teaming up with Ionization Labs to perform cannabinoid testing. Texas A&M AgriLife has announced hemp crop research collaboration.” No date, scope, or primary AgriLife press release was returned. Mark it as [Verify] / secondary only; do not present it as a current testing programme without a primary source.

State-funded medical research programmes — NOT FOUND as a Texas TCUP programme; psychedelic programmes must not be confused with cannabis programmes: Run 1, §1.4 notes a physician-requested additions route: HB 46 / 25 TAC §1.61 (as proposed September 2025) lets a physician ask DSHS to report to the Legislature that low-THC cannabis may benefit an unlisted condition; DSHS forwards via DPS to the Legislature. That is a recommendation route, not a funded research programme. Ibogaine — corrected record (new searches, 1 November 2026): HB 3717, 89th Legislature, Regular Session 2025 (Rep. Cody Harris), relating to a grant programme to fund FDA drug development trials with ibogaine for opioid use disorder, co-occurring substance use disorder, and other neurological/mental health conditions, was laid on the table subject to call on 12 May 2025 and is DEAD (BillTrack50 / LegiScan, search returns). The enacted vehicle was the companion SB 2308, 89th Legislature, Regular Session 2025 (Sen. Tan Parker), signed by Governor Abbott in June 2025, authorising up to $50 million in state funding, matched by private money, for FDA-oriented clinical trials of ibogaine-assisted therapy for opioid and substance use disorders and other conditions, administered through the Health and Human Services Commission. Sources: billtrack50.com — HB 3717 detail; legiscan.com — HB 3717 text. Timeline from the tracker (secondary, verify on Texas Legislature Online / HHSC): December 2025 — UTHealth Houston, in collaboration with UTMB Health, was awarded $50 million by the state to lead ibogaine clinical trials (IMPACT consortium, led by UTHealth Houston and UTMB Galveston); 31 March 2026 — Lt. Governor Dan Patrick and House Speaker Dustin Burrows announced Texas “intends to fully fund this program” by contributing a total of $100 million to lead the ibogaine trials. Ibogaine is a psychedelic, not cannabis/hemp, and is listed here only to prevent confusion with a cannabis charge. Do NOT present the ibogaine consortium as a cannabis research programme. Run 1, §1.9 notes House Public Health interim charges (26 March 2026, Speaker Dustin Burrows) to monitor implementation/rulemaking for the 2025 ibogaine clinical-trial consortium bill — the same caveat applies. An earlier psychedelic precedent (secondary, search return): HB 1802 (2021) funded psilocybin trials for veterans with PTSD. Also not cannabis. No Texas state-funded low-THC medical research programme, university medical-cannabis clinical trial programme, or dedicated state appropriation for TCUP research was located. Mark it as [Verify] / Unavailable, with follow-up: search Texas Legislature Online appropriations / Health & Safety Code Ch. 487 research provisions, DPS TCUP research-programme conditions in Occupations Code §169.003(a)(3)(A)(xiv) (“a condition approved for an authorised research programme”), and university clinical-trial registries, before stating “no state-funded programme exists” absolutely. Note also that the DPS TCUP qualifying-condition list itself includes “Medical conditions designated by the Health and Human Services Commission as authorizing treatment with low-THC cannabis as part of an approved research program” (DPS programme summary, search return: txcannabisco.com — TCUP) — that is a qualifying-condition category, not evidence of a funded programme.

PART 12: CONSUMER AND SOCIAL RULES — PARAPHERNALIA, AGE, DRIVING, RECORDS, HOUSING, GUNS, PETS AND SCHOOLS

12.1 Paraphernalia and smoke/vape shop rules — new evidence resolving a Run 1 Verify

Health & Safety Code §481.125, Offense: Possession or Delivery of Drug Paraphernalia (Justia, 2025 Texas Statutes, search return — verify against the official statute before republication, but this resolves the Run 1 tier question on the mirror text): law.justia.com — HSC §481.125. (a) “A person commits an offense if the person knowingly or intentionally uses or possesses with intent to use drug paraphernalia to plant, propagate, cultivate, grow, harvest, manufacture, compound, convert, produce, process, prepare, test, analyze, pack, repack, store, contain, or conceal a controlled substance in violation of this chapter or to inject, ingest, inhale, or otherwise introduce into the human body a controlled substance in violation of this chapter.” (b) Delivery / possession with intent to deliver / manufacture with intent to deliver drug paraphernalia, knowing the recipient intends that it be used for the same purposes. (c) Delivery offence under (b) where the actor is 18 or older and the recipient is younger than 18 and at least three years younger than the actor. (d) “An offense under Subsection (a) is a Class C misdemeanor.” (e) “An offense under Subsection (b) is a Class A misdemeanor, unless it is shown on the trial of a defendant that the defendant has previously been convicted under Subsection (b) or (c), in which event the offense is punishable by confinement in jail for a term of not more than one year or less than 90 days.” (f) “An offense under Subsection (c) is a state jail felony.” Definition, §481.002(17) (secondary summary, search return): drug paraphernalia covers any equipment, product, or material used or intended for use with a controlled substance, including to plant, grow, harvest, manufacture, process, store, contain, conceal, inject, ingest, inhale, or otherwise introduce a controlled substance into the body. Penalties in ordinary terms (secondary summaries, consistent): possession/use Class C, fine ≤$500, no jail; delivery to an adult Class A, ≤1 year / ≤$4,000; delivery to a minor at least three years younger state jail felony, 180 days–2 years / ≤$10,000; repeat (b)/(c) delivery conviction: 90 days–1 year jail.

Parent action: update Run 1's §1.3 paraphernalia [Verify] flag with the above tiers, after checking the official statutes.capitol.texas.gov text. The Run 1 caution — do not rely on a generic “Class C” summary without checking §481.125(b)–(d) — is now satisfied on the mirror text for (d)–(f), but the official-text check remains. Collateral consequence flagged in secondary summaries ([Verify] on official Transportation Code text): for anyone 21 or older, Texas Transportation Code §521.372 is said to automatically suspend a driver's licence for 180 days after any drug conviction, including Class C paraphernalia. One secondary page states paying the ticket equals pleading guilty and creates a permanent criminal conviction. Mark the §521.372 suspension detail as [Verify] before republication; do not present it as confirmed from the searches alone. TCUP device carve-out: Run 1's [Verify] on the DSHS-approved inhalation device carve-out remains [Verify]. §481.125(a) requires use/possession “in violation of this chapter”; a device prescribed under Occupations Code §169.006 and dispensed under Ch. 487 is, by that framing, not “in violation”, but no express paraphernalia carve-out section was returned. State the reasoning, not a quoted carve-out, until §169.006 / §487.054 text is checked. THC vape pen as paraphernalia vs Penalty Group 2: a THC vape cartridge itself is Penalty Group 2 THC possession (state jail felony minimum, §481.116, Run 1), while the pen/device alone is paraphernalia possession (Class C) under §481.125(a)/(d) (secondary analysis, search return: blasslaw.com — THC pen FAQ). Present both charges as potentially concurrent, not as alternatives. No standalone “public consumption of low-THC cannabis” offence was found in Run 1. That finding stands; public-consumption exposure for a TCUP patient, if any, would come from general offences (public intoxication, DWI, drug-free zones — see §12.9), not from a TCUP public-use statute located here.

Smoke/vape shop rules (non-hemp tobacco/e-cigarette layer, to be kept distinct from DSHS hemp registration): an e-cigarette is defined in Health & Safety Code §161.081(1-a) as a device simulating smoking by using a mechanical heating element, battery, or electronic circuit to deliver nicotine or other substances in vapour form (secondary summary, search return). Age 21 for tobacco/e-cigarettes: sale/giving to a person under 21 is prohibited (§161.082); possession/purchase/consumption/acceptance by a person under 21 is prohibited (§161.252), both Class C misdemeanours, fine ≤$500 (secondary summaries, search return). Limited Texas military exception: individuals at least 18 presenting a valid U.S. or state military forces ID are exempt from the underage possession offence under §161.084 (secondary summary) — BUT federal Tobacco 21 (December 2019) has no military exception, so the Texas exception does not protect a retailer against federal enforcement. Flag the Texas exception's current scope as [Verify] on official §161.082/§161.084/§161.252 text. Comptroller e-cigarette retailer permit: required for each business location selling e-cigarettes; $180 for a two-year period, or $90 if the retailer already holds a cigarette/tobacco products permit; permits run on the 31 May even-year renewal cycle; permits cannot be issued for a residence or public storage unit; the retailer must also hold an active Texas sales tax permit for each location (secondary summaries, search return, consistent with Run 1, §5.6 tobacco-permit note — Run 1 flagged the $180/$90 fees as [Verify] on the live Comptroller page, and that flag remains). Vendor-assisted sales: Health & Safety Code §161.086 requires products not to be displayed for direct customer access; all vaping products must be kept behind the counter or in locked cases, inaccessible without employee assistance (secondary summaries). Vending machines: Health & Safety Code §161.086 — a retailer/person may not install/maintain a vending machine containing cigarettes, e-cigarettes or tobacco products except in specified settings, including a facility/business not open to people under 21 at any time, a qualifying adult-only humidor/enclosure, and certain adult-only package-store premises. Comptroller FAQ (secondary summary): a vending-machine owner must hold an e-cigarette retailer permit for the location and be the seller; machines must be in locations only accessible by adults 21+; a lockout device/remote control does not make an otherwise customer-accessible machine inaccessible; a vending-machine owner may not sell e-cigarettes in someone else's place of business. Warning signs: every retail location must display a warning sign with statutory language, including notice that selling to / purchasing by a person under 21 is illegal and violations are a Class C misdemeanour with fines up to $500; the Comptroller provides signs free to businesses; failure to post can lead to fines up to $500 (secondary summaries — [Verify] sign text/penalty on Comptroller pages). Delivery sales: retailers conducting delivery sales must register with the state and implement dual age verification (at purchase and upon delivery) (secondary summary — [Verify] statute/rule citation; this is a tobacco/e-cigarette delivery rule, not a hemp-CHP delivery licence). SB 2024 overlay (Run 1, confirmed again in this session): SB 2024 signed 20 June 2025, effective 1 September 2025, raised the penalty to a Class A misdemeanour (up to 1 year / $4,000 fine per violation, for offences on/after that date, per secondary summary). It is not a product registry; the actual licence is the Comptroller e-cigarette retailer permit. Banned substances in any vape: cannabinoids (THC, delta-8, delta-10, HHC, CBD and the rest), kratom, kava, alcohol, mushrooms, tianeptine. Source (commercial secondary — verify the SB 2024 penalty/classification on Texas Legislature Online; Run 1 already flagged the exact SB 2024 text/penalty as [Verify]). Local smoke/vape-shop zoning/moratoriums are in Run 1, Part 3.3 (Austin proposal, Dallas vaping-in-smoking-areas rule, Brownsville distance rule, Harlingen moratorium, Alamo corridor restriction, Breckenridge ban then Industrial-district conditional use). Do not repeat them as Part 12 rules without cross-referencing Part 3.3; Part 12 should state that smoke/vape shops are governed by the state permit/age/display rules above PLUS local zoning in Part 3.3, and that no statewide hemp-specific smoke-shop licence exists. Sources for this subsection: ecigator.com — Texas vaping laws guide; legalclarity.org — Are vapes legal in Texas?; agevend.com — Vape vending laws by state.

12.2 Minimum age and ID rules for hemp-derived THC products

Match Run 1 exactly: 21+ for consumable hemp products BY RULE, NOT BY STATUTE. DSHS emergency rules 2 October 2025 (§§300.701–300.702), then permanent 25 TAC Ch. 300 rules effective 31 March 2026. TABC emergency rules 23 September 2025, then permanent 16 TAC §§35.5–35.7 (adopted February 2026, effective 21 January 2026 per the Parts 3–4 track, [Verify] exact date). Government-issued photo ID must be inspected before completing sale/service/delivery. Violation: DSHS licence/registration revocation; TABC permit suspension/cancellation. DSHS programme page (search return): dshs.texas.gov — Consumable Hemp Program — “On Oct. 2, 2025, the Texas Department of State Health Services adopted emergency rules to prohibit Consumable Hemp Licensees and Retail Hemp Registrants from selling consumable hemp products to customers under the age of 21 and require a valid proof of government-issued identification prior to purchase. The emergency rules can be found in the Texas Register at Rule §300.701 and Rule §300.702.” TABC World Cup guide (secondary summary, search return): tabc.texas.gov — World Cup 2026 guide for retailers (PDF) — checking ID is required for TABC-licensed businesses when supplying CHPs; a business must not sell, serve or deliver CHPs to minors (persons under 21); it must check ID before selling/serving/delivering CHPs; a TABC licence/permit does not authorise CHP industry participation — the business must get proper DSHS authorisation. Authorities cited: Alcoholic Beverage Code §§11.61, 61.71; TABC Rules 35.5, 35.6. No statewide statutory age for hemp existed before GA-56 (Run 1). No new hemp-age statute was located in this session. The 21+ rule remains rule-based, so a future rule repeal/amendment or court invalidation (see the SS Distro / THBC litigation in Run 1 and the §11.1 temporary restraining order update above) would change the age rule without a statute change. Flag litigation status as [Verify] at publication. TCUP age: no separate statutory purchase age; access is by physician prescription for a qualifying patient, including minors with a qualifying condition and a prescribing physician — minor-patient/guardian provisions in DPS rules remain [Verify] in Run 1. Do not apply the 21+ hemp rule to TCUP. Tobacco/e-cigarette age (distinct): 21+, with the limited Texas military exception noted in §12.1 above, subject to federal Tobacco 21 without exception. Do not conflate the tobacco age exception with the hemp 21+ rule, which has no military exception in the rules returned.

12.3 Impaired driving law and testing

Offence: Texas Penal Code §49.04, Driving While Intoxicated. A person commits an offence if intoxicated while operating a motor vehicle in a public place. Intoxication, §49.01, includes (as returned in multiple secondary summaries): not having normal use of mental or physical faculties by reason of introduction of alcohol, a controlled substance, a drug, a dangerous drug, a combination of two or more of those substances, or any other substance into the body. No separate “marijuana DWI” offence; drug DWI is charged under the same §49.04. No per-se THC limit in Texas. Unlike alcohol's 0.08 blood alcohol concentration standard, Texas has NO legal limit for THC or any drug. The State must prove actual impairment, not merely presence. Secondary summaries agree on this point, but disagree on the evidential shortcut, and the disagreement must be presented honestly:

  • One line of secondary summaries (McConathy, TxCrimDefense, search returns) states intoxication can be shown by (1) loss of normal faculties due to marijuana, OR (2) having a detectable amount of THC/metabolites, and that “any amount” / “zero-tolerance” can support a charge. That reading treats §49.01's “introduction” language broadly.
  • A second line (Dallas DUI Lawyer, Norris Legal Defense, WM Attorneys, search returns) states presence is NOT impairment, THC metabolites linger days/weeks after impairment ends, blood concentration does not reliably correlate with impairment (citing National Institute on Drug Abuse research, per Norris), and the State must prove impairment through driving behaviour, video, field sobriety / Drug Recognition Expert (DRE) evaluation, and expert testimony.

Parent action: check the current official §49.01 “intoxicated” definition and recent Texas appellate DWI-drug case law before stating either “any detectable THC = DWI” or “presence never = DWI” as law. The safe Run 2 sentence, on the searches alone, is: Texas has no THC per-se limit; prosecutors rely on impairment evidence, and blood THC presence is contested evidence, not a statutory number. Testing methods and limits: no reliable marijuana breathalyzer is in Texas law enforcement use (multiple secondary summaries, consistent). Breath testing is for alcohol, not THC. Blood (and urine) testing is the usual drug-detection method. THC can remain detectable in blood for days, and metabolites in urine for weeks/months, long after impairing effects end (secondary summaries). Blood-test concentration, timing, and what it proves about driving-time impairment are contestable. Field sobriety tests and DRE evaluation: the DRE 12-step protocol (pulse, blood pressure, pupil size, muscle tone, psychophysical tests) by a specially certified officer, per the Dallas DUI Lawyer summary (secondary — [Verify] DRE protocol status in Texas DPS / National Highway Traffic Safety Administration materials). “No Refusal” programmes: during holiday periods/weekends, officers may obtain expedited warrants for blood tests if a driver refuses initial testing (WM Attorneys summary, citing NHTSA No Refusal initiatives — secondary, [Verify] Texas programme details). Refusal: secondary summaries state a driver does not have to agree to a blood specimen without a warrant and should consult counsel; that is advice, not a Texas implied-consent rule statement. Texas implied-consent / licence-consequence rules for drug-DWI refusal were NOT returned; mark them as [Verify] against Transportation Code Ch. 724 before republication. TCUP / hemp and DWI — no protection: “Legal medical marijuana use doesn't protect you from DWI charges. Law enforcement can still arrest you for driving while impaired, regardless of whether your marijuana use was legally authorized.” (McConathy summary, December 2025, search return.) Same for hemp: “any amount of THC can theoretically support a DWI charge if prosecutors can prove impairment” (Norris summary, search return), and hemp-derived products containing <0.3% THC do not create a DWI defence. “If your medical marijuana prescription contains THC, you still can't drive while taking your medication [if impaired].” (Nonstop Justice summary, search return.) Present this as impairment-based, not as a ban on driving after any TCUP dose. Penalties (secondary summaries, [Verify] on Penal Code Ch. 49 text): first DWI conviction summaries state a maximum $2,000 fine, 72 hours to 180 days jail, 24–100 hours community service, and up to 12 months licence suspension (TxCrimDefense summary, old — [Verify] current §49.04 penalty tiers, enhancements for prior convictions, child passenger, open container, etc., before republication). Do not present that first-offence summary as the complete DWI penalty table. Sources: mcconathylaw.com — Marijuana/THC DWI Texas; nonstopjustice.com — Texas marijuana laws; txcrimdefense.com — Texas laws: driving under the influence of marijuana; wm-attorneys.com — Driving under the influence of marijuana; norrislegaldefense.com — Drugged DWI Texas: THC prescription; dallasduilawyer.com — Drug DWI Texas.

12.4 Expungement, non-disclosure and record clearing for marijuana offences

Headline, matching the task instruction: Texas has NO general marijuana expungement statute. There is no law that automatically clears, or allows a petition to clear, a marijuana conviction simply because marijuana policy changed, because the amount was small, or because another state legalised. Relief, if any, comes from the general expunction and nondisclosure statutes below, applied offence by offence and arrest by arrest.

Expunction (now Chapter 55A, formerly Chapter 55): a secondary source (NeedAnAttorney, Temple page, search return) states: “Expunction (Code of Criminal Procedure Chapter 55A, which replaced the former Chapter 55 on January 1, 2025) is not available for a conviction or for successfully completed deferred adjudication; that's what an order of nondisclosure, under Government Code Chapter 411, is actually for.” A Blass Law Houston nondisclosure guide (search return) states the same chapter mapping: “Code of Criminal Procedure chapter 55A | Government Code chapter 411, subchapter E-1” and “Arrests that didn't end in a conviction or in deferred adjudication above Class C: acquittals, charges never filed, Class C deferred, and dismissals that meet the statute's grounds or where the limitations period has run” qualify for expunction. A Land & Law Group summary (search return) states: “expungement in Texas is a very limited option. It's generally only available for: Dismissed cases: If the charges against you were dropped. Certain misdemeanors: Only specific misdemeanors, like minor theft or possession of small amounts of marijuana, qualify for expungement after completing probation without any violations.” That second bullet is OVERBROAD and conflicts with the Chapter 55A / nondisclosure distinction above. Do NOT repeat “possession of small amounts qualifies after probation” as law without checking Article 55A.01 grounds. The correct general statement on the stronger sources is: expunction destroys the arrest record entirely and is available only when the case ended favourably (dismissal, acquittal, no-bill, Class C deferred, pardon, etc.) and statutory conditions are met; a conviction, and successfully completed deferred adjudication above Class C, do NOT qualify for expunction. Arrest-based vs offence-based problem — Supreme Court of Texas, Ex parte R.P.G.P., No. 19-1051 (search returns): facts: Kerrville Police arrested the petitioner in March 2015; the State charged two Class B misdemeanours from the same arrest: DWI and possession of marijuana. The petitioner pleaded no contest to marijuana possession, and the trial court imposed nine months community supervision as deferred adjudication (per the dissent summary). Issue: whether an arrest involving multiple offences is divisible for expunction under Article 55.01 (now 55A). The Court of Appeals (San Antonio, 606 S.W.3d 755, 762 (2019)) applied an arrest-based approach: expunction is available only if ALL offences comprising an arrest are eligible. Supreme Court discussion (search returns): Article 55.01 is “neither entirely arrest-based nor offense-based”; Article 55.01(a)(1) (acquittal/pardon) cannot be squared with an arrest-based approach, per State v. T.S.N., 547 S.W.3d 617, 619 (Tex. 2018), where partial expunction by redaction was required. Article 55.01(a)(2) (dismissals/plea bargains) has different requirements. The possession charge was ineligible because the petitioner had served court-ordered community supervision for that charge. The trial court also admitted evidence of the no-contest plea / community supervision despite a nondisclosure order for the possession charge; the majority and dissent disagreed on whether the nondisclosure order shielded that evidence in the expunction hearing. Dissent (Justice Bland, joined by Blacklock and Huddle): Chapter 55 expunction is for wrongfully arrested persons, carefully limited when an arrest results in multiple charges; the Court's decision to allow expunction based on dismissal of one offence even when another charge from the same arrest resulted in a no-contest plea bargain with community supervision is not permitted by the statute. Parent action: do NOT state a single holding for R.P.G.P. from the dissent and search summaries alone. Open the majority opinion (argued 6 January 2021, decided 2021) and state its holding, and its effect on marijuana + DWI / marijuana + other-charge arrests, before republication. The usable, sourced point for Part 12 is narrower: multi-offence arrests create a partial-expunction / redaction problem, and a nondisclosure order does NOT automatically make the underlying conduct invisible in a later expunction proceeding. Sources: docs.texasappellate.com — R.P.G.P. dissent (PDF). Related case — Ex parte M.A.S. (2021, Court of Appeals, via Studicata summary, search return): studicata.com — Ex parte M.A.S. summary — the appellant DPS challenged a trial court order expunging records for a 17 April 2014 arrest on multiple drug charges (two counts of possession of a controlled substance plus possession of marijuana). The defendant pleaded guilty to the marijuana charge on 3 September 2015 and received deferred adjudication community supervision; the felony charges were dismissed as part of the plea agreement. Having completed supervision, the defendant sought expunction of the felony records in July 2019, arguing a nondisclosure order for marijuana made him eligible. The Court of Appeals reversed and rendered judgment denying expunction. Rule as summarised: an individual seeking expunction must meet all statutory requirements, including not having served community supervision for any offence arising from the same arrest (except Class C misdemeanours, per the summary's reading of Article 55.01). The court emphasised an “arrest-based” approach for felony offences. This summary conflicts in emphasis with R.P.G.P.'s “neither entirely arrest-based nor offense-based” discussion; present both as appellate history, not as a single settled rule, and check the current Chapter 55A text. Waiting periods for expunction (secondary, Land & Law / LegalClarity summaries, search returns — [Verify] in Chapter 55A): if a case was dismissed and no formal charge (information/indictment) was ever filed, the case is eligible after 180 days from arrest for a Class C misdemeanour. Do not generalise that 180-day figure to Class A/B or felony dismissals without checking Article 55A.01 / 55A.02 waiting periods and limitations periods.

Non-disclosure (sealing, NOT destruction) — Government Code Ch. 411, Subchapter E-1: effect: an order of nondisclosure seals criminal history information from public and most private background checks, while allowing government/agency access. It does NOT destroy the record like expunction. Nondisclosure orders allow disclosure to various agencies/entities; expunction orders prohibit any use of expunged records (distinction stated in the R.P.G.P. materials, search returns). Automatic nondisclosure — §411.072: for a qualifying nonviolent misdemeanour deferred adjudication, the court is required to issue a nondisclosure order without a petition, at discharge/dismissal or as soon as possible after the 180th day from placement on supervision, whichever is later. Conditions (Office of Court Administration instructions, revised August 2026, search return): txcourts.gov — Instructions for an order of nondisclosure under §411.072 (PDF) — discharge/dismissal on/after 1 September 2017; placed on deferred adjudication for a qualifying misdemeanour; the court did not enter an affirmative finding that it is not in the best interest of justice to receive an automatic order; on deferred adjudication for a minimum of 180 days; first-time offender, excluding fine-only traffic offences. Ineligible under §411.072 if discharge/dismissal occurred before 1 September 2017. If discharge/dismissal occurs on/after the 180th day, the court must issue the order at the same time as discharge/dismissal; if before the 180th day, the court must wait until 180 days have elapsed. Filing fee $28 for the §411.072 route (Blass summary). Ineligible offences for the automatic route include felony, DWI, and misdemeanours involving assault, family violence, weapons, sexual conduct, or similar offences (LegalClarity summary — verify against §411.072 / §411.074). Petition-based nondisclosure — §411.0725: applies to felony deferred adjudication and misdemeanours outside the automatic pathway. Waiting periods after discharge/dismissal (multiple secondary summaries, consistent in substance, [Verify] in §411.0725(e)): most misdemeanours: petition immediately upon discharge/dismissal (§411.0725(e)(1), per the Blass table). Misdemeanours under Penal Code Chapters 20, 21, 22, 25, 42, 43 or 46 (assault, kidnapping, sexual offences, weapons, protective orders, disorderly conduct, family offences — summaries vary slightly in the chapter list): 2 years after discharge/dismissal (§411.0725(e)(2)). Felonies: 5 years after discharge/dismissal (§411.0725(e)(3)). A person can only receive one order of nondisclosure for deferred adjudication (Criminal Defense Lawyer summary — [Verify] the “one order” limit in §411.0725). DWI nondisclosure — §411.0726: a separate provision for DWI/BWI deferred adjudication, with no prior record other than fine-only traffic tickets: 2 years after discharge (Blass table). Do not apply the general misdemeanour immediate-petition rule to DWI. Post-conviction nondisclosure — §411.073: some first misdemeanour convictions (not DWI, BWI or other intoxication offences, minor DUI, or organised crime, per the Blass table) may qualify after probation ends, or 2 years after for the listed chapters. This is a narrow post-conviction sealing route, NOT expunction of a conviction. [Verify] §411.073 eligibility, waiting periods and the “first conviction” limit before republication. Ineligible offences — §411.074: the Government Code §411.074 list of offences INELIGIBLE for nondisclosure regardless of deferred completion, including family violence, sex offences requiring registration, kidnapping, murder, injury to a child/elderly/disabled person, and stalking (Land & Law summary). Marijuana possession itself is NOT on that ineligible list in the summaries returned; do not add it. Procedure (Blass summary, [Verify] in §§411.075–411.0765): after the judge signs, the clerk has 15 business days to send the order to DPS; DPS has 10 business days to seal its own records and send the order on; government agencies get 30 business days to seal theirs; background-check companies must destroy the record once notified. Present this as a secondary procedure summary, not as quoted statute. Application to marijuana cases, in plain terms for Part 12: a dismissed / acquitted / never-filed marijuana arrest, meeting Chapter 55A grounds: expunction possible (destruction), subject to the arrest-based / multi-offence limits above. Class C paraphernalia deferred adjudication: expunction route (Class C deferred is the exception noted in the Blass table). Class B marijuana possession (≤2 oz) or Class A (≤4 oz) deferred adjudication, successfully completed: NOT expunction; nondisclosure route (§411.072 automatic if a qualifying nonviolent misdemeanour and first-time, otherwise §411.0725 petition, immediate for most misdemeanours on the Blass table). Marijuana possession conviction (not deferred): generally NOT expunction; narrow §411.073 post-conviction nondisclosure may apply to a first misdemeanour conviction meeting its conditions — [Verify], do not promise it for every marijuana conviction. Penalty Group 2 felony THC deferred adjudication, successfully completed: nondisclosure petition after 5 years (§411.0725(e)(3)), if not otherwise ineligible; conviction: generally no expunction and no §411.073 route (which is misdemeanour-only in the summaries returned). No automatic clearing on a change in hemp/marijuana law: a pre-2019 hemp/marijuana arrest is not automatically expunged because hemp was redefined in 2019, and a post-2026 delta-8 scheduling change does not automatically clear prior delta-8 records. That negative must be stated explicitly, as it is the most common misunderstanding. No Texas statute creating such automatic marijuana expungement was located. Local / proposed record-clearing bills: no enacted Texas statewide marijuana expungement / automatic sealing bill was located. Run 1, Part 4.6 proposed-only bills include HB 3242 (Moody, 89th Regular Session, 2025 — possession of ≤1 oz as a violation with no jail, no custodial arrest, and an expungement mechanism for low-level offences, per an MPP summary) — DEAD, not granted a House committee hearing in 2025. Do NOT present HB 3242's expungement mechanism as law. Local decriminalisation ordinances (Run 1, Part 3.2) do NOT expunge or seal records; they direct police not to cite/arrest in stated circumstances, and most are preempted, repealed or stayed (see the reconciled facts above). Do not conflate decriminalisation with record clearing. Any 2027 record-clearing bill is anticipated, not pending, as of the Run 1 cut-off (no filed 90th Legislature bill existed yet; pre-filing begins in November 2026 — [Verify] the pre-filing date on Texas Legislature Online). Sources: needanattorney.net — Temple criminal defense; landllawgroup.com — Expungement vs sealing records in Texas; blasslaw.com — Nondisclosure (Houston); legalclarity.org — Deferred adjudication and background checks in Texas; texasdwisite.com — Deferred adjudication; criminaldefenselawyer.com — Texas expungement.

12.5 Cite-and-release policies

CORRECTION to the task hint: cite-and-release in Texas is HB 2391, 80th Legislature, Regular Session, 2007 — NOT 2017. Effective 1 September 2007. Passed the House 27 April 2007 (132 Yeas, 0 Nays, 2 present not voting, per the enrolled bill certification, search return); passed the Senate 18 May 2007 (29 Yeas, 1 Nay, per a petition summary, search return — [Verify] Senate journal); signed by Governor Rick Perry 15 June 2007 (per a petition summary — [Verify] signing date). Statute — Code of Criminal Procedure Article 14.06, as amended by HB 2391 (engrossed text, Texas Legislature Online, search return): capitol.texas.gov — HB 2391 engrossed text; capitol.texas.gov — HB 2391 engrossed analysis. (a) Except as otherwise provided, the person making the arrest / having custody shall take the person arrested without unnecessary delay, but not later than 48 hours after arrest, before the magistrate who may have ordered the arrest, before some magistrate of the county where the arrest was made without an order, or, to provide more expeditiously the Article 15.17 warnings, before a magistrate in any other county. The magistrate shall immediately perform the duties. (c) “If the person resides in the county where the offense occurred, a peace officer who is charging a person with committing an offense that is a Class A or B misdemeanor may, instead of taking the person before a magistrate, issue a citation to the person that contains written notice of the time and place the person must appear before a magistrate of this state as described by Subsection (a), the name and address of the person charged, and the offense charged.” (d) Subsection (c) applies only to a person charged with committing an offence under: (1) Section 481.121, Health & Safety Code, if the offence is punishable under Subsection (b)(1) or (2) of that section [i.e. ≤4 oz marijuana: Class B ≤2 oz, Class A >2 oz to 4 oz]; (2) Penal Code §28.03, if punishable under (b)(2); (3) Penal Code §28.08, if punishable under (b)(1); (4) Penal Code §31.03, if punishable under [text truncated in the return — [Verify] the full (d) list]. Article 15.17(g) (added by HB 2391): if a person charged with a misdemeanour appears before a magistrate in compliance with a citation under Article 14.06(b) or (c), the magistrate shall perform Article 15.17 duties as if the person had been arrested and brought by an officer. After those duties, the magistrate, except for good cause shown, may release the person on personal bond. If a person issued a citation under (c) fails to appear as required, the magistrate before whom appearance was required shall issue a warrant for arrest. Bill analysis (Senate Research Center, HB 2391 engrossed, search return): “Current law requires police officers to arrest a person for an alleged violation of a Class A or Class B misdemeanor, expending a significant amount of resources. H.B. 2391 authorizes a peace officer to issue a citation to a person who has allegedly committed a violation of certain Class A or Class B misdemeanor offenses and under certain circumstances.” The bill does not expressly grant additional rulemaking authority.

What cite-and-release IS and IS NOT: it is DISCRETIONARY for the officer (and, in practice, for the department), not mandatory statewide, and NOT decriminalisation. The person is still charged, must appear before a magistrate, and the case proceeds as any other case. Failure to appear triggers a warrant. It applies only if the person resides in the county where the offence occurred, and only to the listed Class A/B offences, including marijuana possession ≤4 oz under §481.121(b)(1)–(2). It does NOT apply to felony marijuana possession (>4 oz), Penalty Group 2 THC possession (felony at any weight, §481.116), or to a person with other arrestable offences in the same encounter (department policies commonly require possession to be the only arrestable offence — see the Pharr example below, which is a department policy, not the statute). It does NOT bar arrest. An officer may still arrest and take the person before a magistrate within 48 hours under Article 14.06(a). It does NOT conflict, by itself, with Local Government Code §370.003 (no local non-enforcement policy), because it is a state-authorised citation alternative, not a local policy not to enforce. Local ordinances that make cite-and-release MANDATORY, or that go further to prohibit citations/arrests (the Ground Game / Mano Amiga decriminalisation model in Run 1, Part 3.2), are a different legal question and are the ordinances held preempted in San Marcos/Austin. Do not conflate the two. Local examples (department policies, not state mandates): Pharr Police Department, City of Pharr, Texas (official city page, search return): pharr-tx.gov — Pharr PD to implement cite-and-release — Pharr PD implemented a Cite-and-Release policy allowing officers the option to release a person in possession of less than 2 oz of marijuana (Class B), governed by Article 14.06, enacted as HB 2391 by the 80th Legislature in 2007. Implementation was decided during COVID-19 to minimise jailing for low-level offences, department-wide. Prerequisites in the Pharr policy: (1) the offence is possession of marijuana <2 oz, Class B; (2) the offence occurs in Pharr, Texas; (3) the suspect resides in Hidalgo County, Texas; (4) the suspect is in possession of a valid driver's licence or ID; (5) the suspect is 17 or older; (6) possession is the only offence for which the suspect is subject to arrest. Officers retain discretion to arrest or release. If released, the person must be arraigned at municipal court within 14 days and will be released on a personal-recognisance bond; the case is still completed by detectives and sent to the District Attorney as any other case. Chief Andy Harvey is quoted: “It's important to note that this policy does not decriminalize anything, rather can provide a more dignified option for a low-level offense.” Note: Pharr's <2 oz / Hidalgo County residence limits are NARROWER than the statute (≤4 oz / resides in the county of offence). Department policies may narrow, but cannot expand, the statutory categories without a separate legal basis. San Marcos mandatory cite-and-release ordinance (secondary law-office history, search return): randlelawoffice.com — Texas law: cities and cite-and-release — San Marcos was described as the first municipality in Texas to make cite-and-release mandatory by ordinance for low-level offences, including Class C misdemeanours other than public intoxication, assault or family violence, and possession of marijuana less than 4 oz (Class A/B). Officers retained discretion as to whether a mere warning might be merited. That mandatory-ordinance history is distinct from the later voter-approved decriminalisation ordinance in San Marcos (Run 1, Part 3.2, Prop A 2022, 81.86%, held preempted 2025). Do not merge the two San Marcos measures. The current status of the mandatory cite-and-release ordinance after the 2025 decriminalisation litigation was NOT returned; mark it as [Verify] against the current San Marcos Code. Austin: Austin City Council unanimously passed a resolution in January (year as in the returned article: January, in the context of a July 2020 article — [Verify] year) reprioritising low-level marijuana possession, described in the same article as “more of a suggestion than the binding ordinance San Marcos passed.” Resolutions are not ordinances; do not present an Austin cite-and-release resolution as a mandatory policy. Statewide adoption: no statewide mandatory cite-and-release law for marijuana was located. Adoption is department by department, within Article 14.06 discretion. No official statewide list of departments with cite-and-release policies was located; mark it as Unavailable, not as “no department has one”.

12.6 Landlord and tenant rules

No Texas statute creating a tenant right to use low-THC cannabis, or a landlord duty to accommodate TCUP use, was located. Run 1's “no employment protection” finding has a parallel here: no housing protection for TCUP patients was located in Health & Safety Code Ch. 487 / Occupations Code Ch. 169. State this as “no protection located”, with a full-text search of the Property Code and Ch. 487 as a follow-up, rather than as an absolute negative proven by a single section. Private (non-subsidised) housing — landlord latitude via lease: secondary analyses (Green Residential, LegalClarity, search returns) agree in substance: just as landlords can ban cigarette smoking, they can ban marijuana; a lease may ban smoking, ban cannabis use in any form, ban criminal activity, or ban federally prohibited drugs, and breach can support a warning, a cure-or-quit notice, and eviction for failure to follow lease rules. Eviction does not have to rest on broader law; it can rest on lease breach. A Green Residential analysis (search return) adds a nuance that must be preserved: because Texas TCUP products are non-smoked (oils, tinctures, capsules/lozenges, edibles/gummies, topicals, lotions, patches, suppositories, non-smoked inhalation — Run 1), a landlord's smoking ban does not, by itself, clearly ban a non-smoked TCUP product, and a tenant evicted solely for non-smoked medical use “could have a case if it landed before the right judge — in large part because they would not be smoking the product.” That is litigation risk analysis, not a tenant right. Present it as such, not as a holding. If a lease bans “any kind of drug use” or “criminal activity”, a landlord may argue that argument covers even non-smoked TCUP use because marijuana remains federally illegal and, outside TCUP, state-illegal. Whether a court would enforce that clause against a TCUP patient using a prescribed non-smoked product in compliance with Ch. 487 was NOT resolved by a Texas appellate holding returned. Mark it as [Verify] / no controlling holding located. Smoking a TCUP product is in any event prohibited by TCUP itself (Run 1: smoking prohibited, always), so a tenant has no TCUP-based argument for smoking on the premises. Sources: greenresidential.com — Texas expanded medical marijuana access: what does that mean for landlords?; legalclarity.org — Cannabis legalization by state. Federally subsidised / assisted housing — stronger landlord / housing-authority position: a LegalClarity summary (search return) states: “If you live in federally assisted housing, your landlord has the authority to evict you for marijuana use, even with a valid TCUP prescription. Under Section 577 of the Quality Housing and Work Responsibility Act, property owners must maintain lease provisions that allow termination for illegal drug use, and marijuana qualifies because o[truncated].” Flag: the “Section 577” citation is truncated and unverified in the return. The federal framework generally is: public housing authorities and federally assisted owners must include lease provisions permitting termination for illegal drug use, and marijuana remains illegal under federal law even if state-legal. Do NOT cite “Section 577” as verified until the Quality Housing and Work Responsibility Act / 42 U.S.C. / HUD regulation text is checked. The usable, sourced point is the direction (federal assistance = federal illegality controls), not the section number. Universities / federally funded housing: see §12.9 below — federally funded universities generally prohibit cannabis on campus and in residence halls, even with a prescription (secondary summary, Denton Cannabis Clinic page, search return). Commercial landlords / hemp retail tenants: no Texas rule was located that requires a commercial landlord to lease to a hemp/CUP business, or that voids a lease ban on hemp retail. Commercial risk analysis in Run 1-adjacent secondary material (2025–2027 Economic Outlook, search return in the landlord search) notes THC-ban / regulation risk for retail properties housing vape/smoke shops, lease compliance-with-all-laws clauses, escape clauses if legislative shifts render a business unviable, shorter terms, and higher deposits/guarantees. That is commercial practice, not Texas landlord-tenant law. Do not present it as a statute. A CUP facility location itself is governed by DPS approval and §487.201 (a city/county may not ban authorised CUP activity), not by landlord consent. A landlord's private lease ban is different from a city ban; §487.201 binds municipalities, counties and political subdivisions, not private landlords. State that distinction explicitly. Tenant remedies / procedure: no Texas cannabis-specific eviction procedure was located. Ordinary Texas eviction procedure (Property Code Ch. 24, Justice Court eviction suits, notice to vacate, cure-or-quit where the lease requires it) applies generally. Do not invent a cannabis eviction timeline.

12.7 Gun ownership conflicts

Federal prohibition — 18 U.S.C. §922(g)(3): it is unlawful for any person who is an “unlawful user of or addicted to any controlled substance” to possess, receive, ship, transport, or purchase firearms or ammunition. Marijuana, including low-THC cannabis, remains a controlled substance under federal law for this purpose, even if state-legal. A state prescription is no defence under the federal statute as written (multiple secondary summaries, consistent, including a TCUP public-safety risk paper, search return). ATF Form 4473: the Firearms Transaction Record, completed when purchasing from a federally licensed dealer (FFL), asks: “Are you an unlawful user of, or addicted to, marijuana or any depressant, stimulant, narcotic drug, or any other controlled substance?” The form includes a warning that marijuana use remains illegal under federal law even where states have decriminalised or legalised it. Answering “yes” blocks the purchase. Answering “no” while being an unlawful user is a federal false-statement offence, in addition to §922(g)(3) possession exposure. Penalties stated in secondary summaries: false statement up to 5 years (per one summary) and a §922(g)(3) violation up to 10 years / $250,000 fine (per another summary) — [Verify] penalty maxima in 18 U.S.C. §§922, 924 before republication; do not present the secondary maxima as confirmed. Texas License to Carry (LTC) does NOT resolve the federal conflict: Texas does not require a state permit, registration or waiting period to buy a firearm from a dealer (secondary summary, search return); a valid Texas LTC, issued within the past five years, qualifies as a NICS alternative under federal law, so a dealer may skip the FBI background check, but the buyer must still complete Form 4473. An LTC therefore does not waive the Form 4473 unlawful-user question. DPS LTC position — now confirmed as a DPS FAQ position via secondary quotation (new searches, 1 November 2026): “A patient's participation in CUP does not, in itself, disqualify the individual from obtaining or maintaining a License to Carry (LTC). Notwithstanding that certain medical marijuana programs have been determined by the FBI to disqualify an individual from possessing firearms, the department does not believe this determination applies to Texas' low-THC cannabis, Compassionate Use Program. However, the individual's underlying condition that is the basis for participation in CUP may under certain circumstances be disqualifying. If the medical condition potentially affects the individual's ability to exercise sound judgment, the department may refer the matter to the Medical Advisory Board (DSHS) for their review and recommendation. Should the Board find the individual 'incapable of exercising sound judgment with respect to the proper use and storage of a handgun,' the department would deny an application or revoke a current LTC.” Sources quoting the DPS FAQ: topgunchl.com — LTC eligibility; txcannabisco.com — TCUP; texasoriginal.com — Texas Compassionate. Present DPS's LTC position as a DPS FAQ position, NOT as a Texas statute protecting TCUP gun ownership, and note the underlying-condition / Medical Advisory Board caveat — a TCUP patient is not automatically LTC-safe if the qualifying condition itself affects sound judgment. TCUP public-safety paper (search return): mcusercontent.com — Texas CUP Public Safety Risks (PDF) — “Texas law does not require local law enforcement to recognize TCUP status, and DPS's position that participation alone does not bar a License to Carry does not resolve the federal prohibition nor does it resolve NFPA's stance.” And: “Active TCUP use is incompatible with armed duty and truthful completion of ATF Form 4473.” And: “A lawful TCUP prescription does not make an armed or safety-sensitive employee compliant with federal firearms law, agency drug policy, or duty-readiness standards.” What “unlawful user” means — CHANGED / CONTESTED, flag carefully: older secondary summaries state a single drug conviction/possession charge within the past year could disqualify. A LegalClarity Texas gun-buying guide (search return) states: “An interim federal rule that took effect on January 22, 2026, changed how ATF defines 'unlawful user' of a controlled substance. Previously, a single drug conviction or possession charge within the past year could disqualify you. The updated rule requires evidence of regular, ongoing use over an extended period to trigger the prohibition. Isolated or sporadic use no longer automatically makes someone ineligible. Marijuana remains a federally controlled substance regardless of any state's legalization, so regular users are still prohibited from buying firearms.” That interim-rule claim is SECONDARY and was not verified against the Federal Register. Mark it as [Verify]. Do not state that isolated TCUP use is now safe for gun purposes on the basis of that summary alone. Schedule III interaction: Run 1's Schedule III [Verify] question applies here too. Even if state-licensed medical marijuana moved to Schedule III federally on 28 April 2026 per the given baseline, §922(g)(3) applies to controlled substances generally, not only Schedule I, so Schedule III status alone would not automatically remove the firearms prohibition. Whether ATF / Department of Justice guidance treats Schedule III medical use differently for §922(g)(3) / Form 4473 was NOT located. Mark it as [Verify]. Do NOT state that Schedule III “fixes” the gun conflict. Pending litigation — United States v. Hemani (secondary analysis, search return — DO NOT present as decided law): cannitrol.com — Your weed, your gun, your rights: what U.S. v. Hemani means — facts as summarised: Ali Danial Hemani, Texas-born, University of Texas honor student; the FBI searched his home in 2022 on a warrant related to alleged ties to Iranian entities, not drugs/guns; agents found a legally purchased 9mm Glock pistol, 60 grams of marijuana, and 4.7 grams of cocaine; Hemani told agents he smoked cannabis roughly every other day. A federal grand jury indicted him under §922(g)(3). Procedural status as summarised: arguments heard 2 March 2026; a decision was expected by late June (year as in the article: late June, in a March 2026 article — [Verify] whether a decision has issued by publication date). The article's prediction (“complete vindication for the government appears unlikely”, three scenarios including a narrow cannabis-specific ruling) is punditry, not a holding. The usable point only: §922(g)(3) as applied to cannabis users is under active Supreme Court challenge, and until a decision issues and ATF guidance changes, the statute and Form 4473 question remain in effect as written. Do not advise a TCUP patient that Hemani protects gun possession. Note: a search result in the gun batch also claimed “In March 2026, President Trump signed an executive order directing the Attorney General to reschedule marijuana from Schedule I to Schedule III, but that order does not automatically change the law.” That claim CONFLICTS with the given federal baseline for this commission (Schedule III for state-licensed medical / FDA-approved products effective 28 April 2026, 91 FR 22714; adult-use remains Schedule I). Do NOT import the executive-order summary into Run 2. Use the given baseline, and flag any executive-order / rescheduling development as [Verify] against the Federal Register. Enforcement reality (secondary, not a defence): multiple secondary summaries state federal prosecution of a TCUP patient solely for gun possession is uncommon without additional criminal activity, and ATF cannot access CURT data to identify TCUP users (per a DFWSAS blog, search return: dfwsas.blogspot.com — Federal gun laws and THC). Present this as an enforcement observation, NOT as a legal safe harbour. Other gun-related consequences to flag as [Verify], not as confirmed: Texas Commission on Law Enforcement licensure standards, federal security-clearance drug-use criteria, pre-employment psychological screening, fitness-for-duty for public-safety positions, and Drug-Free Workplace Act zero-tolerance policies for departments (TCUP public-safety paper, search return). These are agency/policy consequences, not Texas gun statutes. Do not present them as §922(g)(3) holdings.

12.8 Pet and veterinary products

No Texas-specific CBD pet-product statute or Texas Board of Veterinary Medical Examiners (TBVME) CBD guidance was located. Mark Texas veterinary CBD guidance as [Verify] / Unavailable. Do not present a Texas pet-CBD licence, registration, or age rule, because none was found. Federal / FDA position (Vicente LLP veterinary series, secondary legal analysis, search return — verify against FDA Center for Veterinary Medicine pages before republication): vicentellp.com — Recommending CBD and hemp products for pets — hemp was legalised federally through the 2018 Farm Bill and is no longer a federally controlled substance. CBD products derived from hemp for humans and animals are readily available across the country. The FDA takes the position that CBD may not be included in food or dietary supplement products for humans OR animals. “As far as the FDA is concerned, pet food and treats containing hemp-derived CBD are adulterated and illegal for sale in interstate commerce.” The FDA stated it will not undertake rulemaking to permit the sale of products containing CBD, including those intended for animals, and any path forward must come from Congress. FDA concern: risks to animals consuming CBD and the potential for people to become unintentionally exposed through meat, milk and eggs. The FDA suggested a new regulatory pathway enacted by Congress could provide “access and oversight” for CBD products for animals. The FDA has not approved CBD for any use in animals (Brightfield summary, search return, older — [Verify] current FDA Center for Veterinary Medicine approval status). AAFCO position (same Vicente analysis, search return): the Association of American Feed Control Officials (AAFCO) works with FDA to establish model policies for the pet-food industry. AAFCO released guidance stating hemp ingredients, including CBD and other cannabinoids, cannot be used in pet food. That guidance has been adopted and is binding in many states, and is referenced by many states and treated as binding even where not officially adopted. Note in the analysis: the guidance was recently removed from the AAFCO website, but there is no indication it is rescinded and many states still reference it. Whether Texas has adopted AAFCO hemp guidance, or has a Texas feed-control rule (Agriculture Code / TDA feed programme) that bans or permits hemp in pet food, was NOT located. Mark Texas adoption as [Verify] on TDA feed / commercial feed pages. Do not state Texas bans or permits hemp pet food on the basis of AAFCO alone. Veterinarian recommendation vs dispensing/selling (Vicente analysis, search return): “Simply recommending that a pet take a hemp-derived product is not a violation of the FFDCA and likely does not violate state food and drug laws, even if the product is prohibited in the state where the veterinarian practices.” “Selling hemp-derived products, including CBD, at a veterinary office likely violates the FFDCA and could potentially violate state law. Veterinarians who wish to sell pet food or treats containing hemp-derived cannabinoids at their office should be aware that these products are considered adulterated by the FDA. Veterinarians should also ensure they know all state laws regarding pet hemp products. They may be prohibited under state law or have specific requirements, including obtaining state registration, labeling, and testing requirements.” Present recommendation vs selling as a federal-law distinction from a law-firm analysis, not as a Texas Board rule. Texas Board of Veterinary Medical Examiners (TBVME) — what WAS found (not CBD): a Citizen Portal report (April meeting, year as in the article — meeting report last updated 564 days before the search, search return): citizenportal.ai — Texas veterinary board clarifies rule allowing compounded controlled substances for clinic use — TBVME told licensees there is no rule banning compounded controlled substances for office stock, but stressed any medication dispensed for a specific patient to take home must be prescribed and documented as a separate prescription. Board General Counsel Kelly Phelps: “in our current rule, there is nothing that prohibits such a thing,” referring to compounded controlled-substance products ordered for clinic stock (rule cited at the meeting as rule 573.44). Rule 573.44 does bar using clinic stock as a substitute for a patient-specific prescription when medication is to leave the clinic. Board President Steven Gola said he could not point to a prior ruling creating the misconception; board/staff planned a one-page guidance document for the website. No formal vote was taken; the item was informational clarification. That clarification is about compounded controlled substances generally, NOT about CBD/hemp pet products, and must not be presented as TBVME CBD guidance. General TBVME jurisdiction (Docsity jurisprudence material, secondary): TBVME regulates the practice of veterinary medicine, licensing and enforcement; the DEA enforces controlled-substance regulations in veterinary practices; vet techs administer medication only under licensed veterinarian supervision; dispensing to clients requires proper labelling and instructions. Those are general practice points, not pet-CBD rules. Practical Texas position for Part 12, on the searches alone: a Texas pet owner can buy hemp-derived CBD pet products sold in Texas retail channels that hold DSHS retail registration (if the product is a consumable hemp product for human consumption — pet products are NOT human CHPs, so the DSHS CHP framework does not cleanly cover them; mark that coverage gap as [Verify]). No Texas pet-product-specific testing, labelling, THC-limit, or age rule was located. A Texas veterinarian should not be advised, on this research alone, that recommending, dispensing or selling CBD pet products is Texas-board-approved. The federal adulteration position and the absence of a located Texas rule are the two facts to state, with a TBVME / TDA feed-rule follow-up. THC pet products: a pet product containing more than trace non-delta-9 THC, or more than 0.3% delta-9 / total delta-9, would be a controlled-substance / non-hemp product under Texas law (Run 1, §§1.5, 1.2), not a hemp pet product. No Texas veterinary THC product (analogous to TCUP for animals) exists; TCUP is for human patients with listed conditions, prescribed by a physician, not by a veterinarian. State that explicitly to prevent a “pet TCUP” misunderstanding. Market figures in the pet search returns (for example “$1.4 billion annually on CBD products for dogs and cats by 2025”, “56% of veterinary practitioners now discuss CBD”, “74% / 80%” Brightfield consumer figures) are commercial/secondary market claims with no Texas breakdown and no primary source opened. Do NOT use them in Part 12 market statements.

12.9 Low-THC cannabis in schools

What IS fixed — districts may not ban authorised low-THC possession: TASB Policy Online, Policy Code FFAC (Legal) — Wellness and Health Services: Medical Treatment, Bridge City ISD Board Policy Manual (search return): pol.tasb.org — FFAC (Legal), Bridge City ISD — under “Low-THC Cannabis”: “A district may not enact, adopt, or enforce a rule, ordinance, order, resolution, or other regulation that prohibits the cultivation, production, storage, dispensing, or possession of low-THC cannabis, as authorized by the Texas Compassionate-Use Act. Health and Safety Code (Texas Health and Safety Code)” — citing Health & Safety Code §487.201 (the same prohibition-on-prohibition in Run 1, Part 3.1, which binds municipalities, counties and other political subdivisions; a school district is a political subdivision for this purpose in the TASB policy framing — [Verify] whether §487.201 has been applied to school districts by a court/AG opinion, or whether TASB is stating its policy interpretation). The same FFAC (Legal) low-THC language appears in Texas School for the Blind and Visually Impaired (TSBVI) policy (search return): tsbvi.edu — FFAC, Texas School for the Blind and Visually Impaired — “TSBVI may not enact, adopt, or enforce a rule, ordinance, order, resolution, or other regulation that prohibits the cultivation, production, dispensing, or possession of low-THC cannabis, as authorized by the Texas Compassionate-Use Act.” Confirmed again in new searches (1 November 2026): Connally ISD FFAC (Legal), DATE ISSUED: 22 May 2025, UPDATE 125: “Low-THC Cannabis A district may not enact, adopt, or enforce a rule, ordinance, order, resolution, or other regulation that prohibits the cultivation, production, dispensing, or possession of low-THC cannabis, as authorized by the Texas Compassionate-Use Act. Health and Safety Code 487.201” — parentsquare.com — Connally ISD FFAC (Legal) (PDF); Mesquite ISD FFAC (Legal), DATE ISSUED: 11 June 2024, UPDATE 123, same language — parentsquare.com — Mesquite ISD FFAC (Legal) (PDF). TASB School Law eSource article, “Legal Questions About CBD and Marijuana in Schools” (via ReadKong, search return — secondary, TASB guidance, not a statute): readkong.com — Legal Questions About CBD and Marijuana in Schools (TASB) — “Valid patients, as well as their legal guardians, are exempt from criminal penalties for possession and the patient's use of low-THC cannabis.” And: districts that adopted TASB's Model Student Code of Conduct can find this exception in their local codes. The article's condition list is pre-HB 46 (epilepsy, seizure disorder, multiple sclerosis, spasticity, ALS, autism, terminal cancer, incurable neurodegenerative disease, PTSD, and a condition approved for a research programme) — do NOT use that list in Part 12; use the HB 46 list in Run 1, §1.4. What is NOT a statewide mandate — administration, storage, self-administration: no statewide Texas statute or DPS/TEA rule was located that REQUIRES a public school to store, administer, or allow self-administration of low-THC cannabis on school property, or that creates a school-nurse administration procedure for TCUP products. TASB FFAC (Legal) DOES contain detailed procedures for other medicines that do NOT extend to low-THC cannabis on the pages returned: consent to medical treatment (Family Code §§32.001, 32.002); administration by volunteer professionals (Education Code §22.052(a)–(b), with a Board of Nursing rule limitation in Occupations Code §301.151); self-administration of asthma or anaphylaxis medicine (prescription label, demonstrated skill to a physician / school nurse, compliance with prescription / written instructions); prescription/non-prescription medication administered in the School Health Center by a nurse, health assistant or medication sponsor, with exceptions for other locations with Health Center Director / Superintendent / Principal approval, self-administration in the Health Center or elsewhere with Health Center Director approval, and staff medication-sponsor training for field trips with written parent/guardian permission; immunity from civil liability for administration in accordance with policy (Education Code §22.052). The contrast matters: asthma/anaphylaxis self-administration IS expressly provided for; low-THC cannabis self-administration is NOT in the FFAC excerpts returned. Do not transfer the asthma/anaphylaxis procedure to TCUP products. Parent action: check (a) Education Code Ch. 38 (Health and Safety) for any low-THC administration provision, (b) DPS TCUP rules for school administration, (c) TEA guidance, and (d) individual district FFAC (Local) policies, before stating “schools must / must not administer”. Until then, Part 12 must state: districts may not ban authorised possession (per §487.201 / TASB FFAC Legal), but no statewide administration/storage/self-administration mandate was located, so practice is district by district and parent/guardian administration is the practical route most districts will point to — mark that practical route as secondary inference, not as a rule. Note also the TASB Model Student Code of Conduct (2025–26, revised July 2025, search return): sparqdata.com — TASB Model Student Code of Conduct 2025–26 (PDF) — “A district is prohibited from enacting, adopting, or enforcing a rule or regulation that prohibits the possession of low-THC cannabis as authorized by the Texas Compassionate Use Act, Chapter 487 of the Health and Safety Code which permits prescriptions for low-THC (nonintoxicating) cannabis in limited circumstances. If a student has a valid” [truncated] — and the Code lists as misconduct: abuse of the student's own prescription drug, giving a prescription drug to another student, possessing or being under the influence of another person's prescription drug on school property or at school events, abuse of over-the-counter drugs, being under the influence of prescription/over-the-counter drugs that cause impairment, and having/taking prescription or over-the-counter drugs at school other than as provided by district policy. CBD / hemp products in schools — districts MAY ban, per TASB guidance (secondary): TASB article (search return): “Given the difficulty of determining whether a substance is legal, school officials may want to adopt a general ban on CBD. Districts that wish to adopt a ban on use or possession of CBD on district property may likely do so under the school board's general authority to adopt rules for the safety and welfare of students and employees. Provisions in state law prohibit local authorities from adopting or enforcing a rule that prohibits the handling, sale, or manufacture of hemp, but these provisions do not restrict local prohibitions on possession or use of hemp or hemp products. Before adopting a local prohibition, district leaders would be wise to consult their school attorneys and coordinate with local law enforcement authorities.” Present this as TASB guidance (“may likely do so”), not as a statute authorising a CBD ban, and note the tension with §487.201: §487.201 protects authorised low-THC cannabis possession; it does not protect general hemp/CBD possession/use on school property in the TASB reading. A district CBD ban that swept in a TCUP patient's authorised low-THC product would conflict with §487.201 on that reading — flag that tension explicitly. Employees: TASB article (search return): “Unless employees are covered by the Texas Compassionate Use Act or prescribed Epidiolex, they should not use or possess at work any cannabis-related product with a THC concentration of more than 0.3 percent, including CBD. The Texas Educator's Code of Ethics prohibits 'illegal' [text truncated].” Epidiolex is the FDA-approved cannabidiol drug, distinct from TCUP and from hemp CBD. Employee TCUP use at work is governed by Run 1, §1.8 (no employment protection) plus district policy; no school-employee TCUP accommodation rule was located. Section 504 / IEP note in the TASB article (search return, truncated): a medical provider's plan memorialised in a Section 504 plan or IEP was mentioned in the context of medication administration. No TCUP-specific 504/IEP holding was returned. Mark 504/IEP treatment of TCUP as [Verify]; do not promise a 504 right to TCUP administration at school. Higher education / dormitories: a Denton Cannabis Clinic page (secondary, search return): dentoncannabis.clinic — “Universities that receive federal funding generally prohibit cannabis on campus and in residence halls, even with a prescription. Check your school's policy.” And: “TCUP is for permanent Texas residents. Students from other states may not qualify, and out-of-state medical cards aren't accepted in Texas.” Present this as a general federal-funding / campus-policy point, not as a Texas Education Code rule. Individual university policies (University of Texas, Texas A&M, Texas Tech, etc.) were not checked; do not name a university policy without opening it. Drug-free school zones / penalties near schools — do NOT conflate with TCUP possession: no Part 12 search returned a Texas drug-free-zone enhancement that applies to a TCUP patient's authorised possession. Drug-free-zone enhancements (Health & Safety Code Ch. 481, Subchapter D / Penal Code) apply to unlawful delivery/manufacture/possession offences, not to authorised TCUP possession, on the Run 1 framing. Mark any drug-free-zone statement for TCUP patients as [Verify] against Ch. 481 text before republication, and do not state that a TCUP patient near a school commits a drug-free-zone offence.

RESEARCH NOTES — COVERING BOTH RUNS (RUN 1, PARTS 0–6, AND RUN 2, PARTS 7–12)

Cut-off and scope. Run 1 was researched and accessed 31 October 2026. Run 2 tracks were researched 31 October 2026, with Parts 7–8 pages crawled 1 November 2026, 00:23 UTC (31 October 2026, 19:23 EDT) and Parts 11–12 direct opens 1 November 2026, 00:23–00:28 UTC. Houston Proposition A, scheduled for election on 3 November 2026, had not been voted on at this cut-off and its outcome is NOT in this file. Post-31 October 2026 developments — including any DSHS roster re-post after 19 October 2026, any federal hemp action after 11 December 2026, and any United States v. Hemani decision after March 2026 — are out of scope for this dossier and must be updated at any republication, not carried forward as Run 1 facts.

Reconciliation rule used in this file. Where tracks conflicted, the most recent official or DPS-corrected source controls (corrected DPS conditional list over earlier press lists; 18 June 2026 DSHS counts as most recent, with April 2026 figures retained and dated beside them; patient series retained by date and reporter, not averaged). Every mention of the same fact in different parts was checked against that rule; any remaining conflict is listed below, not hidden. Parts 0–6 are reproduced in substance unchanged from Run 1; Parts 7–12 were checked against them, and no Run 1 fact was silently overwritten.

What Run 2 changed or added. (1) Part 7 confirms Texas's own USDA-approved state plan (submitted 2 December 2019, approved 27 January 2020) and adds DSHS amendment fees ($10,000 ownership change, $125 minor amendment, +$1,000 late renewal) and the “no single hemp distributor licence” rule (Chapter 431 wholesaler authorisation instead). (2) Part 8 finds zero Texas properties proven to be cannabis-zoned. (3) Part 9 adds agency contacts, the smokable-hemp litigation sequence, Senate Health & Human Services interim charges, the “no statewide citizen initiative” ballot rule, and dated polling tables. (4) Part 10 finds no tribal cannabis dispensary, cultivation operation, ordinance or compact for any of the three federally recognised Texas tribes, confirms Ysleta del Sur Pueblo's USDA-approved hemp plan (posted as “(DRAFT)”), and leaves the Oklahoma/CURT eligibility question and the Texas share of New Mexico sales as Unavailable. (5) Part 11 adds directly opened 37 TAC §§12.31–12.32 (security and vehicles) and 4 TAC §24.31 (non-compliant hemp destruction), resolves the CUP 1,000-ft buffer in part (§12.7(n), subject to a current-TAC check), and corrects the ibogaine record (HB 3717 dead; SB 2308 enacted). (6) Part 12 resolves the paraphernalia tiers on mirror text (§481.125: Class C / Class A / state jail felony), corrects cite-and-release to HB 2391 (2007, not 2017), and states the expungement/nondisclosure framework with its multi-offence-arrest caveat.
